INTERCESSORY PRAYER FOR A WORLD-WIDE 
REVIVAL 


J. C. Massr, D.D. 


Pastor of First Baptist Church, of Dayton, Ohio., Formerly 
President of the American Baptist Home Missionary 
Society, Bible Teacher and Author. 


In the eighteenth chapter of the gospel of Luke, first 
verse, we find this statement: “And he spake a parable 
unto them to this end, that men ought always to pray, and 
not to faint.” They, the disciples, ought always to pray. 
Ought, then is obligation: ought there is duty. The old 
English way of spelling it was d-u-e-t-y; what is due to 
God, what is due to man. 

They ought to pray. That is elemental, fundamental, 
inescapable. The Master is speaking of the most vital thing 
in the disciple’s life, the greatest necessity for the disciple’s 
service. “Men ought to pray!” “Men ought to pray!!” 
“Men ought to pray!!!” It is as if a physician should say 
to one struggling for health: “You ought to breathe;” or a 
nurse should tell a patient struggling toward life: “You 
ought to eat;” or, as if an athlete training another should 
say to him: “You ought to exercise, so that the joints may 
be supple, and the muscles and nerves co-ordinate.” “You 
ought to pray that you may live; you ought to pray that you 
may have strength; you ought to pray that your vital spir- 
itual organs may co-ordinate in the service of God. You 
ought to pray.” Why? 

For personal reasons. I would to God I could say it 
to you as this conviction is in my own heart, that prayer is 

Helpful books by the above author listed on pages 446-449. 


ES 


— 47 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the first instinct of a new-born soul. Prayer is the highest 
privilege of a God-begotten life. Prayer is the most exact- 
ing and searching exercise of one who, having found, would 
walk in the heavenly way. There is a remarkable signifi- 
cance in every word of inspiration, perhaps none more sig- 
nificant than in that word which declares: “Ye have not 
received the spirit of bondage again to fear; but ye have 
received the Spirit of adoption, whereby we cry, Abba, 
Father. The Spirit himself beareth witness with our spirit, 
that we are the children of God” (Rom. 8:15, 16). 

“Likewise the Spirit also helpeth our infirmities: for 
we know not what we should pray for as we ought: but the 
Spirit himself maketh intercession for us with groanings 
which cannot be uttered” (Rom. 8:26). ` 

The most interesting and delightful sound in the 
world is the laughter of a little babe. The cooing of a child 
carries in it the world’s greatest melody. I have been glad 
always in my own home to be able to say: 


“Hush, be still as any mouse 
There is a baby in this house; 
Not a dolly, not a toy, 

But a laughing, cooing boy.” 

The cooing laughter bubbling up out of the clean heart 
of a little baby makes all the world glad. But universal as 
the joy from this source is, the first voice of the child is not 
a laugh nor coo, but a cry. There is nothing so genuinely 
beautiful, so heartily refreshing, so heart satisfying as the 
laughter of a babe. But there is nothing so absolutely nec- 
essary to the very life of the child as a cry. The cry of a 
babe is the universal volapuk. 

I know a woman, a mother: she told me of that hour of 
travail in which she went down to the shadows of death and 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


came again to life and hope. She declares that in the pains 
and pangs of her travail, she put all her soul into the listen- 
ing for a cry that did not come. And the agony of the 
travail itself was not so great as the anguish of that disap- 
pointing silence. For the silence of the birthchamber in 
which there is no infant’s cry is the stillness of death. Is 
not then this the significance of the cry: “Abba, Father”? 
It is the instinctive cry of the new-born soul without which 
there is no evidence of life. The prayerless soul is marked 
by its very prayerlessness as the spiritually unborn soul. 

Will you think with me for a moment concerning that 
statement regarding Christ: “He shall see of the travail of 
his soul, and shall be satisfied” (Isa. 53:11). I am think- 
ing that the eternal heart of the Father in the travail of the 
soul’s birth of one of His sons, listens with all of a heaven- 
crowded passion of intensity and desire to hear the cry of 
His new-born child calling: “Father! Father! Father!” 
Unless you know how to pray, unless you have learned the 
secret way into His presence, unless your heart has 
throbbed out toward God with a child’s cry, beloved, you do 
not know Him, in the holy relation of child. The new 
birth is yet an unknown experience to you. 

“Men ought to pray.” There was a day when He 
would send one of His servants to speak to a man whose 
life had been filled with threatenings and with persecution, 
a man who was to be won for great service. And, when 
His servant demurred at the commission, sensing in it dan- 
ger and perhaps death, the Master said: “Go * * * * 
for;behold, he prayeth” (Acts 9:11). His praying was ev- 
idence that he had been born again; praying in the Name 
in which he prayed; praying so that his prayers might find 
access to God by the blood-marked way by which he came; 


paa 


oe 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


praying, since he voiced his cry by the begettings of the 
Holy Spirit within him; praying! Such praying is evidence 
of life eternal. 

Then you and I know that man who has been born into 
the kingdom is thrust immediately, when he walks forth, 
into the midst of turmoils and struggles and temptations. 
The god of this world flings about him every opposition 
and upon him every possible antagonism. He seeks to en- 
snare him and destroy him immediately, simply because he is 
a child of God. 

I think of Daniel, the incomparable youth of that an- 
cient day, who was beautiful to look upon and comely in 
his physical person, wise in his intellectual knowledge, and 
having great attainments. It is even said of him in the in- 
spired record that he knew all sciences. Here is a man who 
knew all sciences; who lived in a corrupt age and yet earn- 
estly and efficiently served God. 

How was it accomplished? The answer may be easily 
obtained from the record in the book which bears his name. 
He refused even to close his windows upon his secret de- 
votions. He dared to call his soul his own without the 
formality of closing the doors. He did not bother to shut 
out the prying eyes of hateful and hating foes. He prayed. 
This fact the Holy Spirit has deemed worthy of personal 
record in the annals of inspiration. 

His life of prayer marked as a pioneer the pathway of 
countless faithful feet the ages through. His example has 
been the inspiration of multitudes of young men who have 
dared and endured in the strength which came to them by 
the selfsame route of prayer trodden by this ancient hero of 
faith. No other asset of personal faith than the practice of 
the presence of God is worth more to the individual believer 


— 50 — 


ah al 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


than the habit of constant prayer. Therefore, men ought 
always to pray for a personal reason. 

So also men ought to pray for domestic reasons. How 
great are the problems and perplexities which arise out of 
domestic situations! 


Have you any problems at home, Christian friends? 
Fathers, Mothers, have you any shadows upon the pathway 
of your life? Do your bairns, going forth from your doors, 
mark always a pathway of light for your heart? Are you 
never troubled? 


A short time ago, one morning at 2 o’clock an automo- 
bile came in collision with another and two young girls 
were hurt. At 2 o'clock in the morning! They were both 
carried into the little town—not a very large one—to the 
hospital. The next morning by 10 o’clock the news had 
gone abroad over the telephone wires in the neighborhood 
and the morning newspapers, and 186 mothers telephoned in 
to know if it were their daughters who had been hurt at 2 
o’clock in the morning! And mothers, with broken, yearn- 
ing hearts, and problems beyond their power to endure, 
waiting the weary night through! For such, nothing is left 
save God and prayer. 


There is in my heart a profound conviction that if we 
should somehow bring to pass a revival of the famly altar 
in the world we would have a world revival of Christianity. 
I know no barrier more insurmountable to faithlessness and 
infidelity than the barrier of prayer. A boy is not going 
out of the home wherein he has knelt before Almighty God 
and heard a believing father take the name of his Christ in 
adoration and praise, day after day, through eighteen years, 
he is not going out of that home into the halls of some 


HEATA atl 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


college to be quickly moved away from his faith by some 
half-baked scientist. He is not going out to besmirch his 
morals, after his mother has had around him with her own 
arms the arms of her God and has breathed into his soul 
the breath of faith and prayer. 

“Men ought to pray.” I remember hearing Major Cole 
tell’some years ago the story of a Presbyterian elder in Chi- 
cago. It mightily moved me at the time. He said the man 
was a friend of his. He was taken ill. One morning, when 
he seemed to be passing away, already in a state of coma, 
his physician, his minister, his wife and his two sons stood 
at his bedside and out of their keen distress talked about 
him. The minister said: “God help me, he was the very 
best elder I knew! He is dying! I cannot stop his going.” 
The physician said: “God help me, he was the best friend 
I ever had! All that love and science combined has enabled 
me to do, I have done and I cannot save him.” The broken- 
hearted woman said: “He was the best husband a woman 
ever had! He is dying, and love is powerless to stay his 
going!” The two lads, 16 and 19 years of age, at the foot 
of the bed looked on in voiceless but sobbing sorrow. And 
the man on the bed heard, as many a man does hear in a 
coma, what was going on about him, and suddenly he real- 
ized his present situation and the real hollowness of his 
whole Christian profession. He had been an elder because 
he liked the office and the admiration it provoked. He liked 
to hear men speak of him as “Elder.” But as he lay there 
exposed before the presence of God, he saw that it had all 
been mockery and vanity. And out of the depths of his 
soul he cried to God: “Give me another chance and I will 
live for Thee in reality!” And God heard him. Three 
weeks later he came downstairs from his bedroom to the 


— 52— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


sitting-room and gathered his wife and boys about him and 
sent for his servants. When they had gathered, he said: 
“Wife, I have a confession to make.” His wife said: 
“Husband, hush, there is nothing you need to say, you are 
the best man I know.” “But,” he said, “I am dealing with 
God now. You be still!” Then he told them the story as I 
have told it to you. That 19-year-old son had already 
begun to sow his wild oats; on his account the home had 
been filled with anxiety. The father opened the Bible and 
began to read, not by verse, nor by chapter, but page after 
page. For half an hour he read. Then he went down upon 
his knees and began to pray! And, as he prayed, said 
Major Cole, that 19-year-old boy suddenly got up from 
where he was kneeling, rushed across the room and putting 
his arms around his father’s neck, said: “Dad, if you mean 
it like this I am in it with you from now on!” And the 
next Wednesday night in prayer meeting, when the father 
stood up to give his testimony, the 16-year-old boy linked 
his arm into his father’s arm and said: “Father, if you mean 
it like this I am in it with you!” > So in one week he had 
brought to the Master two souls, his own two sons who 
were going down to hell over the mockery of a form of 
Christian life without its power. He had saved himself, his 
sons, his home. “Men ought to pray.” “Men ought to 
pray.” 

For the sake of the home. Our friends all about this 
world who hold the faith of Christ in its simplicity ought to 
re-emphasize the fact that Jesus Christ, and Jesus Christ 
our Lord alone, is the hope of the security, the sanctity 
and the purity of the home. For if the Christian ideals and 
conceptions of truth and virtue fail, God help the world! 
Social chaos and moral anarchy with interminable confu- 


— 53 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


sion will follow, and it will be indeed an impossible and 
hopeless world for us all. For the sake of the home “men 
ought to pray.” For the permanent moral value and Chris- 
tian message of the Godly home and society “men ought to 
pray.” 

I used in Baltimore the other night a story which I 
would like to repeat, for it moved me mightily when I 
heard it: There was a home in one of the New England 
states wherein a son had gone first to the devil and then to 
the dogs. Many a boy goes that road to utter destruction. 
He had dishonored the name he bore and senselessly be- 
smirched it. At last his father said to him: “My son, I 
am truly ashamed to have you bear my name. You must 
leave home and never darken my door again!” And the 
boy went out ashamed, a miserable, broken, drunken wreck. 
Then his mother’s love asserted itself, and she said to that 
father who was her husband: “He is my boy as well as 
yours, and he is not to be cast out! If he can never come 
into this home again I will have to go out of it too. But if 
you are willing to make this promise I will remain. ‘Give 
him one room at the side of the house, with stairs on the 
outside up to that room, and let him and me have keys to 
the door.” He will never come in here, but I will go to him. 
He is my son, I will never give him up.” And so. month 
after month he continued his drunken debauches, trampling 
under foot the very love which could not let him go. 

John B. Gough, the great temperance reformer, came 
to spend a night in that home. When the shadows had 
fallen and the father had gone out to keep an engagement, 
the mother sitting at the fireside with Doctor Gough said to 
him: “My own boy is damned under the curse of drink. 
His manhood is broken. His will is destroyed. He is a 


NG eel 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


wreck, and I am a wreck too because of him. I have 
prayed God to send you and you have come in answer to 
my prayer. Go to my boy’s room and win him for God. I 
want you to go up there tonight, for he is in that room re- 
covering from a drunken debauch.” Mr. Gough objected, 
but the mother would not listen to any objections. At last, 
yielding, he climbed up the steps, opened the door and went 
in. There stood in the middle of the room a fellow with 
face bloated and eyes blood-shot, an utterly disreputable 
wreck of a man. Gough looked at him to see if he could 
find an entrance into the gateway of his soul for Christ and 
spoke his sympathy. The fellow indignantly flung back ev- 


erything he said. “I am done for, let me alone!” Mr. 
Gough said: “Do you not believe in God?” “No.” “Do 
you not believe in Christ?” “No.” “Do you not then be- 


lieve in love?” “Yes, my mother’s love. I believe in love.” 
“Then,” said Mr. Gough, “if you cannot pray to God, and 
cannot pray to Christ, if you believe in love, kneel down 
here with me and pray to Love.” It caught the boy by sur- 
prise. It caught his attention, and Mr. Gough put his arm 
-about the boy’s shoulder and drew him to the floor. So 
with his arm still about his shoulder, he encouraged him to 
pray to Love. Finally the boy in agony cried out, “O 
Love!” The Spirit of God spoke to him, “God is love.” 
Then he cried: “Oh God!” Again the Spirit whispered, 
“God so loved the world, that he gave his only begotten son, 
that whosoever believeth in him, should not perish, but 
have everlasting life” (John 3:16). Then the broken man 
cried: “O Christ!” and the miracle was done, and he was 
on his feet sobbing for joy, redeemed by the power of the 
love of the living God in Christ—the love that would not 


let him go.

I tell you, we ought to pray for the sake of the home. 

We ought to pray for the sake of the church. I think 
of the church as the bride of Christ, and I wonder what it 
really does mean to Him to have a bride. I can feel in my 
heart tonight my own joy in my bride of twenty-three years 
ago. The thrill of it lasts until this moment, and I have no 
higher ambition in this world than to be my wife’s second 
husband, just for that thrill repeated. But my Christ’s 
bride! White garments, the righteous acts of the saints, 
clothing my Christ’s bride, a redeemed people, His own! 
His own body bought with His blood, redeemed by His 
love, saved by His substitution! A marvelous thing, a per- 
fect thing, this bride of the Christ, this church indwelt of 
the Holy Spirit! An organism—not an organiaztion—a 
divine organism, this church of Christ! 

O, but the church has become worldly. The church 
has made herself the friend of the world. We have mis- 
read a proposition. The Lord told us to take the world 
upon our hearts, and we have misread the word upon and 
made it into. And we have taken the world into our hearts. 
Thereby the white purity of His bride has been stained and 
soiled. We have been seduced, and many of us have played 
the harlot with the world. What a pity for the church of 
Christ! The bride needs to be cleansed. We need to pray 
with David: “Purge me with hyssop, and I shall be clean. 
Restore unto me the joys of thy salvation, then will I teach 
transgressors thy way and sinners shall be converted unto 
thee.” And I am believing tonight with all my heart that 
if the church of the living God should come once more to 
prayer, travailing, intercessory, even mediatorial prayer 
(as far as the mediatorial office belongs to the church), 
there would be a quickening of the hearts of men this wide 


RE NN SIS SS OTT Oe eT TEENY EE 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


world over. The church of Christ needs to pray for a re- 
vival within itself, and the cleansing, purifying, quickening, 
redeeming, empowering of its own life, and the definite en- 
ergy that will take hold of a dead world and dead souls and 
quicken them through Christ into the living body of the Son 
of God. 

The church of Christ needs to pray, and not alone for 
the revival of its body and the transformation of its life, 
but it needs to pray as it never has prayed that God will 
thrust laborers into the world field for its reaping. I am 
weary of having every newspaper thrust into our faces the 
fact that the number of theological students is falling off, 
that applicants for the ministry are decreasing: I am sick of 
the fact that letter after letter reaches my desk asking me 
to help find ministers who cannot be found for churches; 
or of the equally lamentable fact that, if one is found, he 
cannot be trusted to the high office of minister to preach 
the gospel of Jesus Christ faithfully. 

I know a church of 1200 members who gave $1,000,000 
for proxy services in three years, but in fifty years have 
never sent a single son or daughter out of its life into the 
gospel ministry. That is a living shame, a crying reproach 
to any church. Fifty years and not a single laborer ac-. 
cepted by the Lord of the harvest from its family for His 
field! i 

I am going to ask you this question: Have you ever. 
called your church together and prayed through for an hour 
that God would take your sons and daughters and thrust 
them out? In your prayer meetings do you pray that God 
would find workers among your sons and daughters? There 
was a young deacon in a country church who was led to 
pray for world-wide missions, and as he prayed his heart 


— 57 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


was mightily stirred and moved, and he said something like 
this: “O Lord God, our Heavenly Father, we thank Thee 
for this knowledge of Thy Son. For His sake, send, 
through our great national convention, some more men to 
preach the gospel. Find some mighty preachers of right- 
eousness and send them out into the world field.” Then he 
stopped praying and his brethren thought he had finished 
his prayer. But suddenly he took up the thread of his 
pleading again: “Not through our national convention, 
Lord, but through our state convention send out a force to 
the field.” Then he stopped and again resumed: “Not 
through any convention, O Lord, but come to this little 
church here in the country. Honor us by calling out from 
our church somebody to go and preach the gospel.” Then, 
burying his face in his hands, and moved with a mighty 
passion his very soul taking hold of God, he said: “God, I 
have an only baby, my first-born, and I love him, ah, how I 
love him, but God, won’t You take him, my little boy, and 
send him out?” That prayer was answered when they sent, 
twenty years later, this boy, J. B. Hartwell, to become one 
of the mightiest missionaries in the church of Jesus Christ. 
For half a century he lived and labored in China to make 
known the message of a prayer-hearing and prayer-answer- 
ing God. 

The church needs to intercede for the saving of lost 
souls, for the thrusting out of laborers, that we may fill up 
the sufferings of our Lord Jesus Christ and complete the 
task He gave us to do in making complete the body of 
Christ that the King may be brought back again! 

I looked into the face of a bride the other day. Her 
husband had kissed her goodbye and gone across the seas 
to do his duty as his country called him to it. I had seen 


— 58 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


that young bride, patient, brave, true, strong, faith- 
ful and courageous. One day finding her in a wistful mood 
I said: “What is the one great thought of your heart all 
the time? Would you mind telling me?” She replied: 
“Pastor, you do not need to ask. It is my beloved. I never 
think of anything else. I wake in the morning and think of 
him. There is no time when I am not thinking of him. 
All the day long I am thinking of him. It seems to me his 
name in my heart is a song which my lips are framing all 
the time. And, one of these days a ship is going to cast 
anchor in the harbor yonder and I will be there, and my 
arms will go out to him and about him. And if ever a 
man’s heart was comforted for the separation and anguish 
of waiting, his shall be comforted.” I wonder if any man 
can so little understand the church as to believe that she 
could live on with no passion of desire to greet once again 
the absent but returning bridegroom. He must come for 
His bride, or we should be forever widowed. How earnestly 
should we pray for His coming again. 

We ought to pray, because we cannot pray save in the 
Holy Ghost. 

Prayer is not made by spontaneous combustion. 
Prayer is not the result of an exercise of the will. Prayer 
comes only as the Holy Spirit works into the fiber of the 
being as desire, the very will of God. Prayer is wrought in 
before it can be voiced out. Prayer is wrought in by the 
Holy Spirit “who helpeth our infirmities.” O, what the 
church of Christ needs is a new infilling of the Holy 
Ghost! a new empowering of the Spirit of God; a new in- 
dwelling of this Divine One who searches us and tries us 
and proves us and empowers us and leads us when once He 
comes in to abide. We need to pray for the Holy Spirit’s em- 


isos 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


powering. And that leads me to say: there is only one condi- 
tion under which that empowering can come. That condi- 
tion is unconditional surrender on your part to His minis- 
try; the absolute yielding of your life to His indwelling. 
You must go all the way with Him if you want power. You 
must go all the way with Him if you would have joy. You 
must go all the way with Him if you want victory. You 
can have victory over sin, victory over danger, victory over 
fear, and victory in the midst of a world filled with per- 
plexity, but only at the price of absolute surrender to the 
indwelling Spirit of the living God. 

A friend of mine came to me at Northfield and said: 
“Come with me and I will show you the most beautiful and 
marvelous bit of scenery in America. So we started out 
through Camp Council up the mountainside. Many of you 
have taken the journey, Iam sure. We moved half way up 
and I said: “What are you going to show me—fir tops, 
rocks and blackberry bushes?” He said: “Come on up.” 
We moved three-quarters of the way and my nerves were 
gone, as well as my ambition. My friend said: “Come on 
up.” We went within fifty feet of the top, and below me 
all I could see were tops of fir trees, ragged rocks and 
blackberry bushes. I said: “You have deceived me, there 
is nothing here to be seen, nothing of beauty.” He put his 
hand under my arm and drew me up the last fifty feet of 
the way, and we stood upon a great flat rock there. “Now,” 
he said, “look!” I looked and for twenty miles above and 
twenty miles below, the Connecticut valley stretched out 
before me. 

In its meadows rich and verdant, beautiful with hun- 
dreds of gardens filled with multi-colored flowers, the birds 
sang their songs, the bees hummed their busy ways as they 


— 60 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


gathered honey, the fragrance of the flowers laded the 
breath of the wind with unthinkable sweetness. In the 
fields horses and farm hands vied with each other in the 
tilling of the soil, while in the pastures cows browsed as 
they sent up to us the tinkling melody of the bells they 
wore. 

It was a scene of indescribable pastoral beauty and 
charm. I felt as I looked down upon it as if I were being 
permitted to see a part of that very garden which God, the 
great Gardener, planted. But all of this beauty and charm 
indescribable came to me as the result of the last fifty feet 
of that upward climb. 

Do you desire power? Take the last fifty feet up to 
the heights with the Christ of God where your life is hid 
with Christ in God. Do you want victory? Go the last 
fifty feet of the way of surrender of your life to the Holy 
Spirit. Do you crave all that is needed of God’s grace in 
your life, in order that you may go out to do His service 
sufficiently equipped? Come the last step of the way into 
the holiest of holy places of perfect harmony with His will. 
O, church of Christ, leave the world; renounce its fellow- 
ship; live with Him in complete companionship, and He 
will take your life—individual or church life—and make it 
His dwelling place and use you according to His will in 
mighty power. There can be no doubt of His willingness 
to do this for you—for He waits to do it for His own sake. 

My last word is that men ought to pray because prayer 
is answered! In February of this year I had the very great 
privilege of participating in a conference similar to this in 
the Moody Institute in Chicago. Upon returning to my 
home I had a letter from a man in that city who signed 
himself “Colonel” somebody. He did not say the Colonel 


Cen he 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


of what, but after I read his letter I decided he was the 
kernel of a nut! I had not spoken on prayer at all. I 
spoke on the theme “In Christ,” and on the theme “Sanctifi- 
cation.” He wrote me a letter in which he said: “I see 
from the Tribune that you are some on prayer. I am writ- 
ing to challenge you. I have a certified check for $1,000 
which I will give you for one single answer to prayer. We 
will have a competent attorney draw up the contract and 
my check is ready for one single proved answer to prayer.” 
I would not waste one-half second with that sort of a fool! 
Bribe God to answer prayer! Come to Him to put Him to 
the test! Ask Him in pharasaical hypocrisy for a sign? 
No! But out of my whole life of forty-seven years I 
KNOW that the eternal God answers prayer. I am as cer- 
tain of that fact as I am that I stand here. And by your 
response to that statement I know I might carry to that 
poor deluded man scores upon scores of those who could 
give in detail their experience and testimony of answers to 
prayer. Why, God never refused answer to a prayer! If 
one time you reach through to God with a prayer you need 
not ever repeat it. Reach God but once with your prayer— 
I do not say your prayers—voice the word till it goes to 
your Father ONCE, and God Himself in heaven stands 
committed to give you what you ask. Pray—for prayer 
cannot go unanswered. Pray—for prayer is breaking 
through the crust of circumstance or sin to God with your 
life bare for His provision for your need. Prayer cannot 
go unanswered! You may ask for what you will—the heal- 
ing of your body, the cleansing of your soul, the empower- 
ing of your strength, the making plain of your path, the solu- 
tion of your problems, the settlement of your difficulties, the 
triumph over your enemies, the victory over your failures,

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the completion of the task He gave you to do, and if you 
will ask it in faith—GOD ANSWERS PRAYER. 

In this age of infidelity why is the church of the Christ 
of God so timid about putting Him to the test for the 
world’s need? Faith must reassert itself to ask in the con- 
viction that GOD ANSWERS PRAYER, and men ought 
to pray because God answers prayer. 

I stood under the high towering waterfall of Niagara, 
and a man told me that 160,000 horsepower had been har- 
nessed to do the will of man, and I said: “Marvelous!” 
But 3,600,000 horsepower remains to be harnessed that has 
not been touched, and I said: “Stupidity. One hundred 
and sixty thousand horsepower harnessed! Knowing that 
it can be done, and yet leave 3,600,000 horsepower unhar- 
nessed!”” Prayer will link the life of the church on to the 
eternal power of God and make if available for this world’s 
needs. 

I think I have had no experience in all my life that 
meant more to me than my first experience on board a trans- 
Atlantic liner. I did not go across that day. I went by 
courtesy of a friend into the Celtic when it was the greatest 
ship afloat, and we went down into the hold beneath the 
boilers. We saw stokers stripped to their waists, shoveling 
coal in preparation against sailing. An engineer, who 
proved to be a Baptist deacon from Wales, was extremely 
courteous and showed us the workings of the machinery. 
He showed us sixteen water-tight compartments, any one 
of which was sufficient to keep the ship afloat. Then he 
brought us to a little room about eight by ten feet, and 
said: “This is where I spend about two-thirds of my life.” 
I said: “This is the place from which you drive the ship 
across?” “Yes, this is the place and it is fifteen feet below 


a e 


=— 63 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


water.” I said: “And yet you drive this ship from this 
place, fifteen feet below water? What is to prevent you 
from running into an iceberg or another ship or on to a 
rock?” He smiled and said: “I am here in this little room 
fifteen feet below the surface of the sea, indeed, and I can- 
not see out, and I do not know the way across. But you 
see that dial hanging there in front of me, as large as a 
clock-face? That index finger is in touch with a similar 
arrangement before the man who stands upon the bridge. 
And the man on the bridge has the chart of the sea and the 
inerrant compass before him; and he has the glass in his 
hands and the sweep of the whole ocean. He knows the 
way, and when I am in perplexity or in doubt or fear, I 
touch this button and the message of my perplexity goes up 
to him. Then there comes back a message by the index 
finger of the dial, “This is the way, turn into it,’ and I sail 
the seas in perfect safety.” 

I had been seeking that way, and I climbed out of the 
hold of that ship conscious of the fact that though my life 
were circumscribed and I knew not the way, the Master on 
the bridge has the sweep of the eternities before Him, and 
there is a connecting link between us. So I cry up to Him: 
“Father !” and there is a voice, even the voice of the Spirit, 
which says: “This is the way, walk ye in it,” and we walk 
in it by faith. 

“Men ought to pray.” “Men ought to pray.” Let us 
believe it! The Father is far more willing to give than we 
are to ask. If the church desires a world revival it may be 
had at any time when the church asks for it in believing 
prayer. The effectual fervent prayer of a righteous church 
will avail much in its working. We ought always to pray, 
and not to faint.

THE WORD OF GOD—THE FOUNDATION OF 
THE FUNDAMENTALS 


Joseru Kyte, D.D., LL.D. 
President of Xenia Theological Seminary, Xenia, Ohio. 


Out of the many passages of Scripture that offer them- 
selves as a basis for the theme on which I am to speak this 
morning, viz., “The Fundamental of Fundamentals—The 
Word of God,” I have selected this: “Thy word is true 
from the beginning, and every one of Thy righteous judg- 
ments endureth forever.” It is found in Psalm 119, verse 
160. Whether we adopt the rendering of the Authorized 
Version, which has just been quoted; or that proposed by 
the Revisers, which reads: “The sum of Thy Word is 
truth,” etc., the Psalmist has thus asserted a claim for 
the Bible which gives it unrivalled distinction among all the 
books that have ever come into the hands of men. This 
is His very positive affirmation: “From everlasting God’s 
Word is true, and to everlasting it endures.” 

Thus are there two characteristics marked which are 
as peculiar to the Word of God as they are essential to its 
title to be reckoned the Fundamental of Fundamentals, and 
the ground of our sure hope, viz., Its Verity in its Entirety 
and Its Perpetuity in its Every Part. 

Three elements combine to give to the Bible its wonder- 
inspiring and faith-inviting verity, namely: trustworthiness, 
completeness and consistency or unity. 

The Bible has just claim upon our confidence. “Thy 
Word is truth,” said the Teacher who came from God. 


oo LOE ee eee ee 
2682 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Who will join controversy with Him? Its history is true. 
Its prophecies have never failed. Its promises are “Yea 
and Amen” in Jesus Christ, that by “two immutable things 
in which it is impossible for God to lie’—-His word and 
His oath—‘“we might have strong consolation.” And of 
its warnings and threatenings all that need be said is this, 
that thrice out of the heavenlies John heard the great voices 
say in the presence chamber of the Judge: “True and 
righteous are Thy judgments.” Of every matter that has 
eternal interest for human hearts, the message of the 
written Word is one with that of the Incarnate Word: 
“Tf it were not so, I would have told you.” 

God’s Word is also complete. Of the subjects with 
which it deals it tells us all we need to know. There are 
circumstances in which incompleteness is falsehood. A 
statement may be absolutely true in its every term as far 
as it goes, and yet it may be, by reason of defectiveness, as 
false as the Father of lies can make it. Witness the great 
Deceiver’s use of Scripture in the temptation of our Lord 
in the wilderness. But God’s Word lacks nothing that is 
essential to the accomplishment of the purpose that it is 
designed to serve. “It is profitable for doctrine, for re- 
proof, for correction, for instruction in righteousness, that 
the man of God may be complete, furnished completely 
unto every good work.” Over the “great gulf fixed,” which 
separates the home of the saved from the prison of the 
lost, these words were spoken long ago to one who had 
asked that his brethren, yet in the body and living in sin, 
might have additional revelation to warn them of impend- 
ing doom: “If they hear not Moses and the prophets 
neither will they be persuaded though one rose from the 
dead,” If this was true then, how all-sufficient must be 


EE SE SE SS eS SS SS 


— 66 — 


Ti, 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the message that now speaks “as the truth is in Jesus?” 

Yet again, God’s Word has consistency, it holds to- 
gether; it has unity; it has integrity, it is an integer. Our 
English term, “integer,” comes from the Latin verb, “in- 
tango”; in its participial form, “Tntectus,” intact, untouched. 
In spite of all that has been attempted to establish the 
contrary, we dare assert that this Word of God is one 
from first to last. Now unity is a very essential factor in 
the problem of the verity of the Scriptures. Indeed there 
is none greater. Hence it is with this feature we shall be 
for the most part concerned. 


Coming to men in the character which it professes to 
possess, and with the purpose which it professes to serve, 
namely: to reveal to them their relation to God, and the 
privilege and the obligations that grow out-of this relation; 
covering a period of at least one thousand years in its 
preparation—for the sake of the argument, let us allow 
the critic to indicate the length of the period that will be 
allowed—and comprehending in its matter the work of so 
many individual writers; the Bible must have unity if its 
verity is to be acknowledged; and if it have unity no rea- 
sonable mind may justly doubt its verity. 


We hold that the Bible is distinguished by a unity of 
design and a harmony of doctrine throughout its every 
portion from the beginning to the close. This is acknowl- 
edged, first of all, in the title which is applied to it by foe 
as well as friend. Originally Biblia, the Books, but “in the 
thirteenth century,” says Dr. Westcott, quoted by Dr. James 
Orr, “by a happy solecism the neuter plural came to be 
regarded as a feminine singular, and ‘the Books’ became 
by common consent ‘the Book,’ in which form the word 


— 67 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


’ 


has passed into all the languages of modern Europe.” In 
this Book we have the most wonderful “E Pluribus Unum” 
that the whole history of the world presents. 

This unity, which is so manifest that only eyes that 
have been blinded by the god of this world have refused 
to recognize it, can be accounted for upon the one principle 
that its authorship is traceable to one mind, and that the 
mind of One who is infinite and eternal and unchangeable, 
knowing the end from the beginning, and never departing 
from His perfect plan nor swerving by a hair’s breadth 
from His single, all comprehensive purpose. In the light 
of the facts that are acknowledged alike by friend and by 
foe, if one has not wholly lost the sense of the wonderful, 
this Bible, marked by definite plan and undeviating purpose 
and unfaltering progress despite the opposition of men and 
of devils, this Bible must be reckoned the marvel of the 
ages. 

Some fifteen hundred years passed from the time when 
Moses began to speak from God as he was moved by the 
Holy Spirit until John was prompted by the same Spirit of 
truth to set upon the Book of books the seal that none may 
break, marking the close of the Apocalypse; for whether 
we reckon this record of wondrous visions, or the Evangel 
that is ascribed to his pen as the last inspired message to 
the world, in either case the beloved disciple was led to 
affix a seal which only sacrilege or blasphemy will touch. 
During these centuries at least forty writers, representing 
almost as many orders and classes in human society, were 
employed in giving this Book of God to the world, the 
vocabularies of three languages furnishing the means of 
communication. Some of the greatest and some of the 
lowliest of God’s people were found in this number. Some 


RS 


— 68 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


were men of the highest culture, trained in schools, over 
which the greatest universities of our day, in view of 
twentieth century advantages, have less reason to glory than 
to be ashamed; others were rated by their contemporaries 
as “unlearned and ignorant men.” 

In that divinely chosen company were prophets and 
priests and kings. There were prime-ministers, and cup- 
bearers to princes, statesmen, judges, generals, legislators, 
doctors, farmers, fishermen, and tax-gatherers. The cir- 
cumstances to which their service was rendered were as 
complex and varied as were the classes from which the 
writers sprang, or the characters which they bore, or the 
times in which they wrought. Let the familiar scenes in 
the lives of some of these be recalled. (And who will say 
that their unrecorded experiences were less marvellous?) 
Think of Moses, of Joshua, of David, of Solomon, of 
Isaiah, of Jeremiah, of Ezekiel, of Daniel and other proph- 
ets of the Old Testament; and of Peter and John and Luke 
among the writers of the New Covenant’s messages to men. 
Gather out of a like stretch of years a like company of 
men and assign to them the task of producing a treatise 
on any subject that has to do with morals and religion, 
leaving them to the guidance of their own unaided powers 
and native sense of propriety, and what a jumble would 
result! Let forty of the high priests of the critical schools 
of our time, who reckon themselves the scholars of the 
world and profess to know exactly what the Bible is not 
and what it ought to be, furnish us with a volume dealing 
with like lofty themes to take the Bible’s place and we shall 
see whether or not a unity such as is a crowning distinction 
of the Word of God is possible without the supervision and 
control of the God to whom the Bible owes its claim to 


— 69 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


be believed. This is a challenge which destructive criticism 
dare not accept. 

The unity of God’s Word finds still further illustration 
and example in the oneness of the subject which is the 
burden of its message from the beginning to the end. 

Jonathan Edwards well styled the Bible “The History 
of Redemption.” Whatever may be the form or method 
or character of its teaching this distinguishing feature of 
its revelation stands out in notable fullness and clearness. 
Not only to the ears that have been opened and the eyes 
that have been unsealed, but also to minds that are yet 
dull and to hearts that are yet slow to believe, it is manifest 
that the Bible has one great theme from its opening sen- 
tences to its final words. One scarlet thread runs through 
its many volumes and binds all into one. “The testimony 
of Jesus is the Spirit of prophecy,’ writes John in The 
Revelation. This is a claim that bears equally upon both 
Testaments. This is most definitely asserted by our Lord 
Himself, when, employing a term which challenges the 
most scrutinizing investigation, He said to the Jews: 
“Search the Scriptures, for in them ye think ye have eternal 
life and they are they which testify of me.” It was into 
His mouth also the Psalmist put like words long before His 
advent, saying: “In the volume of the Book it is written of 
me.” In company with His disciples it was His wont fre- 
quently to open unto them the Scriptures. “Beginning 
from Moses and from all the prophets He interpreted to 
them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.” 
Thus does Luke record this fact in his account of the first 
interview after His resurrection. Thank God for this 
divine interpreter.”—Elihu’s “One among a thousand.” 
The Old Testament would indeed be a labyrinthian maze 


—70— 


EE EE Ee SEL ey a a I 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 
ae a 


through which one must wander without hope of finding 
either resting-place or exit, but for the crimson cord— 
better far than Ariadne’s thread—which is never out of 
sight; and as the Holy Spirit puts it into the hand that has 
submitted to His guidance, Himself meanwhile also holding 
it fast, the way is made plain even to the gates of pearl. 

There is no more interesting exercise to the devout 
soul than is furnished by the patient, trustful following 
of the steps that show the print of the nails throughout 
the way which the Law and the Prophets and the Psalms 
mark out. The starting point is in Eden. There the 
triumph of the woman’s seed, and the venomous malice 
of the serpent were foretold: “It shall bruise thy head, and 
thou shalt bruise his heel.” Next these footprints appear 
at the altar of Abel, and yet again at the door of the Ark, 
having entered with Noah when the rains began and coming 
out when the flood had abated, to go up and down through- 
out the world so cursed with sin; and never to disappear 
until the new heavens and the new earth wherein dwelleth 
righteousness shall take their place in the holy universe 
of God. 

In illustration of this unity which characterizes the 
Word of God, this harmony of doctrine with which the 
redemption that is in Christ Jesus is displayed “by divers 
portions and in divers manners,” the Bible furnishes a 
great variety of types and symbols. Each has its own 
peculiar office to serve, its own particular phase of truth 
to foreshadow and represent in setting forth the one great 
subject of divine revelation; but all are needed to give 
worthy exhibition of the mercy that is in the heavens, the 
faithfulness that reacheth unto the clouds, the righteousness 
that is like the great mountains, and the judgments that

are a mighty deep. With their many voices—like the 
“sound of many waters’—but with not one discordant 
tone, they all unite in the blessed proclamation: “Behold, 
the Lamb of God, that taketh away the sin of the world!” 
There are typical persons, such as Melchizedec, King of 
Salem, i. e., King of Peace—who is also King of Right- 
eousness by name, and Priest of the Most High God. It 
is he of all the priests who ever officiated at God’s altar 
who is chosen to represent the priestly line and order in 
which Christ came. Aaron also in his High-Priestly station 
in Israel, and his peculiar service on his people’s behalf, 
shares in the typical representation of Him who is both 
priest and sacrifice to the great end of taking away sin. 
Moses stands at the head of the prophetical line, announc- 
ing the coming of One who should be God’s own prophet 
and yet a brother-man; One by whose advent the almost 
hopelessness of Job, prostrate under the hand of the 
Almighty should be exchanged for the blessed hope; for 
never again will any son of affliction, face to face with 
God under the shadow of some mysterious providence, 
have reason to say: “Neither is there a daysman between 
us who might lay his hand upon us both.” In David the 
royalty of the Savior who is Christ the Lord is typified, 
and in some of the experiences of the man who, in a 
special sense, was divinely designated to Israel’s throne, 
there was the dark foreshadowing that a crown of thorns 
must mar the Messiah’s brow as a necessary anticipation 
of the crown of glory that should be His as King of kings 
and Lord of Lords. The King, Who is David’s Lord as 
well as David’s son, Himself has said it—proclaiming Him- 
self the conqueror and the Lord of Death and Hell even 
as He spoke the words: “Ought not the Christ to have

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


suffered these things and to enter into His glory?” Of 
this fundamental Gospel truth every sacrificial rite of the 
fiery Law was the definite fore-announcement. 


There were also typical scenes and incidents under the 
legal dispensation that prefigured Gospel principles, and 
therefore bear witness to the glorious unity and harmony 
of the Book of books. 


Not to multiply references to these, call to mind the 
lifting up of the brazen serpent on the banner-pole, that 
the Israelite through whose veins the fiery venom of the 
serpent was_ coursing, whose eyes were glazing under the 
touch of death, might look and live. Jesus Christ saw in 
this gracious provision for the recovery of men, who were 
reaping the harvest of their own sinful sowing, a most 
signal illustration of His work in redeeming men from the 
curse of the Law by being made a curse for them. “As 
Moses lifted up the serpent in the wilderness, even so 
must the Son of Man be lifted up; that whosoever believ- 
eth may in Him have eternal life.” Who has equal right 
with Him to put interpretation upon this act in which Law 
bears accordant witness with Gospel? Think also of the 
Manna, the bread of angels, that fed Israel’s hunger for 
forty years; and of the Rock in the wilderness from whose 
stricken side floods of water streamed to quench their 
thirst. Let Paul be our inspired interpreter of these mar- 
velous events, and also our authoritative harmonist of the 
teaching of the Old Testament and the New. “I would 
nof, brethren, have you ignorant, how that our fathers were 
all under the cloud, and all passed through the sea; and 
were all baptized unto Moses in the cloud and in the sea; 
and did all eat the same spiritual meat; and did all drink 


—73— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the same spiritual drink; for they drank of that spiritual 
Rock that followed them; and that Rock was Christ.” 
(I Cor. 18: 1-4.) 

Not less distinctly, but rather with augmented signifi- 
cance does the truth appear, that “The New Testament 
lies concealed in the Old and the Old Testament stands 
revealed in the New,” as the noble phrase of Augustine 
has it, in the typical sacrifices of the former dispensation. 
In this respect it is the wealth and not the poverty of 
example that perplexes the seeker after truth. Let pass- 
ing notice be made, first of all, of that most notable incident 
recorded in Genesis 3:21, “And Jehovah God made for 
Adam and for his wife coats of skins and covered them.” 
The word of condemnation had been spoken, but sentence 
of expulsion from the Garden had not yet been announced. 
Mercy at once runs before judgment, and never from that 
day onward has she fallen behind. Before the victims of 
their own sin go into exile provision is made for the cover- 
ing of their nakedness and the relief of their sense of 
shame. But whence came the covering? Innocent crea- 
tures that had been their companions in that happy home 
out of which they are soon to go must give their life, that 
through their sacrifice the melancholy consequences of sin 
might in some measure be averted. Vicarious sacrifice 
had its origin in Eden even before the doom of exile had 
fallen upon our race. Thenceforward the golden truth 
which lies at the heart of the Old Testament and of the 
New alike is this: “Where sin abounded grace did much 
more abound: that as sin hath reigned unto death even so 
might grace reign, through righteousness, unto eternal life, 
by Jesus Christ our Lord.” 

In the Passover also we have a distinguishing mark of 


SEAE 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the unity of God’s plan, and of the Bible which reveals 
His method of grace. Instituted to commemorate the de- 
liverance of Israel from bondage by the mighty hand of 
God, it had nevertheless a significance in which all nations 
of the world have interest. For believers of every age 
and people and tongue have been taught to say: “Our 
Passover also hath been sacrificed, even Christ.” Calvary 
is the realization of the Passover promise of hope. The 
night of. Israel’s deliverance was one “long to be remem- 
bered,” Calvary’s day is one which to all eternity shall 
never be forgotten. 

It is true that the Law, “having a shadow of the good 
things to come, and not the very image of the things” 
themselves, must necessarily have temporary and incom- 
plete character as a revelation of the will and the purpose 
of God; but let it be marked that it is the very sunlight 
which reveals the substance that causes the casting of the 
shadow; and that there must, in some notable sense, be 
points of resemblance between them. A tree cannot be 
made to cast the shadow of a man. They may not be 
substituted the one for the other, even as the darkness and 
the light of the day may not exchange places. Separately 
they do not speak with the same tones nor utter the same 
words. The Law says: “The soul that sinneth it shall 
die!” The Gospel says: “Deliver from going down to the 
pit, I have found a ransom.” But they are never marked 
by contradiction. Together—the Gospel being the fulfill- 
ment of the Law—they speak to the world the one great 
truth for all the ages: “The wages of sin is death; but the 
free gift of God is eternal life in Jesus Christ our Lord.” 
Calvary announces the first of these complemental phases 
of the one eternal truth even more emphatically than does

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Sinai; and Sinai hushes its thunders to speak in the “still, 
small voice” from the “cleft of the rock” the blessed assur- 
ance that is couched in the terms of the second. The Holy 
of Holies had the Law. In its dread presence who might 
stand? But “that which was against us” was covered with 
the Mercy Seat. That Mercy Seat with the cherubim of 
glory overshadowing was the throne of the invisible God. 
“There will I mect with thee, and I will commune with 
thee from above the Mercy Seat.” That Mercy Seat, Kap- 
‘poreth, represents Jesus Christ and His all perfect right- 
eousness, covering the Law, satisfying to the full measure 
its every requirement. And the cherubim, formed out of 
one solid piece of gold with the Mercy Seat, represent 
manhood redeemed and glorified in Christ Jesus—those to 
whom to live is Christ, in Christ and Christ in them the 
hope of glory. Bending over with faces downward they 
recognize that all they have and all they are they owe to 
Him who died for them and rose again. In communion 
with Him “Who dwelleth between the cherubim”, in min- 
gled supplication and thanksgiving they seem to say: “Be- 
hold, O God, our shield, and look upon the face of thine 
Anointed.” This petition uttered by the Psalmist a thous- 
and years before the advent of our Lord, we take today 
and make it the expression of our deepest longing and our 
fondest hope. The same Holy Spirit who then gave it 
origin, now makes it a most suitable help to our infirmity 
in prayer. 

It is a source of great comfort and assurance to note 
that the unity of the Bible is such as the very gates of Hell 
cannot destroy. The fiercest darts of criticism have found 
in it a very rock of adamant and they have fallen brunted 
and broken at its foot.

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


In the first efforts at dismemberment of the Scrip- 
tures, “J” and “E” had no companions. Then “D” and 
“P” came to their help. By and by in the critics’ extremity 
it was found necessary to have additional assistance, and 
then by some mysterious power the Redactor, “R,” was 
summoned and he came. But not yet was the stress re- 
lieved, and soon at least three “Js,” and three “Es,” and 
four “Ps,” and three “Rs,” and three “Qs,” are all enlisted 
in the common cause. Still later all of these seem to have 
been merged into “extensive schools,” in the desperate at- 
tempt to find “sources” sufficiently numerous to meet the 
exigencies of the case; namely, the critics’ multiplicity of 
assignments. Nevertheless, the critics treat the Bible as 
one Book. They find these “schools” scattered throughout 
the whole, practically common to all, and not a few repre- 
sentatives here and others there. The multitude of writers 
and editors have all to do with one revelation—if the critic 
will allow us to employ this term in speaking of the Scrip- 
tures. 

A still more significant fact appears on making com- 
parisons among critics; for it is well known that there is 
great difference in the representations that are made as to 
the character of the Bible and as to the effect of criticism 
upon the trustworthiness of the book. Wellhausen is re- 
ported to have said, in answer to William Robertson 
Nichol’s inquiry, whether or not the Bible could retain its 
authority over the minds and hearts of men in the light in 
which criticism had placed it, “I do not see how it can.” 
There is little reason for surprise in this judgment, coming 
as it does from one who confessed himself a “polytheist.” 
Kuenen’s estimate of the Bible’s authority may very readily 
‘be estimated, if the statement of his biographer is true.

According to that statement, this prince among destructive

acknowledged his indebtedness to them. But others, as 
Doctor Briggs, and Professor Driver, and Sir George 
Adam Smith, are on record as repeatedly affirming that the 
“critical conclusions” which they have accepted are not “in 
conflict either with the Christian creeds or the articles. of 
the Christian faith,” and that neither the inspiration nor 
the authority of the Scriptures is injuriously affected. 
Knowing what these “critical conclusions” are, we are as- 
tonished beyond measure at such professions of orthodoxy, 
and of confidence in the Bible. What is the explanation of 
this anomaly? What can it be but this: German criticism 
has swayed their reason, but it cannot wholly dominate 
their conscience. Under the spell of German scholarship, or 
in dread of being condemned by critical inquisitors, they 
have denied that the Bible is altogether what it claims to 
be; but removed from this tribunal, Galileo-like, their own 
Christian consciousness compels the confession, neverthe- 
less, 1t 1s the Word of God. It is a tacit acknowledgment 
of the unshaken integrity of the Scriptures that these men, 
while they talk of the “assured results of criticism” in the 
speech of Ashdod, employ the language of Israel as they 
confess their inspiration and authority. In spite of the 
presumptuous claims of its open enemies, and the feeble 
concessions of its timid friends, the sum of God’s word is 
truth. It is true from the beginning. Its unity has not 
been broken—its integrity has not been impaired. We have 
yet, for length and breadth, all the truth that lies between 
the vision of the sinless garden and the apocalypse of the 
Holy City; for height and depth, all the truth that sep-

arates yet links together the blessed announcement of a

and completeness, all the truth that compasses the all- 
sufficiency of Jesus Christ and the all-deficiency of man; 
for center and circumference and comprehension, Him 
who “was and is and is to come,” “all and in all,” “the way, 
and the truth, and the life.” 

But we have not yet reached the bounds of this text. 
From everlasting God’s Word is true, and to everlasting it 
endures. 

Here is a Word that has perpetuity, ay, it has eter- 
nity. Solomon has said that God has “set eternity in the 
heart.” Surely this is true of the heart in which His Word 
is hidden. What a priceless treasure in a world of fleeting 
change! 

When Moses addressed Israel for the last time from 
the foot of Nebo his eye was not dim and his strength gave 
no sign of decay, although he had borne the burdens of 
more than a century; nevertheless he went to the summit 
of the mountain to die. The hand that had wrought such 
wonders in Egypt and the wilderness, and the tongue that 
had spoken judgments such as never fell from the lips of 
any other only human soon moldered into grave-dust. 
Every other human hand and human tongue that had a 
part in the first proclamation of this Word of Life, save 
those of Enoch and Elijah, had a like end; but the Word of 
the Lord lives on. The heralds of the centuries past have 
gone to receive their crowns; but the Word of the Lord 
remains. One minister may go and another may come; 
but the Word of the Lord abides. Egypt, Assyria, Baby- 
lon, Persia, Greece, Rome, and other peoples whose history 
is interwoven with the chronicles of Israel, have perished,

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


and the covenant nation itself is “scattered and peeled;” 
but the Word of the Lord endures, to remind the scattered 
prodigals of the gracious promise of restoration to the 
Father’s favor. 

Macaulay’s New Zealander may one day “take his 
stand on a broken arch of London Bridge to sketch the 
ruins of St. Paul’s,” events at least as wonderful have taken 
place in our world within the last five years. But if ever 
this strange fancy shall have become a stranger fact, out of 
the vast solitude this prophecy from the heavens shall 
thunder in his ears: “The grass withereth, the flower 
fadeth; * * * * * * surely the people is grass; but the 
Word of our God shall stand forever.” And when the 
“heavens shall roll together as a scroll, and the elements 
shall melt with fervent heat,” that most stupendous of 
physical changes, and most fraught with momentous con- 
sequence, will itself be but the fulfillment of this great, 
almighty Word which liveth and abideth forever. 

Closely linked with the principle of perpetuity—indeed 
one of its essentially constituent elements—is that of effi- 
ciency. It cannot endure if it be unable to accomplish the 
purpose it was intended to serve. But here fear of failure 
may at once be flung to the winds. Hear the declaration 
of the eternal God of truth. “My word shall not return 
unto me void; but it shall accomplish that which I please, 
and it shall prosper in the thing whereto I sent it.” This 
is the firm foundation which standeth sure, our ground of 
hope as we speak its message to the world. Relying upon 
pledges such as this, David, filled with the Spirit, sum- 
moned all the lands to praise, calling upon them to serve 
the Lord with gladness and to come into His presence with 
singing, Not content with the issuing of the summons, he

sounded also the note of triumphant anticipation: “All 
the ends of the earth shall remember and turn unto Je- 
hovah; and all the kindreds of the nations shall worship

Psalmist of Israel” might have looked almost to the 
utmost boundaries of the entire territory which then owned 
the sway of the King of kings, nevertheless in faith in God 
he sent out the call to every nation and people and tongue. 
To what sublimity of confidence does he rise in that one 
hundred and seventeenth Psalm. “Oh praise Jehovah, all 
ye nations; Laud Him, all ye peoples. For his loving 
kindness is great toward us; and the truth of Jehovah 
endureth forever. Praise ye Jehovah.” Thus does he 
anticipate the glorious day when his own “Hallelujah 
Chorus” shall be upon every tongue, circling the world 
with praise to the Redeemer-King, and Zion’s songs, as 
well as Zion’s sons, shall have come back to their own. 
In like exultant hope and triumphant faith Paul 
preached this single, eternal word of God’s grace. He 
knew nothing among men but the one great theme of the 
Scriptures, “Jesus Christ and Him crucified.” To the Jew 
such a Gospel was a stumbling-block, to the Greek it was 
foolishness; nevertheless Paul dared to affirm that God had 
highly exalted this crucified Christ, and had “given Him a 
name which is above every name, that at the name of Jesus

should confess that Jesus Christ is Lord to the glory of 
God the Father.” O brethren, I covet for myself, and for 
us all, the faith of David and of Paul; the faith that could 
thus look out over millenniums of years and uncounted 
leagues of land and sea, and claim all the world for Jesus

Christ. “We see not yet all things put under Him.” The

“It fortifies my soul to know 

That, though I perish, truth is so; 

That howsoe’er I stray or range, 

Whate’er I do (He) does not change. 

I steadier step when I recall,

There is a scene pictured in the Gospel story which is 
an unfailing source of comfort to witnesses for the truth, 
as the truth is in Jesus; assuring them as it does of their 
Lord’s present interest in the struggle between light and 
darkness, truth and error, and of the speedy triumph that 
shall come in His own best time. The twelve disciples are 
in a little fishing boat in the midst of Galilee, hopelessly 
struggling against the power of the sea. It is a time of

Lord is praying. But they are not forgotten, nor can cloud 
and darkness hide them from His sight. “He saw them 
toiling in rowing ;” and “making the cause of their dismay 
the means of His approach,” (the fine phrase of Doctor

McLaren), walking on the waves that threatened to engulf

although but a moment before they were in the midst of the
