THE BIBLE’S TESTIMONY TO ITS OWN 
INSPIRATION 


James M. Gray, D. D. 


Dean of the Moody Bible Institute of Chicago; author of “Synthetic 
Bible Studies,” “Great Epochs of Sacred History,” “The 
Christian Workers Commentary” “How to 

Master the English Bible,” etc, 


The title at once raises a question and awakens a 
challenge. 

Why should the witness of the Bible be accepted as to 
its inspiration? 

Is it not reasoning in a circle to do so? Is it not like 
accepting a man’s own testimony that he tells the truth 
about himself ? 

To this we reply that had we sufficient evidence out- 
side of the man himself that it was his habit always to tell 
the truth, we might then reasonably believe him even when 
he spoke about himself. 

And this is the case for the Bible. The evidence of its 
credibility as to matters of fact is beyond controversy, and 
growing stronger with every decade. 

It is not the province of this address to go into that; 
but on the assumption that the Bible may be believed upon 
other matters it is fair to assume that it may be believed 
about itself. 


I. What then does it say as to its own inspiration? 

To begin with the Old Testament, the late Dr. James 

H. Brookes is sponsor for the statement that the phrase, 
Helpful books by the above author listed on pages 446-449. 


es wi EE nn 
SARDES 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


“Thus saith the Holy Ghost,” or its equivalent, is used at 
least 2,000 times in the Old Testament. To be sure this is 
an evidence of revelation rather than inspiration, and there 
is a strong difference between the two. As Doctor Chalmers 
puts it, the one is the influx, the other the efflux. One is 
divine information communicated by God to men and the 
other is divine information communicated by God through 
man to other men. 

But when the phrase occurs 2,000 times in a writing 
no larger than the Old Testament it approaches inspiration 
so closely as to be almost identical with it. 

To illustrate, take the book of Leviticus. It has 
twenty-seven chapters, and the expression, “The Lord 
spake unto Moses,” or its equivalent, occurs fifty-four 
times, or an average of twice for every chapter. To be 
sure, it does not say that Moses wrote what the Lord thus 
spake to him, but the inference is so strong that Moses, 
and none other, must have written it, as to make the rev- 
elation and the inspiration of that book practically one and 
the same. Therefore, we are not surprised to find the 
author of the Epistle to the Hebrews (9:6-8) declaring the 
Holy Ghost to be the one who caused the directions for 
the Tabernacle services to be recorded in Leviticus. 

THE PSALMIST AND THE PROPHETS 

But we are not limited to such an argument; for when 
we reach the work of David, perhaps the second most vol- 
uminous human author of the Old Testament, he says ex- 
plicitly, “The Spirit of the Lord spake by me, and His 
Word was in my tongue” (II Samuel 23:2). 

And our Lord Himself confirms this statement. In 
Matthew 22, when the Pharisees in reply to His question, 
said that Christ was simply the Son of David, He inquired: 


(EEE 


SR ae 


ES 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


SC 
Tao aan aaa 


“How then, doth David in the Spirit, call Him Lord if He 
is His son?” In other words, the 110th Psalm containing 
those words of David was inspired of God, according to 
Jesus Christ, and if so, were not his other Psalms likewise 
inspired? 

It is of still another Psalm that Peter speaks in Acts 
1:16, when urging upon the apostles a successor to Judas: 
“Men and brethren,” said he, “this scripture must needs 
have been fulfilled, which the Holy Ghost by the mouth of 
David spake before concerning Judas, which was guide to 
them that took Jesus.” It is the 41st Psalm that is in 
question here. 


The early church had no hesitancy in ascribing the 
2d Psalm to the Holy Spirit in the same way, testifying 
that God “by the mouth of Thy servant, David, hath said, 
Why did the nations rage?” (Acts 4:25.) 


The 95th Psalm is similarly identified as to its divine 
authorship in Hebrews 4. At the third verse we have, 
“Even as He (the Holy Ghost) hath said;” at verse 4, 
“For He (the Holy Ghost) hath said;” at verse 7, “He 
(the Holy Ghost) again defineth a certain day, saying in 
David,” etc. 

And the books of the prophets are declared to be in- 
spired in the same manner as the Psalms. 

For example, in Acts 28:25, Paul says, “Well spake 
the Holy Ghost by Isaiah the prophet,” and then follows a 
quotation from Isaiah. 

In Hebrews 10:15, it is written, “And the Holy Ghost 


also is a witness to us; for after that He hath said,” and 
then follows a quotation from Jeremiah. 


a e a s a a o e aS 


NE yA 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


GENERAL STATEMENTS 

But not to go further into detail, we find such general 
statements of the inspiration of the Old Testament as those 
in I Peter 1:10, 11; II Peter 1:21 and II Tim. 3:16. 

In the first-named, we read that “the prophets inquired 
* * * * what, or what manner of time, the Spirit of Christ 
which was in them did signify when He testified before- 
hand the sufferings of Christ and the glory that should 
follow ?” 

Here is a clear witness that it was not so much the 
prophets themselves who recorded these predictions about 
the first and second coming of Christ, but the Holy Spirit 
who recorded them through the prophets. And indeed the 
latter were so far from understanding the nature and mean- 
ing of what they wrote, that after it was recorded by them, 
they began to study it for themselves. 

The second passage tells us that “no prophecy ever 
came in old time by the will of man; but men spake from 
God being moved by the Holy Ghost.” 

It is capable of proof that “prophecy” in this case 
means written prophecy, and that the word is not limited to 
the prediction of events, but includes whatever God has 
caused to be recorded without reference to the element of 
time. 

ARGUMENT For II Timoruy 3:16 

The third is that well-known testimony of Paul: “All 
scripture is given by inspiration of God, and is profitable 
for doctrine, for reproof, for correction, for instruction in 
righteousness: That the man of God may be complete, 
completely furnished unto all good works.” 

The phrase “inspiration of God” means literally, “God- 
breathed.” “All scripture is God-breathed.” 


at Sa feeeee 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


The Revised Version renders this a little differently, 
giving an opportunity to cavillers to say on the basis of it, 
that some Scripture is inspired of God, and some is not, 
and that the “profitableness” extends to the former and not 
the latter. The precise words of the Revised Version are 
“Every scripture inspired of God is also profitable.” 

But we reply (1) that Paul would hardly express so 
weak a truism as that; (2) that the King James version is 
the better Greek in the sense that it is more consistent with 
the similar construction in other places of the New Testa- 
ment; (3) that a large proportion of the revisers voted 
against the change; (4) that the revisers place the King 
James rendering in the margin as of practically equal auth- 
ority with the text; (5) that some devout scholars, like 
Gaussen and Wordsworth, for example, staunch defenders 
of plenary inscription, accept the revised version, and by 
interpolating a word for which they believe they have 
authority, make it even a stronger statement than the other, 
viz., “Every scripture (because) inspired of God is also 
profitable.” 

The limits and the character of this address will not 
justify a fuller treatment of this text, but I may add that I 
accept the King James rendering at its full face value, “All 
scripture is given by inspiration of God.” 

II. But thus far we have been dwelling on the in- 
spiration of the Old Testament only; and yet if that is in- 
spired it almost goes without saying that the New Testa- 
ment must be inspired also, because it is the later and more 
important revelation of the two. 

The opening verses of the first and second chapters of 
Hebrews warrant our saying this, which read: “God, who 
at sundry times and in divers manners spake in times past 


a 


Dgo 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


unto the fathers by the prophets, hath in these last days 
spoken unto us by His Son, * * * Therefore, we ought to 


give the more earnest heed to the things which we have 
heard.” 


And this inference is rendered all the stronger by the 
fact that, as Gaussen says, the New Testament sometimes 
explains, sometimes proves, and sometimes even dares to 
repeal ordinances of the Old Testament. For an illustra- 
tion of the last, take Galatians 5:6, “For in Jesus Christ, 
neither circumcision availeth anything, nor uncircumcision.” 
One could hardly require a stronger evidence of the author- 
ity of the New Testament over the Old than this and hence 
its divine inspiration. 


Furthermore, the New Testament writers were es- 
pecially qualified for their task. The Holy Ghost would 
teach them all things and brings all things to their remem- 
brance, Christ said. He would show them things to come. 
They were promised inspiration of the most absolute char- 
acter as to what they should speak on certain occasions 
when their words would be but limited and temporary; how 
much more likely therefore that they would receive it for 
what they should write when it was to cover the whole 
range of revelation and to last forever? (Matt. 10:19, 20; 


John 14: 26, etc.) 


It may be added that the New Testament writers 
directly claim inspiration. In Acts 15:23-29, James says, 
“It seemed good to the Holy Ghost, and to us, to lay upon 
you no greater burden than these necessary things.” This 
is a very clear affirmation that the Holy Ghost is the real 
writer of the letter to the Gentile churches which then 
follows, 


— 90 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


In Ephesians, Paul speaks of the mystery of the 
church “which in other generations was not made known 
unto the'sons of men, as it is now revealed unto His holy 
apostles and prophets by the Spirit.” This revelation was 
incident to its recording by one of these same apostles in 
this letter. 

Again in II Peter 3:2, the Apostle places his own 
words on a level with those of the prophets of the Old Tes- 
tament, and in verses 15 and 16 he does the same with the 
writings of Paul, classifying them as he does, “with the 
other scriptures.” 

III. Having now produced the direct evidence of the 
Bible to the inspiration both of the Old and New Testa- 
ments, there arise certain questions to be answered. 

For instance: First 

WHAT Is MEANT By INSPIRATION? 

To this we can only say that it is an enduement of the 
Holy Spirit coming upon the writers of the Old and New 
Testaments directing and enabling them to write those 
books free from all error. 

And to this we must add that it is unique in the sense 
that it came on no other men, at no other time and for no 
other purpose. 7 

It is not human genius, in other words, which is a 
natural gift to some men; neither is it spiritual illumina- 
tion, which is a spiritual gift in a greater or less degree to 
all regenerated men. Inspiration is different from either of 
` these things. 

Its mode is inscrutable. We do not know how the 
Holy Spirit operated on the minds of the human authors 
to produce the books, any more than we know how He op- 
erates in human souls in regeneration. We simply accept 


Bop 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the one mystery, as we do the other, on the testimony of 
the Word of God and of that experience which appeals to 
faith. “The wind bloweth where it listeth, and thou canst 
not tell whence it cometh, and whither it goeth; so is every- 
one that is born of the Spirit.” And so, it may be said, is 
every one who has been inspired by the Spirit to record 
God’s word. 

Second. DorEs THIS INSPIRATION EXTEND TO THE 
TRANSLATIONS OR VERSIONS OF THE BIBLE. And if not, 
what practical value has it for us, inasmuch as the originals 
have long since perished? 

We reply that the inspiration is limited to the original 
autographs and does not apply in the same sense to the 
versions or translations; nevertheless the science of Biblical 
criticism has given us in our English translation, the Re- 
vised Version for example, that which for all practical pur- 
poses is the very autographs of the inspired writers. 

To illustrate the point, take the Declaration of Inde- 
pendence. How many of us have ever seen the original 
document? How many know whether it still exists? And 
yet if it were destroyed or lost, how many of us doubt that 
exact copies of it are to be found in all our school histories 
and in the libraries of other nations? 

As a matter of fact, the opponents of plenary inspira- 
tion have never set much value on any argument growing 
out of the fact that we do not possess the original docu- 
ments. Builders do not drive piles into the earth if they 
never expect to touch bottom, and Biblical critics would 
not dispute about the Scripture text, and minutely examine 
the history and meaning of single words if at the end there 
were no close approximate to an absolute. As Dr. George 
H. Bishop once said, “Our concordance every time we take 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


it up, speaks loudly to us of a once inerrant parchment.” 

Third. That word “inerrant” suggests a third inquiry, 
namely as to THE EXTENT OF THE INSPIRATION OF THE 
SCRIPTURES. 


That is to say, we can appreciate its necessity so far 
as the predictive prophecies are concerned, and the pro- 
founder revelations of the New Testament epistles, and 
the words of Jesus; but what about the historical books 
whose data might be gathered in a more usual way? Are 
we to suppose that they were inspired as truly as other 
parts of the Bible? 

1. Our answer is first, that “all scripture is given by 
inspiration of God.” That is what the Bible itself says, 
and “all scripture” includes the historical books as well as 
the others. 

The data of the historical books indeed, might have 
been obtained from natural sources, but what about the 
guidance of the writers in the selection and narration of 
those data? 

The record of the creation, the fall and the deluge as 
given in Genesis compared with that record unearthed by 
the excavations in Bible lands ought to satisfy us on this 
point. The puerility and grotesqueness of the latter in 
many cases, bear sad evidence of the human and sinful 
mould through which they ran, and show the need of a 
higher and holier power than man to lead him out of the 
labyrinth of error into the open space of truth. 

2. Furthermore, the historical books are in some re- 
spects the most important in the Bible, since they afford 
the basis for all its doctrinal teaching. They are the foun- 
dation of the superstructure of revealed truth. The doc- 
trine of sin is dependent on the history of the fall; the doc- 


ce RE EERE A RR A A RR NR RE A EE EE 


— 93 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


trine of the atonement is elucidated by the Levitical offer- 
ings; the priesthood of Christ is only understood by that of 
Aaron and his sons. 

Paul emphasizes this in 1 Cor. 10:6-11, where, refer- 
ring to the Old Testament history of Israel, he says, “Now 


all these things happened unto them for ensamples; and . 


they are written for our admonition upon whom the ends 
of the age are come.” 
Thus without going further, we may say with a dis- 


tinguished American theologian, that “it is impossible to- 


secure the religious infallibility of the Bible, if we exclude 
Bible history from the sphere of its inspiration.” 

Fourth. But the extensive conception of inspiration 
raises a fourth inquiry AS TO ITS INTENSIVE CHARACTER. 


In other words, there are some who concede the ` 


plenary inscription of the Bible and the whole Bible for 
that matter, the historical books as well as the other books, 
but they limit it nevertheless to the thought rather than the 
words which express the thought, to the substance but not 
the form. 3 

To this we take exception. As the late A. J. Gordon 
once said, “to deny that the Holy Spirit speaks in Scripture 
is an intelligible proposition; but to admit that He speaks, 


is it not impossible to know what He says except as we ` 


have His words?” 

That is to say, men think in words, and the more im- 
portant the thought the greater the care to find words in 
which to express the thought. The two are inseparable— 
the thought and the word—as much so “as the sum and its 
figures, or a tune and its notes,” or the sun and its light. 

But to dismiss the philosophy of the matter and look 
at the facts;

we OF tans 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


1. There certainly were some occasions when the 
very words were given of God to the human agents. Balaam 
is an instance, Saul is another, and Caiaphas another 
(Numbers 22:38; I Sam. 10:10; John 11: 49-52). When 
the last named said of Christ, “it were expedient that one 
man should die for the people,” John expressly adds, “This 
spake he not of himself, but he prophesied.” 

And to this agrees the circumstance of the day of 
Pentecost when “men began to speak with other tongues as 
the Spirit gave them utterance.” 

2. The Bible plainly teaches that inspiration extends 
to its words. 

God said He would be with Moses’ mouth and teach 
him what he should say (Exod. 4:10-12). 

We have before quoted David to the same effect, that 
God’s word was in his tongue (II Sam. 23:1, 2). 

Jeremiah testified that the Lord said unto him, “Be- 
hold I have put my word in thy mouth” (1:6-9). 

Peter said “No prophecy ever came by the will of 
man,” which seems to preclude the independent operation 
of the writer’s thought, and to focus the fact of inspiration 
on his words written. 

In the New Testament Paul very particularly distin- 
guishes between the “things” or the “thoughts” which God 
gave him and the words in which he expressed them, claim- 
ing inspiration for both (I Cor. 2:12, 13). 

In Gal. 3:16 he employs not merely a single word, but 
a single letter of a word as a basis of an argument for a 
great doctrine, saying: “Now to Abraham and his seed 
were the promises made. He saith not, ‘And to seeds, as 
of many; but as on one, And to thy seed, which is Christ.’ ” 

The author of the Epistle to the Hebrews bases a sim- 


— 95 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


ilar argument on the word “all” in Chapter 2:8; on the 
word “one” in 2:11; and on the phrase, “yet once more” 
in 12:26, 27. In the last instance he is quoting the prophet 
Haggai in whom God says, “Yet once more, I shake not 
the earth only, but also the heaven ;” on which he comments 
by saying: “And this ‘yet once more,’ signifieth” so and so. 

3. But perhaps the most unique argument for the in- 
spiration of the very words of Scripture is the relation 
which Jesus Christ bears to them. 

He tells us that He Himself was inspired as to His 
words, and He uses the Scripture as though those who 
wrote them were inspired as to their words. 

As to Himself He says: “The Father gave me a com- 
mandment what I should say and what I should speak” 
(John 12:49). As to the other Scriptures, we find Him 
overcoming the tempter in the wilderness by three quota- 
tions from Deuteronomy, without note or comment except, 
“It is written.” As Adolph Monod says: “I know of 
nothing in the whole history of humanity, nor even in the 
field of divine revelation that proves more clearly than this 
the inspiration of the Scriptures. Jesus Christ, the Lord of 
heaven and earth, calling to His aid in that solemn moment 
Moses His servant! How can we explain that spiritual 
mystery, that wonderful reversing of the order of things, 


if, for Jesus, the words of Moses were not the very words. 


of God?” 

IV. Time will not permit the further pursuit of the 
subject. 

I admit that there are difficulties in the way of accept- 
ing all that has been said. 

Supposed discrepancies in the text will be quoted; sup- 
posed contradictions between the teachings of the Bible

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


and the facts of science; variety in style among the dif- 
ferent writers; various readings of the manuscripts extant 
and other things. 


But two or three general replies may be made. 


In the first place, there is no difficulty that can be 
named today, that has not been known, examined and 
amply met ages ago. Moreover with the lapse of time these 
difficulties have not increased but lessened. This is notably 
so as to the supposed contradictions of science and discrep- 
ancies of the text. 

I may say also without, I trust, unduly speaking of 
myself, that I have personally and carefully examined 
every difficulty that has been named, and am satisfied that 
all of them are slight in comparison with the difficulties 
that must present themselves to any other view of the 
Bible, or of its inspiration, whatever. 

Therefore I covet the privilege of concluding this ad- 
dress, as I concluded another on the same subject some 
years ago, with a concrete testimony of the General Assem- 
bly of the Presbyterian Church of America. 

This assembly as you know, represents and contains in 
its membership some of the strongest intellects, the most 
cultivated as well as the most devout Christian men, clerical 
and lay, in any church and in any country on the earth. 
As I am not a Presbyterian I can say this with the greater 
freedom. 

-At its august gathering in the city of Washington, 
D. C., not so many years ago, it put forth this declaration 
which it has never altered nor abridged; and which de- 
serves to be inscribed in letters of gold in the enduring 
stone of every church edifice in the world, or flashed forth 


ae ee 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


in electric fire from every tower, or steeple, or capitol, or 
arch, namely: 

“The Bible as we now have it, in its various transla- 
tions and revisions, when freed from all errors and mis- 
takes of translators, copyists and printers, is the very Word 
of God, and consequently wholly without error!” Upon 
that declaration I am willing to take my stand and to stake 
thy life forever.

THE WITNESS OF HISTORY TO THE INSPIRA- 
TION OF THE WORD. 


W. H. Grirrira Tuomas, D.D. 


Formerly Principal of Wycliffe Hall, Oxford, Author of Grace 
and Power, Christianity is Christ, and The Apostle Peter. 


It is a familiar statement, and as true as it is familiar, 
that all the religions in the world have their sacred books. 
It would seem as if a religion were impossible without a 
book. Tradition is uncertain and truth cannot be handed 
down by word of mouth to successive generations without 
becoming involved in error. For this reason the old saying 
is both true and pertinent, littera scripta manet, “the written 
word abides.” 

The Bible has occupied a special place through all 
the Christian centuries, and this constitutes our present 
topic, “The Witness of History to the Divine Character of 
Scripture.” It will only be necessary to appeal to facts, to 
those matters which are capable of verification by all. 

I. The most important evidence is that which is af- 
forded by the Christian church. Starting from today we 
can see what this means in all the ages of Christianity. 
The Bible is found everywhere today in connection with 
Christian life and work. It is used for preaching and 
teaching, in connection with commentaries, books and arti- 
cles, and as the court of appeal for the various aspects of 
Christian truth. 

When we work backward we can see the same thing 
during the four centuries or more in which we have had 

Helpful books by the above author listed on pages 446-449, 


— 99 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


a printed Bible. No one can question the unique place 
occupied by the Bible throughout this period. Then behind 
the printed Word there are various versions of the Middle 
Ages, especially that of Wycliffe. The authority of the 
Scripture was equally evident then. From this time we 
can work back to the age of the manuscripts in the fourth 
century. Of these, which are very numerous, there are 
three very important ones. There is Codex Vaticanus in 
Rome; Codex Sinaiticus in Petrograd and Codex Alex- 
andrinus in London. Scarcely less important is the Freer 
manuscript now in America. From the same century 
come no fewer than ten catalogs of New Testament books, 
six of these being identical with ours and the other four, 
while omitting some of ours, do not include any books 
not found in the present New Testament. 

Further back still, there are quotations by various 
writers representing both the Eastern and Western 
branches of the church, quotations that afford clear testi- 
mony to belief in the divine authority of Scripture. Then 
in the second century there are versions like the Latin 
and Syriac and this brings us to within fifty years from 
the time of the Apostle John. In the first century there is 
the evidence of the well-known and representative Jew, 
Josephus, whose testimony clearly proves the identity of 
the Old Testament of his day (our Lord’s time) with that 
of ours. 

These are a few of the important points made in that 
old but (in this respect) still valuable book, Paley’s “Evi- 
dences of Christianity.” And it shows that all through 
the centuries the church has regarded the Bible as an 
unique book, as the record of divine revelation and there- 
fore the voice of God for human life. There is scarcely 


100 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


anything more impressive than the unanimity of belief in 
divine character of the Scripture in the early centuries. 
Westcott in his Introduction to the “Study of the Gospels” 
points out how, amid varieties of attitudes, influences, 
temperaments and places, with differences of education and 
character, the essential agreement of the Fathers of the 
first three centuries is all the more remarkable. There 
was “one great idea of inspiration in the scattered societies 
of ancient Christendom” (p. 417). 

II. Another witness is that of Heresy, for every 
erroneous idea asserting itself to be Christian claimed 
to be based on Scripture. In the second century Gnosticism 
appealed in this way, and one heretic, Marcion, drew up 
his own list of New Testament books, rejecting everything 
that did not agree with his own views. The same testimony 
is seen in the Sabellian heresy of the third century and the 
Arian of the fourth. So it has been ever since, errors of 
various kinds, ancient and modern, claiming their war- 
rant from Scripture and thereby testifying to the unique- 
ness of the Bible in Christianity. Swedenborgianism, 
Christian Science, Russellism, Theosophy, Spiritualism and 
the like, all call attention to the Bible and attempt to 
justify their positions from the Word of God. These things 
clearly indicate what the Bible is in the eyes of those who 
wish to prove that their views are in harmony with Chris- 
tianity. 

But at this point comes the inquiry as to wherein lies 
the authority of the Bible. Some say the Bible was given 
by the church and that on this account the authority con- 
sists in the volume as handed down through the ages. But 
another question at once emerges: When did the church 
do this, and what church did it? History will be sought 


— 101 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


in vain for any action of a church council settling the 
Bible. When, for the first time, in the fourth century, the 
church as a whole dealt with this subject, it was not to 
settle by decision what was to be the New Testament, but 
simply to bear witness to what beliefs already existed in 
the various churches regarding these books. The separate 
books had their own authority from the moment they 
were written and issued by properly qualified men, and 
the church in the fourth century in its corporate capacity 
testified to the way in which these books had long ago 
received authority from Christian people. The reason why 
these writings were regarded as authoritative was the 
belief that they contained divine revelation. Thus when 
Jeremiah wrote the contents of chapter 36, the truth was 
at once recognized as authoritative because of the prophetic 
authority behind it. And so also when Paul wrote Romans 
it was immediately authoritative and would have continued 
to be so, even though it had never been included in a 
volume. The inclusion into a volume did not give authority 
to what did not before possess it, but merely recognized 
the existence of that authority. The gathering of all the 
separate books is called canonization and as some one has 
truly said: “Canonization created a book, not a revelation.” 
The collection into a volume was inevitable, especially in 
view of the example of the Old Testament, but it must 
never be forgotten that the gathering together into one 
volume did not for the first time constitute the books au- 
thoritative. The heart of this whole question has been 
well put in words that deserve special emphasis and careful 
consideration: “the New Testament is not an authorized 
collection of books, but a collection of authorized books.” 
The authority lies in the books, not in the collection. For 


— 102 — 


2 : 
GOD HATH:SPOKEN 
—_—_—$—$—$<———— 
this reason when heretics collected their books, the church 
naturally bore testimony to what it believed to be the 
inspired and authoritative Scripture. 

III. There is the witness of other books. It is well 
known that a number of other books “hovered” on the 
verge of the canon of the Old and New Testaments for 
some time, but were finally rejected. Thus the books of 
the Apocrypha, though closely associated with the Old 
Testament, were never recognized by the Jews as part of 
their Bible, and the fact that they are now included in the 
Scripture by the Church of Rome was largely due to the 
ignorance of Hebrew on the part of Augustine. Men like 
Origen and Jerome made no mistake, and always differen- 
tiated between the Old Testament and the Apocrypha. So 
also in regard to the New Testament, certain books like 
the Epistle of Barnabas were set aside because of the 
proofs that they did not come from apostolic sources and 
were therefore lacking divine authority and inspiration. 
The bearing of this on our present subject can be seen 
from some words of that great scholar, Professor Gwatkin, 
of Cambridge, England: “There is no more striking con- 
trast in the whole range of literature than that between the 
creative energy of the Apostolic writers and the imitative 
poverty of the sub-apostolic. The difference of the canon- 
ical and uncanonical, so studiously ignored by some of the 
literary critics, is not a fiction of some church authority 
but a fact which no serious reader can fail to notice. * * * 
We miss the spiritual depth and intellectual force and 
clearness of the New Testament.” (Early Church His- 
tory, Vol. 1, 98, 99.) 

This contrast shows what the early church and indeed 
what the church of all ages has felt as to the uniqueness 


— 103 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


of the books contained in our Bible. As another profound 
scholar, Dr. George Salmon, Provost of Trinity College, 
Dublin, remarked: “The Scriptures by their own weight 
* * * crushed all rivals out of existence.” 

IV. The evidence of opponents of Christianity is 
another point of importance. Their attacks have invariably 
been centered on the Bible as the citadel of the faith, for 
they evidently realised that the destruction of holy Scrip- 
ture would involve the destruction of the Christian religion. 
Thus in the second century, Celsus attacked the truth of 
the New Testament, in the third century the Roman im- 
perial authorities significantly ordered all sacred books to 
be given up for destruction, and in the fourth century 
Porphyry concentrated his opposition on the book of 
Daniel because of its predictive and therefore supernatural 
element. And so it has been ever since; men have attacked 
the Bible because of its place and power in Christianity and 
this fact is one of the striking testimonies to it as a book 
of divine authority and inspiration. 

V. The explorations in Eastern lands can also be 
brought forward in support of the uniqueness of the Bible. 
For over 60 years there have been wonderful discoveries 
in Babylonia, Egypt, Palestine and Asia Minor, and these 
have shed singular light on the contents both of the Old and 
also of the New Testament. Indeed, it is a remarkable 
and profoundly impressive fact that not one of these dis- 
coveries has proved the inaccuracy of the Bible, but on 
the contrary, many of them have confirmed the truth of 
the Scripture in regard to facts of history and customs 
of ordinary life. Very much in support of this contention 
can be derived from “The Deciding Voice of the Monu- 
ments”, by Dr. M. G. Kyle. 


— 104— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


VI. A comparison of the Bible with the sacred books 
of other religions is another proof of what history says 
of the Christian Scriptures. During recent years much 
attention has been given to the comparative study of re- 
ligion, and this method is capable of producing very valu- 
able results, because the more Christianity is studied in 
comparison with other faiths the more it will be seen to 
be, as Dr. Joseph Parker once said, not a comparative, but 
the superlative religion. We have only to look at other 
books making the same claim as the Bible to see the vast 
difference. Max Muller translated some of the sacred 
books of the East, but was compelled to omit great sec- 
tions because of the absolute impossibility of reproducing 
these books verbatim into English. When people now read 
these translations and observe their interest and beauty, 
their truth and purity, they forget, if indeed they know, 
that sections of very different character have been omitted. 
But the fact of an expurgated edition tells its own tale. 

To the same effect is the evidence of another great 
authority, the Oxford scholar, Sir Monier Monier-Wil- 
liams, whom I heard several years ago say that the funda- 
mental difference between all those books of the East and 
the Bible is that the former taught salvation by works and 
merit, while the latter teaches it as coming by grace through 
faith. 

And the results of these books on human life, as 
compared with what the Bible has done and is doing, is 
a strong and striking proof of the divine power of the 
Word of God. If you want to see what other books can 
do, look at the countries where they have been influential, 
If you want to know what Hinduism can do, look at India. 
If you want to know what Buddhism can do, look at 


— 105 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


India and Japan. If you want to know what Confucianism 
can do, look at China. If you want to know what the 
Koran can do, look at Turkey and Egypt. We have no 
fear about the comparison. We feel perfectly certain that 
every comparison will show the infinite superiority of the 
Bible over all these books of the various religious systems. 

VII. Last of all, and crowning everything else is the 
circulation of the Bible. Bible societies in various countries 
are circulating the Scripture in whole or in part in over 600 
languages and dialects. There is no book in the world as 
widely translated and distributed. Not only so, but there 
is no book so capable of proper translation. Many books 
cannot be adequately rendered from the original language 
into another, for the “flavor” becomes lost. We have only 
to think how much of Shakespeare would be left if one 
of his soliloquies were rendered (say) in Chinese. But 
the Bible loses very little by translation and its message 
of salvation finds expression, at once adequate and satis- 
fying, in every tongue into which it is rendered. 

And the effects of all this circulation of the Bible 
are apparent everywhere. The war alone has done much to 
prove the power of Scripture over human life. It is not 
too much to say that the Bible House in New York and the 
Bible House in London are among the finest evidences 
of Christianity. 

Surveying this summary of history in relation to the 
Bible, three things stand out. 

First. The Singularity of the Scripture. It is unique. 
There is no other book in the world of which these things 
can be said. There is no real alternative: either this book 
is divine or else there is no other. 


Second. The Sufficiency of the Scripture. In the Bible 


PEIEE BERTE ee 


SSN) 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


SS 
————————— 


_ we have a little book, easily obtained and quickly read. 
It has transformed individuals, uplifted communities, per- 
meated literature, influenced philosophy, faced empires, 
dominated civilization and demands attention wherever 
it goes. ; 

Third.. The Supremacy of the Scripture. History 
shows that the Christian revelation and the Bible go to- 
gether and that Christ never reveals Himself fully apart 
from the Scripture. The Bible is supreme over human 
reason because reason is only a channel, not a source of 
truth; an opportunity for testing revelation, not the creator 
of it. And Scripture is supreme over the church, because 
the church was created by the Word of God, first spoken 
and afterwards written. We do not set aside either reason 
or church, but simply say that for the purest, clearest, 
fullest revelation of Christ as our supreme authority we 
have to turn to the Bible. It is our final court of appeal, 
and contains the last and supreme word on everything 
connected with the spiritual life. We cannot imagine any 
book giving us the last word on astronomy, geology, biology 
or any of the other sciences, because there is always some- 
thing new to be discovered and stated. But here is a 
book nearly 2,000 years old which contains the best and 
last teaching about God, sin, redemption and eternity. And 
for this reason some of us rejoice in these words of one 
of the Articles of the Episcopal Church: “Holy Scripture 
containeth all things necessary to salvation, and so that 
whatsoever is not read therein or may be proved thereby is 
not to be required of any man that it should be believed 
as an article of the faith or be thought requisite or neces- 
sary to salvation.” This is what David Livingstone meant 
when speaking to Stanley he said, “Stanley, I read the 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Bible through four times while I was waiting at Manyuema. 
All that I am I owe to Christ Jesus, revealed to me in His 
divine Book. O, Stanley, Stanley! here is the source of 
strength and transforming power.” 

Among other testimonies to the same fact, the words 
of President Grant may be given: “Hold fast to the Bible 
as the sheet-anchor to your liberties. Write its precepts in 
your heart and practice them in your lives. To the influ- 
ence of this Book we are indebted for all the progress 
made in true civilization, and to this must we look as our 
guide in the future”. 

In the Louvre in Paris, there is a picture of the 
great Council of Constantinople, 381, which dealt with 
the heresy of Macedonius in regard to the deity of the 
Holy Spirit. The bishops present are depicted as seated 
in a semi-circle and in the centre is a throne, with a roll 
on it indicating Scripture as the final appeal. And at 
another Council, that of Chalcedon, 451, the Gospels were 
placed similarly in the centre for the same purpose. 

And so we say to everyone: examine these facts of 
history and experience, sift them to the very last possi- 
bility, and without a doubt the conclusion will be: “Thy 
word is true from the beginning;”’ “Thy word is very 
pure;” “Thy word is a lamp unto my feet;” “the grass 
withereth, the flower fadeth, but the word of our God 
shall stand forever.”

THE WITNESS OF HUMAN EXPERIENCE TO 
THE INSPIRATION OF THE WORD 


PAW- PRILEOT 


Pastor of the Gospel Tabernacle of Hamilton, Ontario, a church 
which he founded and has rebuilt four times, doubling its 
capacity with each rebuilding. 


In undertaking to address you on this subject, I would 
like to say that I am not attempting to defend the Holy 
Bible. I have never felt that to be my mission, nor for 
that matter have I ever felt that the Word of God needed 
defending by any man. For over 25 years I have proven 
that if the gospel is preached, it will manifest its divine 
power and defend itself. i 

I sometimes think that we take the higher critic by 
far too seriously and this gives them a sense of importance 
that is altogether out of proportion with their influence. 

It was Mr. Spurgeon I think who used to tell the story 
of the two little boys, one a very bad little boy, and the 
other a very good little boy. The bad little boy went out 
to throw mud at the moon and the good little boy took 
a basin of water and went out to wash it off, but what do 
you suppose that the good old moon was doing all the time 
the boys were throwing mud and splashing water? Why 
it was just shining all the time—and so the Word of God 
is unaffected by every attempt to annihilate or shorten it. 
It never changes and simply refuses to appear in any other 
form for He has said, “One jot or one tittle shall in no 
wise pass * * * till all be fulfilled.” (Matt. 5:18.)

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


I have a feeling that those who believe in the Word 
of God and have experienced its power in their lives and 
ministry will not be greatly disturbed over the “shorter 
Bible” and the “Christless Church.” As a matter of fact, 
a number of churches have been Christless for a long time, 
and I should think a shorter Bible would be to such a 
church much more convenient than the real living Word 
of God. 

Probably never since the church began have the 
Scriptures been so fiercely, so subtly and so cleverly assailed 
as at the prerent time, and yet, never were they so widely 
circulated, so highly prized and so earnestly studied as at 
this very hour. 

Recently the British and Foreign Bible Society reported 
that since its foundation in 1804, they have circulated the 
surprising total of 3,000,000 volumes, each being a Bible 
or a Testament or a complete Book of the Scripture, and 
during the war years, 1914 to 1918, that same Society 
printed versions of some parts of the Bible in 34 fresh 
languages and dialects. Now, add to this the record of the 
American Bible Society, the National Bible Society of Scot- 
land, and the Hibernian Bible Society, the Scripture Gift 
Mission and the Pocket Testament League, and you will 
see that the Old Book not only lives, but it is thriving on 
opposition. 

Why, you might just as well take a watering-can and 
try to quench the sun shining in yonder heaven, or under- 
take to remove the earth to the Planet of Mars with a 
teaspoon as to try to destroy or shorten the Bible. “Heaven 
and earth shall pass away, but My Words shall never 
pass away, saith the Lord.”

Last eve I paused besides the blacksmith’s door, 
And heard the anvil ring the vesper chime; 


And looking in I saw upon the floor 
Old hammers, worn with beating years of time. 


“How many anvils have you had,” said I, 
“To wear and batter all these hammers so?” 
“Just one,” said he, and then with twinkling eye, 
“The anvil wears the hammers out, you know.” 


And so, I thought, the anvil of God’s Word, 
In ages, skeptic blows have beat upon, 

Yet, though the noise of falling blows was heard, 
The anvil is unharmed, the hammers gone. 


It is significant that the Coat of Arms of the French 
Bible Society is a Bible in the form of an anvil with many 
broken hammers around it and the motto is: “The Ham- 
mers Break, the Anvil abides forever.” 


The previous speaker has given you historical evidence 
as to the inspiration of the Scriptures, and I am requested 
to submit some human experience. The historical and the 
experimental go together, as Dr. Griffith Thomas has said 
in one of his books: “You cannot separate the historical 
Jesus from the Christ of experience without losing both,” 
but I think in the strict order of precedence there is a 
sense in which Christian experience comes before Christian 
Doctrine. The experience prepares the heart for the doc- 
trine and the doctrine gives expression to the experience. 
Jesus, in speaking of the Holy Spirit, who is the author 
of the Scriptures, says: “I will send him unto you * * *,” 
“Howbeit, when he, the Spirit of truth is come, he will 
guide you into all truth” (John 16:7, 13). Do you think 
that Saul of Tarsus could have possibly grasped the plan 
of God for the church and the world in the way that he 
did had he not first have had that wonderful experience on 


—111— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the way to Damascus when his eyes were opened and he 
saw Jesus as the annointed Son of God and was filled with 
the Holy Ghost? Listen to his own testimony: “It is 
written, ‘Eye hath not seen nor ear heard, neither have 
entered into the heart of man the things which God hath 
prepared for them that love Him, but God hath revealed 
them unto us by His Spirit, for the Spirit searcheth all 
things, yea, the deep things of God.’ But the natural man 
receiveth not the things of the Spirit of God, for they are 
foolishness unto him, neither can he know them, because 
they are spiritually discerned.” (1 Cor. 2:9, 10, 14.) 
Which is another way of saying that the qualifications for 
receiving the revelation of God call for more than a big 
brain or a great education. There must be the “new heart” 
and the “right spirit.” 


Take the aspostles of our Lord Jesus Christ: How 
perplexed they were, how befogged their brains, until that 
day when the Holy Spirit came and possessed them. Why, 
in a few minutes after that experience, Peter had a com- 
prehensive grasp of the plan and purpose of God through 
Jesus Christ. 


We sometimes talk about the miracles, the great won- 
ders that were wrought through the ministry of these fisher- 
men, but to me these are not nearly so marvelous as the 
great change the Holy Spirit made in the men themselves. 

A few years ago I heard a scholar of some note say 
that when he was a student in one of the Western Uni- 
versities, there came one day to the school an old preacher, 
who was asked to address the graduating class, and he 
greatly insulted the young men by telling them that while 
they had learned a great deal during those years of hard 


— 112— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


study, that when the Holy Spirit got possession of their 
lives He would teach them more about God and divine 
things in one hour than they had learned in that University 
in seven years. “This made me desperately angry,” said 
the minister, “but I couldn’t get away from that statement, 
and inside of a week it had driven me to my knees, and 
sure enough when He came and possessed me I knew 
more about God and His will in ten minutes than I had 
even known before in all my life.” 


Now I am convinced that a great many of our brethren 
who are opposing the great fundamentals have never been 
born again. Indeed, many of them ridicule the thought 
of any such change, hence they cannot grasp these spiritual 
things nor can they “see the Kingdom of God.” 


Now we recognize that this Book comes to us with 
enormous claims, both as to its divine origin and its right 
to control our lives, and we believe that every claim it 
makes is fully sustained and we bow to its absolute author- 
ity, and are “ready to give an answer to those who ask a 
reason for the hope that is in them.” “How would you 
answer a man who does not believe in the miracles of the 
Bible?” asked a preacher the other day. “Why I would 
have a few fresh miracles of my own,” I replied. The 
man who really believes God’s Word and preaches it is 
sure to have some first hand evidence of the power and 
inspiration of the Bible. “That which we have seen with 
our eyes, which we have looked upon and our hands have 
handled of the Word of Life, for the life was manifested 
and we have seen it and bear witness.” (I John 1:1, 2.) 

That is the testimony of fact, and facts are stubborn 
things. No amount of argument or reasoning can destroy 


— 113 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN | 
= 
them. A personal experience of “the washing of regenera- 
tion and the renewing of the Holy Ghost” is one of the 
things that cannot be shaken in these days of apostasy and 
perplexity. 

I tell you people, it is a great thing when the Scriptures 
are assailed, to be able to step back with both feet on your 
own experience and face men and demons with your testi- 
mony: “I know these things are true, for God has verified 
them in my own life.” 


John wrote his gospel that we might believe and have 
eternal life, and he wrote his epistles that men who believe 
might know that they have that life. In the gospel we find 
the expression: “Life,” or “eternal life” eighty different 
times, and the word “believe” over forty times. In the 
epistle, there are five chapters, and they all treat the great 
subject of knowing that we are children of God. The word 
“know” or “knowledge” occurs in that little epistle over 
fifty times. He concludes his gospel by saying “these are 
written that ye might believe that Jesus is the Christ, the 
Son of God; and that believing, ye might have life through 
His name.” (John 20:31.) The corresponding passage 
is found in 1 John 5:13: “These things have I written 
unto you that believe on the name of the Son of God, 
that ye may know that ye have eternal life.’ The one 
thing that you can be absolutely sure of is eternal life. 


I know that I am born of God and belong to Him 
better than I know that I am the son of the precious little 
woman that I call “mother.” A little time ago I was in the 
home of a brother minister when a young man came in 
and was introduced as “the son of a prominent man in 
that town.” After he went out the minister said to me 


EE Y 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


“You know, he is not their own son, although he thinks so. 
When he was an infant of only a few days, he was left 
on the door step in a basket, and he has never found it 
out.” Now I got serious right away, and I began to ask 
myself the question whether I was really a Philpot or not— 
I could see that there was a possibility of being left on the 
door step when I was a youngster, too—but there need be 
no uncertainty about the second birth. If we are born of 
God, we know it. We were there when it took place and 
gave the consent of our own will to the transaction—and 
we say with John, “We know that we have passed from 
death unto life.” Paul says, “If any man”—now note that 
word, not only the Jerry McAuleys, Sam Hadleys or Mel 
Trotters, but “any man, be in Christ, he is a new creature. 
Old things are passed away; behold all things are become 
new.” Now that is an experience—something that you 
know and something that other folks can see and appreciate. 
I know a man who thirty years ago was a vile blasphemer. 
His language was foul and disgusting; because of the habit 
he loathed himself, tried desperately hard to break it, but 
failed again and again, and one night he accepted Christ 
as a Savior, was given power to become a son of God 
and power to live as a son of God—and from that day 
there has never come a suggestion of an oath to his lips. 
We say this to the praise of the glory of God’s grace “ye 
shall receive power, after that the Holy Ghost is come 
upon you, and ye shall be witnesses unto Me.” (Acts. 1:8). 


‘When Peter and John were brought before the Jewish 
council for teaching and preaching “through Jesus, the 
resurrection from the dead” there stood with them a man 
who had been made whole through the power of that 


a s, 


— 115 — 


pa 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


wonderful Name, “and beholding the man which was 
standing with them, they could say nothing against it.” 
That was the testimony of fact. Peter used that restored 
cripple to illustrate Christ’s power to save men from their 
sins. In The Acts 3, verse 16, we read: “And His Name, 
through faith in His Name, hath made this man strong, 
whom ye see and know—yea, the faith which is by Him 
hath given him this perfect soundness in the presence of 
you all.” Then in verse 26 he says, “Unto you first God, 
having raised up his son Jesus, sent him to bless you in 
turning away every one of you from his iniquities.” Then 
in chapter 4:9, 10, 12, “If we this day be examined of the 
good deed done to the impotent man, by what means he 
is made whole, be it known unto you all and to all the 
people of Israel that by the name of Jesus Christ of 
Nazareth whom ye crucified and whom God raised from 
the dead, even by him doth this man stand here before you 
whole—neither is there salvation in any other, for there 
is none other name under heaven given among men whereby 
we must be saved.” And those Jewish leaders, prejudiced 
as they were, took knowledge of these fishermen that they 
had been with Jesus. In that man, made whole, whom they 
all knew, the preachers had irrefutable evidence of the 
resurrection of the Lord Jesus. 


When Abraham’s servant went forth to seek the bride 
for Isaac, he not only had a great story to tell about his 
Master’s riches, he also carried some of the treasures with 
him, which he produced as evidence, and so we who are 
out seeking the Bride for Christ, should have some proof 
of our Master’s power and grace. We should carry a few 
samples with us.

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Now I have been requested to give you a little personal 
testimony as to the power of the Holy Scripture in my 
life and ministry. About 30 years ago I had the privilege 
of hearing the late D. L. Moody in one of his great meet- 
ings. When I first saw him I thought that he was the 
most unattractive preacher I had ever met—a poor voice, 
extremely plain and simple. He had no great organization. 
If I remember correctly he was without his singer at that 
time. And yet the great building was packed at every 
meeting, with a staff of policemen at the doors to regulate 
the crowd, and the power of God was most mightily mani- 
fest in the salvation of hundreds of precious souls. 

A little later Dr. A. T. Pierson and Dr. A. J. Gordon 
came to the city of Toronto for a week’s Bible Conference. 
Now these men were giants in their intellect. They were 
striking in personality, and charming speakers, and yet, the 
same power was manifested with the same results, but I 
was made to feel that it was not because of these men’s 
imperial abilities or Moody’s simplicity, but God was honor- 
ing the ministry of these men because they were honoring 
His Word—for, oh, how they magnified the Scriptures, 
how they exalted the name of Jesus, as living Savior 
and coming King. 

At that time I was a disappointed and discouraged 
preacher, on the eve of giving up my work, but in those 
meetings it was revealed to me that my ministry had failed 
very laregly because I did not know my Bible, and was 
not preaching the gospel. I was preaching a little bit of 
gospel with a whole lot of something else, and God was 
not honoring my ministry. I went to Him, confessing my 
sins, and seeking the face of Jesus Christ, and it was 


—117— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


then that I made a covenant with Him that in the future 
I would study His Word, I would make it the Man of 
my Counsel, and would preach it, no matter what it might 
cost, no matter how it might jostle my preconceived notions 
and ideas. And, oh, how I thank God today for that new 
vision—what it has meant to me I could not attempt to 
tell you, but I will say with one of our great English poets: 

“I know the Bible is inspired, 

Because it inspires me.” 
Now just after that experience, I began my present pastor- 
ate, and made up my mind to test the power of the gospel 
in the city to which I had been called, so I began to sow 
the seed of the Word, and also to reap and gather the 
grain. For you know He has said, “that if we sow this seed, 


there must be fruit.” It is true that some falls on stony ` 


ground and some is caught away by birds of prey, and 
some will be choked with thorns, but some, bless God— 
some falls on good ground. It is not all wasted or lost. 
Some will find an honest heart and produce “thirty, sixty 
or a hundred fold.” 

When I began that work, it was but a mission, with 
about thirty-five members. The meeting place was an old 
saloon that had been transformed, seating about 150 people. 
During these twenty-four years we have changed a good 
many times, lengthening our cords and strengthening our 
stakes, till now we have a Sunday congregation of about 
1800, and a regular Friday night Bible class ranging from 
700 to 1000, and best of all, we have hardly known one 
week to pass in all that time without souls being saved. 
Every Lord’s Day we see men and women turning to 
Christ from sin. We have now some twelve missionaries 


: 
| 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


on foreign fields, all members of our own church and all 
supported by our own people, and we will send forth two 
new workers this year. Now this work has been brought 
about and is being sustained simply through the preaching 
of the gospel of Jesus Christ. We have never resorted to 
any kind of schemes to draw or interest the people. I 
believed at the beginning that the gospel was the “power 
of God unto salvation” and I have been proving it week 
after week during these years. 


It is generally conceded that the Protestant Church 
has been losing ground at an alarming rate for these last 
few years, and there is a feeling that something must be 
done if she is to be saved, and all sorts’ of remedies are 
being suggested and undertaken, but friends, I tell you 
frankly I haven’t the slightest faith in anything but the 
one thing that God has given us to do, and that is, preach 
the gospel. When the church is true to her calling and 
does that one thing, she will not only accomplish the pur- 
pose of God in the saving of men, but she will do more 
to solve the problems that are disturbing the world than 
she ever will by any other service, no matter how good 
and commendable these enterprises may be of themselves. 


Someone has said that there are two things that never 
change, the human heart and the gospel. The need of 
the human heart is just the same today as it was 2000 
years ago and the gospel was given to meet the need then. 
It is the only thing that can meet that need today. Why, 
bless you, I look over my congregation every Sunday and 
I see there men and women who in the past were bruised 
and broken by sin; some of them were sinners of the worst 
sort, but they have been changed—they are clothed and 


——— nn! 


—119— 


LS 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


in their right mind. The homes that they represent which 
were once little better than hells, have been transformed. 
The children are cared for, being brought up under Chris- 
tian influence, the gospel has made that change, and keeps 
right on doing that sort of work. Why should we look for 
_ something else to substitute this, God’s remedy, for the 
sorrows, sufferings and sins of the human family? 


A few years ago I spent a Sunday in a little town in 
the north part of Ontario. I preached in the afternoon 
in the public hall. It was thought by the Christian people 
that if the service was held in that place, possibly some 
would attend who never went to church. At the close of 
the message I made an appeal for decisions. We were 
standing singing the last hymn when a big man walked 
up the aisle and stood near the front. Being a stranger 
in the town I thought probably that this man was a Chris- 
tian, but I observed that the people were whispering to 
each other, and perceived that he might be a seeking soul. 
I stepped down by his side and asked him if he were a 
Christian, and he replied, “No, sir, but I want to be one 
right now.” I told the people what the man had said and 
there was great joy in that meeting and many tears were 
shed; for this was a truly bad man. At the very hour that 
he was there a number of his companions were behind the 
prison bars for a crime that resulted in the death of one 
of the townsfolk. Some few months after this had taken 
place I was invited back to that town for a special meeting 
one day. This brother wrote me a letter, insisting that 
I should come to his humble little home and have a cup 
of tea with the family. I gladly consented. On my ar- 
rival he met me at the train, and took me to their little 


— 120— 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


house on the outskirts of the town. He went across the 
field to milk a little Jersey cow which he had purchased 
recently, and I sat in the house, and his wife told me 
something of the change that had come to them since her 
husband became a Christian. She said, “Mr. Philpot, you 
know that Sunday when you preached in the hall? Well, 
my husband came home for supper about 6 o’clock and 
we were greatly surprised, for he hardly ever spent a Sun- 
day at home. It was a day for drinking and gambling, but 
he said nothing during the meal hour, after which he 
went out and we thought it was for the night, but to my 
surprise about nine o’clock he came in again. I was with 
the children in that room there” (and then she remarked 
“You know we only had these two rooms then. We have 
made this other addition to the house these last few weeks.) 
When he came in that evening I couldn’t understand what 
it alk meant,” and then she said, “He was so quiet out 
here in this room. At last I crept out of bed and looked 
through the door, and here sat my husband at the table, 
reading the Bible. I could not believe my eyes, I was 
spellbound by that sight, and I never moved, thinking it 
was all too good to be true, but finally he pushed back 
the chair and knelt down to pray. I couldn’t stand it any 
longer, I thought I would die for joy. I ran out and threw 
my arms around his neck and said, ‘Mort, if you’re going 
to pray, so am I. We'll walk this road together.”” And 
then after a while she looked in my face and said, “Mr. 
Philpot, I don’t know what it will be like in heaven, but 
the change surely cannot be much greater than that which 
has come to our house, and lives, since my husband brought 
Christ home that Sunday afternoon.”

Yes, this living Word! This is the gospel of power.

— 122 — 


GOD—HIS PERSONALITY, CHARACTER AND 
ATTRIBUTES 


A. B. WINCHESTER. 


Pastor Knox Presbyterian Church, of Toronto, Ont., and 
Bible Teacher. 


At the very outset, as we together pursue our study 
of this most vital doctrine of our holy faith, may our aim 
be not merely to gather data for an accurate, and as com- 
prehensive a statement of a doctrine as time limits will 
allow, but that through the precious Word the blessed 
Holy Spirit may disclose to our spirits more of the beauty 
and manifold glories of the Person of the Everlasting 
Father. As we meditate, let us preserve the consciousness 
ever alive, that we are even now, as always, in the Presence 
of Him before whom “All things are naked and open unto 
the eyes of Him with whom we have to do.” (Heb. 
4:13). How much of the hurtful and confusing “wisdom 
of man which is foolishness with God” had we been spared 
from, if all those who have thought and written and 
spoken on this theme had been mindful of His ineffable 
and searching Presence! 

Our Beloved “standeth behind our wall, he looketh 
forth at the windows showing himself through the lattice.” 
Song of Sol. 2:9. 

With hearts bowed in reverence and loving adoration, 
relying wholly on the Holy Spirit, may we unfeignedly 
adopt the inspired Word of the Psalmist: “Show me thy 
ways, O Jehovah; teach me thy paths. Lead me in thy 


— 123 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


truth, and teach me; for thou art the God of my salvation; 
for thee do I wait all the day.” (Ps. 25: 4-5.) 


“Shine Thou upon us, Lord, 
True light of men today 

And thro’ the written Word 
Thy very Self display ; 

That so from Hearts that burn 
With gazing on Thy face; 

The humble ones may learn 
The wonders of Thy grace.” 


We are not concerned in this Conference about 
“Philosophy and vain deceit”; nor are we interested in 
the reported origins of the world’s religions, yet it is of 
value to state some of the facts substantiated by history 
which prove that the “world by wisdom knew not God.” 
(1 Cor. 1:21.) It is also salutary and timely to recall the 
injunction in Jude 3: “Beloved, while I was giving all 
diligence to write unto you of our common Salvation, I 
was constrained to write unto you exhorting you to con- 
tend earnestly for the faith which was once for all deliv- 
ered unto the Saints.” By the “faith” is certainly meant 
the doctrines of the Gospel, the great principles of Chris- 
tian credence both in the Apostolical Church and among 
true believers today. That “faith” was given once for all, 
and supernaturally, to the church. And if it was neces- 
sary in Jude’s day “earnestly to contend,” ¢. e., a deter- 
mined defending and maintaining of the principles of the 
Gospel—much more is it necessary today. 

The “world by its wisdom” compassed much knowl- 
edge. The writings of famous men have come down to 
us through the centuries, attesting at once their princely 
intellects and prodigious labors. They explored not only 
those districts of knowledge having utilitarian ends—such 


— 124— 


er SESE 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


i 
S a 


subjects as related to the principles of agriculture, mathe- 
matics, the practical sciences, the laws of commerce and 
political economy and history—but they were also Titans 
in architecture, sculpture, painting, poetry, oratory, phi- 
losophy, and even religion. The works of some which 
have been preserved for us through the wasting influences 
of thousands of years, are still the admiration of the 
philosopher and the envy of the artist. 

What stars of first magnitude in the domain of phi- 
losophy were such as Thales, Herodotus, Plato, Aristotle, 
Socrates, Democratus, Hippocrates, Epicurus, and Zeno! 
And what incomparable orators and poets were such as 
Cicero and Demosthenes; Homer, Virgil, and Ovid! If 
the natural man could ever by his unaided reason have 
discovered the true God, surely these would have found 
Him. But Him they found not. 

It was not that they were without certain ideas of 
God. Nor that they did not prosecute the science of 
theology. Yet with all their intellectual greatness, pro- 
found researches, soaring imagination, and surpassing 
culture, they have but demonstrated that without the hum- 
ble acceptance of the revelation God has given of Himself, 
man can never attain to that knowledge of the true and 
living God of which our Lord speaks in John 17:3. 
What an indictment against such, and against all who 
oppose with “vain reasonings” God’s self-revelation through 
nature and Scripture and providence, is that awful word 
in the first chapter of Romans! 

It is there declared that even the heathen who never 
heard of Scripture were morally culpable, if they were 
ignorant of a personal righteous God with “whom they 
have to do.” God has through nature manifested that 


EY 
— 125 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


behind the phenomena of the material and providential 
worlds is a Personality of whom a humble heart may be 
as certain as a man is of his own consciousness and con- 
science. 

“Because that which is known (or knowable) of God 
is manifested in them. For the invisible things of Him 
since the creation of the world are clearly seen, being per- 
ceived through the things that are made, even His ever- 
lasting power and divinity, that they may be without ex- 
cuse.” (Rom. 1: 19-20.) 

There is not a word in Scripture to support that basic 
speculation of modern philosophy—the evolutionary hy- 
pothesis. The Bible shows nothing of an “ascent” of man 
from the atom to the molecule, the bioplast and thence 
through the varied grades of brute being up to the abo- 
riginal savage man; but it does unequivocally speak of 
the “descent” of man from the innocent, and at that stage, 
perfect creature, made in the image of his Creator, down 
through sin—his countenance fallen, his conscience vio- 
lated, his heart corrupted, the god-like image in which he 
was formed, defaced; expelled from Eden, and “because 
he did not like to retain the knowledge of God,” and could 
not endure the Presence of Him who was holy and just 
and good, he went forth from the Presence of Jehovah. 
“And God saw that the wickedness of man was great in 
the earth, and that every imagination of the thoughts of 
his heart was only evil continually.” (Gen. 6:5.) 

What a weariness and sadness it is for those who 
have the Word of God in their hands and in their hearts; 
and who in order to meet the difficulties of such as are 
led captives by Satan through foolish imaginations, have 
endeavored to follow the labyrinthian maze of bewildering 


— 126 — 


eR S 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 
(ee ee eee 


and contradictory speculations of great intellectuals who 
have undertaken the task of tracing the philosophy of 
religion back to its supposed source! For example, Au- 
guste Comte, with his limited fetichism theory, alleging 
that the phenomena of the material creation excited primi- 
tive man’s curiosity, his gratitude, his fear, or his hope, 
and so religion was begun. Or Pfleiderer, a speculative 
theist, in his “Philosophy of Religion” agrees with Reville 
in distinguishing between “the worship of animated nature 
(Naturism) which ‘he holds’ must certainly be placed 
first as the earliest in time; and the belief in spirits 
(Animism, Spiritism) developed out of the former as a 
subdivision of the latter, the worship of the spirit of an- 
cestors.” 

Or Herbert Spencer’s theory of the origin of religion 
in “Ghost-worship,” etc., etc. 

Enough of that; we have the inspired revelation, not 
of how savage man slowly evolved from the lowest forms 
of thought, the fetish, the nature power, the tribal god, 
the national god, up to the concept of a Supreme Being; 
but that man having a knowledge of God from creation, 
devoluted and degenerated through pride and unbelief, and 
is without excuse, “Because knowing God, they did not 
glorify Him as God, nor thank Him, but proved futile in 
their ways of thinking, and their unintelligent heart was 
darkened. Asserting themselves for wise they turned 
fools, and transmuted the glory of the immortal God in a 
semblance of the likeness of mortal man, and of things 
winged, quadruped, and reptile.’ (Bishop H. C. G. 
Moule’s translation of Rom. 1:21-22.) Oh, that the 
haughty eulogist of human nature, its dignity and achieve- 
ments, could but stop and ponder this affecting and hum- 


— 127 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


bling revelation. Thus did God “destroy the wisdom of 
the wise and brought to naught the discernment of the 
discerning.” (1 Cor.1:19.) That is the inspired account 
of the genesis and development of the philosophy of reli- 
gion. The remaining portion of that first chapter of 
Romans should be read alone after prayer and commu- 
nion with our Holy God. When men “exchanged the truth 
of God for a lie, and worshiped and served the creature 
rather than the Creator * * * even as they refused 
to have God in their moral knowledge, God gave them up 
to an abandoned mind.” (Verses 25, 28.) And this in 
a most illustrious age when civilization and philosophy, 
poetry, and the arts, flourished in a pre-eminent degree. 


So that the origin of philosophy grew out of man’s 
choosing not to retain the knowledge of God. The noble 
instincts of a creature made in God’s image, created for 
the truth, leaving the knowledge of God they turned to 
“vain reasonings,” and laid the foundations of the false 
religions by which our race has been deluded and cursed. 
They changed the glory of the incorruptible God into an 
image-likeness of corruptible man.” (V. 24.) How did 
they effect this awful change? They spent strength and 
time in “vain reasonings,” and unintelligent imaginings. 
“Imaginings” is just another form of “image-makings,” 
and the most learned unregenerated philosophers’ ideal 
images of the infinite God are as worthless and perverse 
as. are the Totem poles or stone images of the most de- 
graded heathen idolaters. The difference is only in degree. 


And their “progress” is retrograde; their “evolution” 
devolution; their “ascent,” a descent. 


“In the history of systems an inexorable logic rids 


— 128 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


them of their halfness and hesitancies, and drives them 
straight to their inevitable goal.” (Martineau.) 

Luthardt, with his penetrative and profound spiritual 
insight, says: 

“The way in which a man thinks of God and of the 
world, and their relation to one another, is decisive for 
the whole tendency of his thought, and even in the ques- 
tions of the purely natural life.” 

The Supreme Authority says: “As a man thinketh 
in his heart so is he.” According to a man’s thought of 
God, even so will the man himself be. Conversely, what 
a man IS complexions his thought of God. A man’s reli- 
gion conditions his ethical conceptions and moulds his con- 
duct and character. What a vivid illustration of this is 
given in that first chapter of Romans! The creed of the 
Hindu who worships at the shrine of the bloodthirsty god- 
dess Kali—(“ with her necklace of skulls, and the hands 
of slaughtered giants round her waist as a girdle. Her 
eyebrows and breast appear streaming with the blood of 
monsters she has slain and devoured. She is the goddess 
of death and destruction, and goats and other animals are 
sacrificed on her altars. Ancient Hindu books even en- 
joined human sacrifices to her. Her worship is character- 
ized by vile secret rites” )—inevitably pollutes and blunts his 
conscience, and his degraded conscience in turn reflexively 
and corruptingly affects his belief. 

Looking with horror into that awful abyss of degrad- 
ing idolatry, we shudderingly ask “how ever came God- 
imaged man to conceive of his holy Creator under a form 
of such repulsive and debasing unreality?” Simply by 
the inevitable course of “vain reasonings.” A dozen years 
ago a well-known philosopher, writing of “Our Anxious

Morality” says: “For all that touches upon our moral 
life we still have the choice of our illusions.” * * * “In 
any case nothing is decided. We are still in suspense.” 

O the sadness of it, that that word aptly represents 
all those—and they are legion—who have sought God and 
rest, apart from God’s own appointed way! My brethren, 
is it not a spectacle to make the most apathetic Christian 
weep to see a British Lord, three Sir Knights, and a Doc- 
tor of Science from platforms in England and Scotland at 
this present time, trying to ease the pain of aching hearts 
in that land which has given ungrudingly of her hero sons 
for the defense of altar and hearth, by proclaiming that 
“Spiritualism is the biggest thing that has come into the 
world, and it is what the hearts of war-victims, bereaved 
fathers, mothers and wives are crying out for, consciously 
or unconsciously.” 

What if the doubting mind or troubled heart, echoing 
the ancient cry “O that I knew where I might find Him,” 
were told to follow the path of cosmological philosophy 
with its Realism and Dualism—accepting mind and matter 
as realities; or Materialism, inviting us to make such gen- 
eralizations as we can from the evidences of our senses— 
and nothing more. 

Or Idealism, with its insistence that the subjective 
existence is not only the original but the only existence, 
and dependent wholly upon the mind of the thinking sub- 
ject; and so Phenomenalism, Agnosticism, Idealistic-phe- 
nomenalism, Monism, Pantheism, Nihilism, Hegelianism, 
et al.? How can the average man learn from these how 
to know Him “Whom to know is life eternal?” Almost 
all these ignore God and His Holy Word. 

Or consider so-called Christian Science, Theosophy, 


— 130 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


and other subtle and ensnaring delusions of our time. Are 
they not all the legitimate fruits of the same “reasonings” 
which in that land of subtle intelligences has produced the 
goddess Kali—that hideous, bloody, outrageous caricature 
of divinity? 

Are not some of the mental image-makings in some 
of our higher seats of learning, as for generations in Ger- 
man Universities, resulting in debasing the minds of youth 
to practical atheism, by affecting a superior culture which 
despises Holy Scripture and elevates “Reason” to the place 
of the Supreme Arbiter? 

Is not this the peril in the Church itself? Is it not 
today? As much as on Mars Hill, the cry is for some- 
thing new, and Satan has seen to it that there is no lack 
of suggestion or stimulus to acquisitional questionings and 
speculations. The Apostle Paul feared for the converts 
in Corinth, for he was “not ignorant of Satan’s devices,” 
as alas! many Christians are today. He knew that other 
teachers would offer them a doctrine apparently more 
erudite, philosophical, and appealing more to the natural 
tastes of the people. He said: “For we are not as the 
many corrupting (making merchandise of) the Word of 
God“i(2* Corn 2:17)" “But rear lést-by any means) 
as the serpent beguiled Eve in his craftiness, your minds 
(thoughts) should be corrupted from the simplicity (one- 
foldress) and the purity that is toward Christ” (2 Cor. 


11:3). Alas! my brethren, if we do not “tremble at His 


Word” and study “in the Spirit” as well as “pray in the 
Spirit” how easily may we be corrupted from “the sim- 
plicity that is in Christ!’ It is imperative that by His 
grace we “cast down imaginations (reasonings), and every 
high thing that is exalted against the knowledge of God, 


= 131 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


and bring every thought into captivity to the obedience of 
Christ.” Surely no one could so misunderstand as to 
suppose that we meant to teach that the New Testament 
belittled the faculty of reason. Reason in its own province 
is always a potency of great value. But as Pascal says: 
“The last discovery of reason is that there is an infinity 
of things beyond reason.” The vaster realm beyond reason 
is that of things spiritual and eternal; in this domain faith 
is supreme. Need we remind ourselves that reason is the 
organ of the mental nature of man, is discursive and deals 
with facts in the district of things visible? Faith, on the 
other hand, is the organ of the spiritual nature of men, 
is perceptive rather than discursive, rises on mighty pinions 
to the altitudes of things spiritual and eternal; hears things 
inaudible to reason, and sees the invisible. “Faith,” said 
Coleridge, “is a more primary and basal faculty than rea- 
son, and the truths apprehended by it are higher than those 
cognized by the discursive reason, and are even surer 
certainties.” 

The prevalent assumption among moderns that reason 
is the chief arbiter in the religious sphere, is unscientific, 
violates the fundamental constitution of our human per- 
sonality and is contradicted by Scripture and history. 

To try to find God by mere reason is more hopeless 
than for a man lost in the Mammoth Cave of Kentucky 
to prefer to find a safe path and exit by the phosphorus 
of a glow-worm than to accept an arc light. 

The question yet remains, Who and What is God? 
It is the supreme question. We have seen that the efforts 
of the greatest minds of the race to find God by pure 
reason, through pride of heart and unbelief, has only led 
to darkened minds and debasing moralities, and that the
