a a 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 
E OQ 


world—the ignorant, the cultured, the religious world— 
by sts wisdom, has never known and can never know God. 
Who, then, is sufficient? Happy those who utter the cry 
of reverent yearning in faith: “Oh send out Thy light and 
Thy truth: let them lead me; let them bring me unto Thy 
holy hill. * * * Then will I go unto the altar of God, 
Unto God my exceeding joy.” (Ps. 43:3, 4.) 

Who ts God? Ask that little child who has learned 
to repeat the first of the Thirty-nine Articles and he will 
give you the sublime answer: “There is but one living 
and true God, Everlasting, without body, parts or passions; 
of infinite power, wisdom, and goodness; the Maker and 
Preserver of all things both visible and invisible.” 

Or, ask that other fortunate child who has been in- 
structed in the Shorter Catechism, and he will reply in 
terms so simple, comprehensive, and sublime as to in- 
finitely surpass the wisdom of all sages: “God is a Spirit, 
infinite, eternal, and unchangeable, in His being, wisdom, 
power, holiness, justice, goodness and truth.” 

Happy the children who are taught these great doc- 
trines in their most receptive and impressionable years. 
Their simplicity of language enters the child-mind, and 
grows with his growth, expanding his mind, purifying his 
heart and providing an anchorage for his soul in storms of 
doubt and darkness which may in later years break upon 
him. 

What is God? God is a Person. What an immense 
gain is this thought of a Personal God to a world bewil- 
dered and baffled between Polytheism, Pantheism, Atheism, 
and Idealism! It is profoundly significant that the only 
Monotheistic peoples the world has ever known are the 
Israelites, Christians and Mohammedans. And even the

Moslem also derived his knowledge of the One God from 
the one divine revelation, the Holy Scriptures. 

Yet man might, and ought to, have learned from the 
phenomena of things visible the Personality of God. For 
“The heavens declare the glory of God; and the firma- 
ment showeth his handiwork. Day unto day uttereth 
speech, and night unto night showeth knowledge. There 
is no speech nor language; their voice is not heard. Their 
line is gone out through all the earth, and their words to 
the end of the world.” (Ps. 19: 1-4.) 

One great musician has given us “Songs without 
Words,” but from the beginning our God has given us in 
nature wordless but most instructive discourse. Though 
lacking articulate speech, day unto day preaches, and season 
unto season proclaims the glory of God. “For the invisible 
things of him since the creation of the world are clearly 
seen, being perceived through the things which he made, 
even his everlasting power and divinity; that they may 
be without excuse.” (Rom. 1:20). Even now, despite 
the shadows, from what I humbly believe to be a wholly 
unwarranted evolutionary hypothesis, which have fallen 
upon much scientific investigation, two facts of great im- 
portance are well established. 

First, that it is now, I believe, universally accepted 
that all the forces of nature are but varied manifestations 
of one Force. Light, heat, electricity, etc., are not different 
and separate as was before supposed, but varied mani- 
festations of one Force, which is indestructible and in- 
capable either of increase or diminution. 

Second, that that one Force is neither material, nor of 
material origin, but is Spiritual. Matter is dead, and inert, 
and incapable of originating force. 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 
con esl 


What then, is this Spiritual Force which alike holds 
worlds in leash and adjusts the tremendous weight of 
atmospheric pressure to the fragile tenderness of snow- 
drop and crocus; to which spring responds with brilliant 
blooms and autumn fields wave with golden harvests; which 
silently pulls the tides of ocean, flashes in the sun-rise, 
trembles in the earthquake or gives voice to the thunder 
and the storm? Science cannot tell us. She can only 
answer, as true science ever does, humbly acknowledging 
her limitations, but declaring, from reasonable inference, 
that that one Force, which is Spiritual, is also Personal. 
It manifests itself in operations which predicate intelli- 
gence, will and power, and these are attributes of Per- 
sonality. 

But the humblest child of God speaks with assurance, 
saying: “By faith we understand that the worlds have 
been framed by the Word of God, so that what is seen 
hath not been made out of things which do appear.” 
(Heb. 11:3): 

It is a PERSONAL GOD who causeth the sun to 
shine and the rain to fall. "Twas God who “gave to the sea 
His decree. Hitherto shalt thou come, and no further.” 

“He commanded and it stood fast. The waters saw 
Thee, O God, the waters saw Thee; they were afraid.” 

Even the

Turning from the objective to the subjective, we reach 
the same conclusion. The ultimate fact and foundation of 
all sensation and of all reflection is personality. “All human 
observation implies that the mind, the ‘I’ is a thing in itself, 
a fixed point in a world of change, of which world of 
change its own organs form a part. It is the same yester- 
day, today, and tomorrow. It was what it is, when its 
organs were of a different shape and consisted of different 
matter from their present shape and matter. It will be 
what it is, when they have gone through other changes.” 
(Quoted by Dr. Van Dyke from Sir James F. Stephen). 

“Persons, then, are the most real and substantial ob- 
jects of our knowledge. We live in a world of persons, far 
more truly than in a world of phenomena or laws or ideas.” 

As man intuitionally recognized certain primary facts, 
the first of which is his own personality, so likewise he 
is certain he is not his own cause; that he is, in his per- 
sonality and constitution, an effect, a production demanding 
a sufficient cause behind it. The combined facts in the 
outer and inner worlds—the former displaying unity, intelli- 
gence, will, power, and order, and the latter with facts 
of personal intelligence, will and conscience—suggest at 
least, and intuitionally demand, that behind all the phe- 
nomena of that outer and this inner world there is a Per-

That unto all may be rendered according to their works.

character of the Father if manifested in His love to men 
is, if possible, more gloriously displayed in its expression 
of love towards the Eternal Son.

in Whom He is well-Pleased. “In Him” the saints were

GOD—HIS RELATIONS TO MAN IN CREATION 
AND IN REGENERATION 


R. A. Torrey, D. D. 


Dean of the Bible Institute of Los Angeles, President of the 
Montrose Bible Conference, Bible Teacher and Author. 


[hose who have already spoken upon that most funda- 
mental, most vital and most important of all subjects, God, 
have dealt with four great factors in the Bible teaching 
about God, namely, His Existence, His Personality, His 
Nature and His Attributes. I have been requested to ex- 
pound the fundamental truth regarding God in His rela- 
tions, His relations to the universe, and especially to man, 
God as Creator, and as Father. I have no desire to give 
you any metaphysical speculations about God, my own or 
those of others, supposedly orthodox or heterodox. I am 
simply to set forth in a coherent, systematic and orderly 
way what this Book, the Bible, teaches. It can be proven 
to the complete satisfaction of any honest seeker after 
truth, that the Bible is the Word of God. All that we 
really know about God is what God has been pleased to 
reveal to us in this Book. What this Book teaches about 
God is absolutely sure, and it is marvelously complete, sig- 
nificant, comforting and satisfying. Give up the Bible, and 
God is as Herbert Spencer and his innumerable host of im- 
measurably smaller satellites pronounce Him to be, “The 
Unknowable.” Accept the Bible, and we may know Him, 
and enjoy Him, and have communion with Him. By the 
truth of this Book made a living thing in my own soul by 


Helpful books by the above author listed on pages 446-449. 
S E 
cS haa ; 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the power of the living Spirit of God, I know Him, and re- 
joice in Him, and have communion with Him every day. 
I know Him more intimately, and more fully and more 
surely than I know any man in this Conference, or my 
most intimate friend, and some day I shall know Him even 
as He knows me today, perfectly. I shall know Him then 
even as I am known by Him today, or as Paul puts it in 1 
Cor. 13:12: “For now we see in a mirror darkly; but 
then, face to face; now I know in part; but then shall I 
know fully even as also I was fully known.” Years ago, 
when I had reached the mature wisdom of 18 years of 
age, and was a senior in Yale College, I reveled in the spec- 
ulations of Fichte, and Schelling, and Schopenhauer, and 
Hartmann, and Des Cartes and Spinoza and Kant, and 
above all Hegel. I was wondrous wise in those days, “And 
jumped into a bramble bush, (the bramble bush of meta- 
physics), and scratched out both my eyes.” Thank God the 
day came when I found indisputable evidence that the 
Bible is the Word of God, and to it alone I now go for 
God to teach me about Himself, and He does. 


I. God as Creator 


First then, WHAT DOES THIS BOOK TEACH ABOUT GoD 
AS CREATOR? 

In the first place, the Bible teaches that God is Creator 
of all created things and persons, i. e., of all persons and 
things that exist except the three uncreated and eternal 
persons, God the Father, God the Son and God the Holy 
Spirit. 

(a) He is Creator of the whole material universe. 
That is the first statement of the Bible regarding God, and 
that first statement stamps it as the inspired Word of God 


a ce me a 


Ss 


OO EEE 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


———————— eee 


in its first utterance. These are God’s first words regarding 
Himself: “In the beginning God created the Heavens and 
the earth” (Gen. 1:1). Note please that He says that He 
“created not the earth only, but the Heavens also, with all 
the worlds visible and invisible therein contained. This is 
the truth about God, as distinguished from the soul-de- 
stroying error of the materialist (who maintains that mat- 
ter is eternal in substance though ever changing its forms) 
and the equally soul-destroying error of the pantheist (who 
maintains that the universe is not only from God, but is 
God). Pantheism in countless Protean forms, Christian 
Science, Theosophy, Occultism, Esoteric Buddhism, New 
Thought, and much of the so-called New Theology, (which 
in reality in all its essential features is almost as old as 
when the Devil first began to lead men astray), etc., etc., I 
say, Pantheism in its countless Protean forms, is the ruling 
philosophy of the day. Evolution is sometimes material- 
istic, sometimes pantheistic, sometimes a strange mixture 
of both, and with a large share of its advocates in grammar 
schools, high schools, colleges, universities and theological 
seminaries, is only words, that is all, just words, as nebu- 
lous and gaseous as “the primitive fire mist,” just hot air. 
But the first verse of the Bible rules out Pantheism as well 
as Materialism. There was a time when matter was not, 
“in the beginning” (of the history of the universe) “God 
created the heavens and the earth,’ and furthermore, God 
is not Pan (everything) (Pantheism): “God created the 
heavens and the earth,” and stands apart from them though 
everywhere present in them, and passes judgment upon 
the universe that He has created. Or as we read in Gen. 
1:4: “And God saw the light that it was good,” and in 
Gen. 1:31, “And God saw everything that He had made,

and, behold, it was very good.” It is quite the fashion in 
some quarters in our day, that have an exceeding great de- 
sire to be considered scholarly, to ridicule the science and 
philosophy of the first chapter of Genesis, but if any one 
will study it carefully, he will find here a profounder phil- 
osophy, and a philosophy far more accordant with the ob- 
served facts of the universe, than that taught in any univer- 
sity that discredits the Bible. 

(b) God not only created the material universe, He 
also created all those beings that were created who were 
personal and essentially spiritual. This we learn trom Col. 
1:15-17: “Who is the image of the invisible God, the 
firstborn of all creation; for in Him were all things created, 
in the heavens and upon the earth, things visible and things 
invisible, whether thrones or dominions or principalities or 
powers; all things have been created through Him and unto 
Him; and He (that is, Christ) is before all things, and in 
Him all things consist.” Man and God are not the only 
personal and spiritual beings that there are in the universe. 
There is a great hierarchy of angels, and principalities, and 
powers, and world rulers, good and bad, (Eph. 1:20-22; 
6:12), and God created them all. 

(c) God created man, and He created man in His own 
image. The story of his creation is found in the first and 
second chapters of Genesis, which some of our learned crit- 
ical friends tell us are not only separate, but contradictory 
accounts of the creation, one being by Professor Elohist 
and the other being Professor Jehovist of - - - 
University. But in so saying, like the philosophers of 
Paul’s day, “professing themselves to be wise, they 
became fools,” (Rom. 1:22), as any one can see for 
himself who studies the accounts long enough and honestly

enough to see what they really say. The “image” of God, as 
is so evident from Col. 3:10 and Eph. 4:23, 24, has to do 
primarily with mental and moral likeness, with the power 
of thought, of moral judgment, the capacity of love and 
hate, the power of self-determination, and, as we came 
from God’s hand, upright. Therefore, all men are God’s 
“offspring.” The thought of intellectual and moral likeness 
in His essential moral and intellectual constitution. is what 
Paul had in mind when he quoted the Greek poet to the 
epicurean and stoic philosophers upon the Areopagus. This 
is evident if you will look at the words of Paul in their 
connection, (note especially Acts 17:28, 29). 

Second, God created all things in and through the 
eternal Word Who afterward became incarnate in the 
person of Jesus of Nazareth. This we learn from John 
1:1-3: “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word 
was with God, and the Word was God. The same was in 
the beginning with God. All things were made through 
Him and without (better, apart from) Him was not any- 
thing made that hath been made.” We learn the same 
thing from a passage already quoted, Col. 1: 15-17, “Who 
is the i image of the invisible God, the first fruit of all crea- 
tion; for in Him were all things created, in the heavens 
and upon the earth, things visible and things invisible, 
whether thrones or dominions, or principalities or powers; 
all things have been created through Him and unto Him; 
and He is before all things, and in Him all things consist 
(stand together, or hold together).” This same thought is 
found in the first three verses of the Bible, and also in the 
twenty-sixth verse of the first chapter. In the first three 
verses of the Bible, we have the three persons of the Trin- 
ity; “In the beginning God, (God the Father), created the

— 145 —

heavens and the earth, and the earth was without form and 
void; and darkness was upon the face of the deep, and the 
Spirit of God, (here we have the Spirit), moved upon the 
face of the waters, and God said (here we have the Word 
of God), let light be: and light was.” In a similar way we 
have the multiplicity of persons in the Godhead, and their 
union in the creative work in the twenty-sixth verse of the 
chapter, “And God said, let us make man in our image 
after our likeness.” We have not time to go into it tonight, 
but the plurality of persons in the Godhead is found hun- 
dreds of times in the Old Testament. Every time we find 
the word God in our English version, in the Hebrew text 
we have a plural name for God. It was the puzzle of the 
ancient Jewish lexicographers and grammarians, how the 
Jews, with their intense monotheism, should have a plural 
name for God. The best explanation that they could give 
was that it was the Pluralis Majestatis, that is the plural of 
majesty. To say nothing of the fact that this assumed 
figure of speech is somewhat doubtful in the Bible, even 
admitting that that figure was used in the Bible in other 
connection, there is an explanation far nearer at hand and 
far more rational, and that is that the Jews in spite of their 
intense monotheism had a plural name for God because 
there was a plurality of persons in the one Godhead. 


II. God as Father An , 


So much as to God as Creator, now let us see WHAT 
THE BIBLE TEACHES ABOUT Gop AS FATHER. 

First. The first question that confronts us is, Of 

whom is God the Father? We hear much in these days 

of “The Universal Fatherhood of God,” and of God 

being “the Father of all men.” Is this the teaching of the 


— 146 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Book, God’s own declaration about Himself? Most em- 
phatically, it is not. Read for example John 1:12, “But 
as many as received Him, to them gave he the right to 
become the children of God, even to them that believe on 
his name.” Here we have the plainest possible declaration, 
a declaration the meaning of which it is impossible to 
escape, that those who receive Jesus Christ become children 
of God. Certainly then they could not be children of God 
before they received Christ. “But,” some one will say, 
“does not Paul say in Gal. 3:26, ‘Ye are all sons of God!’ ?” 
He does, but to whom does he say it? Read the rest of 
the verse and you will find God’s answer, “through faith 
in Christ Jesus,” believers in Jesus Christ, and they only, 
have God for their father. The Lord Jesus said to certain 
men in His own day, and they were very decent men, and 
very religious men, too, “Ye are of your father the Devil” 
(John 8:44). And that is what He is saying to multitudes 
of men today, and multitudes of religious men, too, and some 
I presume who are in this audience tonight. Some years 
ago, when Mr. Alexander and myself were holding meetings 
in Ballarat, Australia, there came into the meeting one night 
quite a number of educated Chinese. I was speaking that 
night upon the text “Where art thou?” I came to a division 
in my sermon where I put the question, “Are you a child of 
God or a child of the Devil?’ My first thought was as I 
drew near to that part of my sermon, that I would leave it 
out, that it would do no good and might unnecessarily antag- 
onize some one. But God would not let me leave it out, and 
so I put it in. The next night these Chinese were out at 
the meeting again, and when I gave out the invitation for 
all those who would accept Jesus Christ as their personal 
Savior, and surrender to Him as their Lord and Master, 


eens 
— 147 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


and confess Him as such before the world, to rise, this 
whole row of Chinese arose, and came to the front with 
the rest and made a public confession of Christ. When 
they had taken their seats after this public confession of 
Christ, I turned to one who seemed to be the leader, and 
asked him if he would mind telling us just what had led 
him to take the step he had taken that night. He arose 
and faced the audience, and said in excellent English, 
“I was here last night and heard the preacher say 
that we must either be a child of God or a child of the 
Devil. I knew I was not a child of God, so concluded 
I must be a child of the Devil. I made up my mind that 
I would not be a child of the Devil any longer, so I came 
back tonight and accepted Jesus Christ and became a child 
of God.” I wish that some of you educated Americans 
would have as good sense tonight as that educated China- 
man had in Ballarat, Australia. 


Second. The next question that arises in regard to 
the Fatherhood of God is, Why are believers in Jesus 
Christ, and they only, children of God? 


The Bible answers the question. Believers in Jesus 
Christ are children of God, and they only are the children 
of God because they and they only are begotten of God, 
and made partakers of God’s own nature. This we are 
told over and over again in a variety of forms of state- 
ment. For example, we read in James 1:18, “Of his 
own will begat he us with the word of truth, that we should 
be a kind of first fruits of his creatures.” In 1 Pet. 1:23, 
we read, “Being born again (the context shows that the 
ones spoken of are those who believe on Jesus Christ), 
not of corruptible seed, but of incorruptible, by the word

are, “Whereby he hath granted unto us his precious and 
exceeding great promises; that through these (that is 
through the exceeding great and precious promises through 
the Word of God) ye may become partakers of the divine

Apostle Paul, “If any man is in Christ, he is a new crea- 
tion: the old things are passed away: behold, they are 
become new.” Believers in Jesus Christ, and only believers 
in Jesus Christ, are begotten of God, and thus made par- 
takers of God’s own nature, and therefore they, and they 
onlv, are children of God. 


Third. As the Father of all those who receive Jesus 
Christ, those who take the Lord Jesus as their personal 
Savior, and surrender to Him as their divine Lord and 
Master, and confess Him as such before the world, What 
does God our Father do for us? This subject is a very 
large one, and I can only give you a glimpse down the open 
vista of wondrous, yes, infinite blessings. You may spend 
the remainder of your life, yes eternity, in exploring them. 


(a) First of all, as I have already said, God imparts 
His own nature to us as His children. This we are told 
in the passage already quoted, 2 Pet. 1:4, “Whereby He 
hath-granted unto us His precious and exceeding great 
promises; that through these ye may become partakers of 
the divine nature, having escaped from the corruption that 
is in the world by lust.” In our natural generation we are 
born with a corrupt nature, a corrupt intellectual, emo- 


es ee 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


tional and volitional nature. We are born with a mind that 
is blind to the truth of God, or as Paul puts it, “The natural 
man receiveth not the things of the Spirit of God; for they 
are foolishness unto him and he cannot know them because 
they are spiritually judged” (1 Cor. 2:14). We are born 
with affections out of harmony with those of God. We 
love the things that God hates, and hate the things that 
God loves. We are born with a will that is perverse, a will 
that is set on pleasing self rather than on pleasing God. 
What pleases self may not be gross, and vile and immoral ; 
it may be refined and cultured, but nevertheless, pleasing 
self is the very essence of sin. 

In regeneration, God imparts to us His own nature, 
His intellectual, emotional and volitional nature. He gives 
to us a mind that is open to the truth of God, that thinks 
God’s thoughts after Him. He gives us affections that 
are in harmony with His own. And He gives us a will 
that is surrendered to the will of God, that is bent on 
pleasing God and not on pleasing self. While “The mind 
of the flesh (that is our own natural mind, and the Greek 
word here translated mind, covers thought, affection and 
will) is enmity against God,” so that “they that are in 
the flesh (that is unregenerate), cannot please God” (Rom. 
8:7, 8), in regeneration, God imparts to us “a mind” that 
is in harmony with Himself, so that we can please God. 
Regeneration is an instantaneous process. I am sometimes 
asked if I believe in sudden conversion. I believe in 
something far more wonderful than that, I believe in 
sudden regeneration. Conversion is simply an outward 
turning around, regeneration is the impartation in the in- 
most depths of our being of a new nature, a radical trans- 
formation, a “new creation.” Through the acceptance of

Jesus Christ we receive from God this new nature, a new 
intellectual, emotional and volitional nature. I know a 
man today, who, up to the time that he was forty-two 
years of age, was one of the vilest of men, a criminal, once 
invited to be a member of the Jesse James’ gang. At the 
age of forty-two, he had given to him by God through the 
acceptance of Jesus Christ, a divine nature, and he is today 
the most Christ-like man I know on earth. 


(b) In the next place, God as our Father blesses 
those who become His children by accepting Jesus Christ 
with every spiritual blessing. As Paul puts it in Eph. 1:3; 
“The God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ hath 
blessed us (that is, believers in Jesus Christ) with every 
spiritual blessing in the heavenly places in Christ.” It is 
not so much that God is to bless His children hereafter, 
He has already blessed them. In Christ Jesus He has 
already provided for every one of us every possible spirit- 
ual blessing. Our part is to claim by faith, what God has 
already provided. 


(c) God as our Father, makes all things work 
together for our good. This we are told in Rom. 8:28, 
“We know that to them that love God, all things work 
together for good, even to them that are called according 
to His purpose.” If the great truth there is in this 
passage gets into your mind and heart and stays there, you 
will never have another anxious moment as long as you live, 
for whatever comes to you, no matter how hard it may 
seem, no matter how contrary it is to what you would have 
chosen, you will know that it is one of the “all things,” 
and that God will make it work together with other things 
for your highest good; and your life can be full of sun-

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


shine from morning till night, under any circumstances, in 
any environment, under any conditions that can possibly 
arise. 

Fourth. Once more, God as our Father, undertakes 
to hear and answer our prayers. As our Lord Jesus said 
to His disciples in Matt. 7:11, “If ye then being evil, 
know how to give good gifts unto your children, how much 
more shall your Father who is in Heaven give good things 
to them that ask Him.” The whole doctrine that God 
answers our prayers rests upon the rock foundation that 
God is “our Father.” In the prayer which our Lord taught 
His disciples, the opening words are, “Our Father,” and 
this is the key note of the whole prayer, as well as the 
governing thought of the whole section of the sermon on 
the mount in which the prayer is found, (see Matt. 
6:6-15). Take away the doctrine that God is our Father, 
the Father of all believers in Jesus Christ, and the doctrine 
that God answers prayer falls to the ground. We have 
nothing but the inexorable forces and immutable laws of 
nature, and our prayers are idle breath and silly supersti- 
tion. But establish the Fatherhood of God, that the God 
Who created and rules the forces and laws of nature is our 
Father, and all the supposedly profound and philosophical 
arguments against God’s answering prayer because of the 
uniformity of law, are puerilities, unworthy of.a moment’s 
notice. And the doctrine of the Fatherhood of God, that 
God is the Father of believers, is established beyond an 
honest question. Therefore, the Lord Jesus could say in 
John 14:13: “And whatsoever ye shall ask in my name, 
that will I do, that the Father may be glorified in the Son. 
If ye shall ask anything in my name, that will I do.” And 
He could say again in John 15:7: “If ye abide in me and

be done unto you.” And John also, after sixty or more 
years of reflection upon these wonderful promises of our 
Lord Jesus, and more than sixty years of experience of 
their truth, could say in 1 John 5:14, 15. “And this is the 
confidence that we have toward him, that, if we ask any- 
thing according to his will he heareth us: and if we know 
that he heareth us whatsoever we ask, we know that we 
have the petitions which we have asked of him.” I would 
rather know that God is my Father, and had covenanted 
as such te answer my prayers, than to have the wealth of 
a Rockefeller. The time is coming for every man when 
human wealth will not avail, when difficulties will arise that 
no amount of wealth can deliver one from; but the time is 
never coming when the prayer of God’s children will not 
prevail, and when the power of God will not avail to bring 
deliverance and blessing.

THE SON OF GOD—HIS HUMANITY 


Lewis SPERRY CHAFER 


A rarely gifted Bible teacher; author of Salvation, Satan, He that 
is Spiritual, True Evangehsm. 

The Bible presents the strongest contrasts, but it con- 
tains no contradictions. That which in the Scriptures may 
seem to be a contradiction is never such in reality. God’s 
Book, written by the Holy Spirit and concerning the Infinite 
must contain the unfolding of truth which exceeds the boun- 
daries of the finite. Naturally, there are some disclosures 
which are full of mystery. In the present limitation, our 
guiding spiritual faculty is faith. We can believe every word 
that God has spoken. To believe what cannot be understood 
is not the dethroning of human reason: it is supplementing 
reason where reason cannot go. The Book of God is sealed 
until we do believe. 

That Jesus Christ was both very God and a perfect 
human being is the greatest contrast in the Scriptures. The 
acceptance of these two facts is not an optional interpre- 
tation. These two facts appear on almost every page of 
the Scriptures. It is natural and almost universal to place 
a disproportionate emphasis either on the deity or on the 
humanity of Christ. In either case it is a serious error 
and the cure is to believe just what the Bible teaches. 
Though we confront unsolved mystery at every step, we 
never confront anything that a devout mind cannot believe. 

There is but little infidelity concerning the humanity 
of Christ; but the humanity of Christ is the occasion for 
an ever increasing infidelity concerning His deity. There 


Helpful books by the above author listed on pages 446-449, 


—155— 


— rir TEESE D 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


has been no question raised as to the deity of God the 
Father, or the deity of God the Spirit. Nor would there 
have been any question concerning the deity of God the 
Son had He not subjected Himself to blind unbelief by 
taking upon Him the human form. His deity was never ° 
diminished though He became flesh. And who can meas- 
ure the sin of those who take occasion, because of His 
marvelous, condescending, redeeming grace, to question His 
eternal position as very God? 

In the following Scriptures both the humanity and the 
deity of Christ are sharply contrasted: 

“In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was 
with God, and the Word was God. The same was in the 
beginning with God * * * And the Word was made flesh, 
and dwelt among us” (John 1:1, 2, 14). 

“Who, being in the form of God, * * * was made in 
the likeness of man” (Phil. 2:6, 7). “God was in Christ, 
reconciling the world unto himself” (2 Cor. 5:19). 

“And without controversy great is the mystery of god- 
liness: God was manifest in the flesh, justified in the 
Spirit, seen of angels, preached unto the Gentiles, believed 
on in the world, received up into glory” (1 Tim. 3:16). 

“God, ** * hath in these last days spoken unto us by 
his Son, whom he hath appointed heir of all things, by 
whom also he made the worlds; who being the brightness 
of his glory, and the express image of his person, and 
upholding all things by the word of his power, when he 
had by himself purged our sins, sat down on the right 
hand of the Majesty on high” (Heb. 1: 1-3). 

“Forasmuch then as the children are partakers of 
flesh and blood, he also himself likewise took part of the 


meee ST 


— 156 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


same; that through death he might destroy him that had 
the power of death, that is, the devil.” ‘Wherefore in all 
things it behooved him to be made like unto his brethren, 
that he might be a merciful and faithful high priest in 
things pertaining to God, to make reconciliation for the 
sins of the people” (Heb. 2:14, 17). 

I have been requested to present the revelation con- 
cerning the humanity of the Son of God. In approaching 
this great doctrine, I ask you to consider it under two 
general divisions—I. The fact of His humanity, and II. 
The reason for His humanity. 


I. The Fact of the Humanity of Christ. 


We will find this presented in the Scriptures from 
seven viewpoints: 

First, Tae HUMANITY OF CHRIST WAS PURPOSED BE- 
FORE THE FOUNDATION OF THE WORLD. 

This is stated in Rev. 13:8, where Christ is declared 
to be the “Lamb slain from the foundation of the world.” 
All references to Christ as the “Lamb” are of His hu- 
manity. They concern His human body, the perfect sacri- 
fice for sin. The humanity of Christ, like the whole plan 
of redemption was purposed by God before the foundation 
of the world. The cross, with its human sacrifice, is time- 
less in its purpose and effect. 

Second, Tue OLp TESTAMENT EXPECTATION IS OF A 
Human CHRIST. 

The expectation is two-fold—(a) the types and (b) 
the prophecies. 

(a) The Types. 

Of the multitude of types we will consider but three: 


— 157 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


(1) The Passover Lamb Type (Ex. 12:1-28). The 
blood of the passover lamb became a propitiation between 
an offended God and the offending sinner. When God 
undertook to judge sinners in Egypt and wished to save, 
He must shelter those whom He would save under a 
propitiation through sacrificial blood. This type finds its 
antitype in the human blood of the Son of God which was 
freely shed for us. “Whom God hath set forth to be a 
propitiation through faith in his blood.” Thus the pass- 
over lamb type anticipates the human body and shed blood 
of Christ. a 

(2) The Scapegoat Type (Lev. 16:8-28). Two 
goats were required to complete this type. Sin must be 
canceled as a debt. This was typified in the death of the 
first goat. Sin must also be blotted out, removed, and 
forgotten. This was typified in the banishing of the second 
goat into the unknown wilderness. It all speaks of the 
Antitype. Christ has reconciled us to God by taking the 
offense “out of the way,” and He has removed our trans- 
gressions from us, which were contrary to us, as far as 
the east is from the west. The scapegoat type also antici- 
pates the humanity of Christ. 

(3) The Kinsman Redeemer Type (Lev. 25: 25-30). 
In the commonwealth of Israel, any lost estate might be 
redeemed by a kinsman. He could not be of the immediate 
family else he would be involved in the calamity; but: a 
near kinsman who possessed the substance, ability and will- 
ingness might redeem. This is the story of Ruth. Her 
estate, though forfeited, was redeemed by her kinsman, 
Boaz. We may also add that, after he had redeemed, he took 
Ruth to be his bride. All this typifies the work of Christ as 
expressed in these words: “But when the fulness of the

a

me 


Phen is Tees,

GOD HATH SPOKEN 


time was come, God sent forth his Son, made of a woman, 
made under the law, to redeem them that were under the 
law, that we might receive the adoption of sons” (Gal. 4:4, 
5). “Husbands, love your wives, even as Chirst also loved 
the church, and gave himself for it; that he might sanctify 
and cleanse it with the washing of water by the word, that 
he might present it to himself a glorious church, not having 
spot, or wrinkle, or any such thing; but that it should be 
holy and without blemish” (Eph. 5: 25-27). 

The kinsman redeemer type likewise anticipates the 
humanity of Christ. 

(b) The Prophecies. 

From the great body of prophecy concerning the hu- 
manity of Christ we need quote but a few verses. “And 
I will put enmity between thee and the woman, and between 
thy seed and her seed; it shall bruise thy head, and thou 
shalt bruise his heel” (Gen. 3:15). “Therefore the Lord 
himself shall give you a sign: Behold a virgin shall con- 
ceive, and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel” 
(Isa. 7:14). 

A virgin conceiving and bearing a son is human; yet 
this is to be Immanuel, which being interpreted is “God 
with us.” 

“For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given; 
and the government shall be upon his shoulder: and his 
name shall be called Wonderful, Counsellor, The mighty 
God, The everlasting Father, The Prince of Peace. Of 
the increase of his government and peace there shall be no 
end, upon the throne of David, and upon his kingdom, to 
order it, and to establish it with judgment and with justice 
from- henceforth even forever. The zeal of the Lord of 
hosts will perform this” (Isa. 9:6, 7). 


— 159 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


A child born, with government upon his shoulder, ex- 
ecuting justice from the throne of David is human: A 
Son given, Who is the Wonderful, Counsellor, The mighty 
God, The everlasting Father and The Prince of Peace, 
is in the fullest degree divine. | 


The patriarch Job was conscious of an insuperable 
distance between himself and God. His desire was for a 
“daysman” who could both lay his hand upon God and 
upon man. This is his cry for a mediator: “For he is not 
a man, as I am, that I should answer him, and we should 
come together in judgment. Neither is there any daysman 
betwixt us, that might lay his hand upon us both” (Job 
9:32, 33). The Old Testament expectation in types and 
prophecies is of the humanity of Christ. 


Third, THe Fact OF THE HUMANITY oF CHRIST IS 
SEEN IN His ANNUNCIATION AND BIRTH. 


We have already seen this expectation in the prohpecy 
concerning a virgin who was to conceive and bear a son, 
and concerning a child that was to be born. This is de- 
clared again in Luke 1:31-35: “And, behold; thou shalt 
conceive in thy womb, and bring forth a son, and shalt 
call his name Jesus. He shall be great, and shall be called 
the Son of the Highest: and the Lord God shall give unto 
him the throne of his father David: and he shall reign 
over the house of Jacob forever; and of his kingdom 
there shall be no end. * * * The Holy Ghost shall 
come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall over- 
shadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall 
be born of thee shall be called the Son of God.” 

The humanity of Christ is thus revealed in His annun- 
ciation and birth.

Fourth, THe Fact or THE HUMANITY oF CHRIST IS

“Wherefore in all things it behooved him to be made 
like unto his brethren.” He is declared to be perfectly 
human, 

‘(a) By His Names. Jesus is His human name. It 
is related to His human life, His body, His death and the 
acquired glory bestowed because of His redeeming grace 
(Phil. 2:5-9). Several times He is called “The Man 
Christ Jesus,” and about eighty times He is called “The 
Son of Man.” This latter title was the name He most 
often gave Himself. It was as though, from the divine 
standpoint, the human aspect of His person needed most 
to be disclosed. 

(b) By His Human Parentage. Several unmistak- 
able phrases are used of Christ concerning His parentage: 
“fruit of the loins,” “her firstborn,” “of this man’s seed,” 
“seed of David,” “His father David,” “the seed of Abra- 
ham,” “made of a woman,” “sprang of Judah.” His hu- 
manity is stated by each of these phrases. 

(c) By the Fact that He possessed a Human Body, 
Soul, and Spirit. 

Note these scriptures: “Hereby know ye the Spirit 
of God: Every spirit that confesseth that Jesus Christ is 
come in the flesh is of God: and every spirit that con- 
fesseth not that Jesus Christ is come in the flesh is not 
of God: and this is that spirit of antichrist, whereof ye 
have heard that it should come” (1 John 4:2, 9). “Then 
saith he unto them, My soul is exceeding sorrowful, even 
unto death” (Mt. 26:38). “When Jesus had thus said, 
he was troubled in spirit” (John 13:21), 


pS 


— 161 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


(d) By His Human Limitations. At this point we 
are confronted with the strongest contrasts between the 
deity and the humanity of Christ. 

He was weary; yet He called the weary to Himself 
for rest. 

He was hungry; yet He was “the bread of life.” 

He was thirsty; yet He was “the water of life.” 

He was in an agony; yet He healed all manner of 
sicknesses and soothed every pain. 

He “grew and waxed strong in spirit”; yet He was 
from all eternity. 

He was tempted; yet He, as God, could not be 
tempted. 

He was self-limited in knowledge; yet He was the 
wisdom of God. 

He said, “My Father is greater than I” (with refer- 
ence to His humiliation, being made for a little season 
lower than the angels); yet He also said, “He that hath 
seen me hath seen the Father,” “I and my Father are 
one.” 

He prayed, which is always human; yet He Himself 
answered prayer. 

He said, “This is your hour and the power of dark- 
ness”; yet all power is given unto Him in heaven and in 
earth. 

He slept on a pillow in the boat; yet He arose and 
rebuked the storm. ‘ 

He was baptized, which was only a human act; yet at 
that time God declared Him to be His Son. 

He walked two long days’ journey to Bethany; yet 
He knew the moment that Lazarus died. 

He wept at the tomb; yet He called the dead to arise.

He confessed that He would be put to death; yet 
He had but a moment before received .Peter’s inspired 
declaration that He was the Christ, the Son of the living 
God. 


He said, “Whom do men say that I the Son of Man 
am?”; yet John tells us, “He needed not that any should 
testify of man: for He knew what was in man.” 


He was hungry; yet He could turn stones into bread. 
This He did not do; for had He done so, He would not 
have suffered as we suffer. 


He said, “My God, My God, why hast thou torsaken 
me”; yet it was that very God to Whom He cried Who 
was “in Christ reconciling the world unto himself.” 

He dies; yet He is eternal life. 

He freely functioned His earth-life within that which 
was perfectly human, and He as freely functioned His 
earth-life within that which was perfectly divine. His 
earth-life, therefore, testifies as much to His humanity as 
to His deity, and we believe both of these revelations to 
be equally true. 

Fifth, Tue FACT or THE HUMANITY OF CHRIST IS 
SEEN IN His DEATH AND RESURRECTION. 

It was human blood that was required and that was 
shed as a sacrifice for sin. It was also the blood of the 
Son of God. 

It is perilous to attempt a close analysis at this point. 
We must not say that the deity of Christ forsook His 
humanity on the cross, else any human sacrifice might 
have sufficed. It was God that was in Christ and the 
reconciling work was not done until He had really died.

GOD HATH SPOKEN. 


God did not use Jesus as a sacrifice: God was in Christ. 
We are not said to be redeemed alone by the precious blood 
of Jesus—His human name: it is by the blood of Christ. It 
is “Jesus Christ the righteous” Who is the propitiation for 
the sins of the whole world. No separation can be per- 
mitted. It was the human blood of the Son of God. “For 
it is not possible that the blood of bulls and of goats should 
take away sins. Wherefore when he cometh into the 
world, he saith, Sacrifice and offerings thou wouldest not, 
* * * neither hadst pleasure therein; which are offered 
by the law; then said he, Lo, I come to do thy will, O God. 
* * * By the which will we are sanctified through the 
offering of the body of Jesus Christ once for all” (Heb. 
10: 4-10). 

He fulfilled the types of propitiation, reconciliation 
and redemption. ‘‘Forasmuch as ye know that ye were 
not redeemed with corruptible things, as silver and gold, 
from your vain conversation received by tradition from 
your fathers; but with the precious blood of Christ, as of 
a lamb without blemish and without spot” (1 Pet. 1:18, 
19). “Christ hath redeemed us from the curse of the law, 


being made a curse for us: for it is written, Cursed is 


every one that hangeth on a tree” (Gal. 3:13). 

_ Furthermore, His resurrection body was a glorified 
human body. He said to Thomas, “Reach hither thy finger, 
and behold my hands; and reach hither thy hand and 
thrust it into my side: and be not faithless, but believing.” 
He said to the eleven, “Behold my hands and my feet, that 
it is I myself: handle me, and see: for a spirit hath not 
flesh and bones, as ye see me have.” The women “held 
him by the feet, and worshipped him,” and He ate food 
before them all.

Every aspect of the death and resurrection of Christ 
is proof of His humanity. 


Sixth, THe FACT OF THE HUMANITY oF CHRIST IS 
SEEN IN His ASCENSION AND PRESENT MINISTRY IN 
HEAVEN. 


While they steadfastly looked they saw Him go into 
heaven with His resurrection human body. He sat down 
“at the right hand of the throne of God.” He is also 
spoken of as “The Son of man which is in heaven.” Ste- 
phen, when he saw Him long after His ascension, said 
“Behold, I see the heavens opened, and the Son of man 
standing on the right hand of God.” Through His hu- 
manity, Christ has been made “a merciful and faithful 
high priest in things pertaining to God.” He is now in 
heaven as our High Priest. His humanity is declared by 
His ascension and present ministry in heaven. 


Seventh, Tue FACT or THE HUMANITY OF CHRIST Is 
REVEALED IN His Cominc AGAIN AND IN His KINGDOM 
REIGN. 


The angel messengers said, “This same Jesus, which 
is taken up from you into heaven, shall so come in like 
manner as ye have seen him go into heaven.” He said 
of Himself, “They shall see the Son of man coming in 
the clouds of heaven.” He will then “sit on the throne 
of his glory,” “He shall sit on the throne of his Father 
David.” The humanity of Christ is seen, then, in His 
return to the earth and in His kingdom reign. 


From every viewpoint the Scriptures declare Christ 
to be perfectly human as well as perfectly divine. We may 
yet consider 


— 165 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


II. The Biblical Reasons for the Humanity of 
Christ. 

These are seven: 

First, HE Came To REVEAL Gop. 

In presenting a reason for the Word having been 
made flesh, John states, “No man hath seen (fully seen) 
God at any time; the only begotten Son, which is in the 
bosom of the Father, he hath declared him.” In Christ, 
the infinite God has revealed Himself in terms adapted 
to the finite understanding. So Christ could say, “He that 
hath seen me hath seen the Father,” and “Neither knoweth 
any man the Father, save the Son, and he to whomsoever 
the Son will reveal him.” Again we read, “God com- 
mendeth his love toward us, in that, while we were yet 
sinners, Christ died for us.” “Hereby perceive we the 
love of God, because he laid down his life for us.” He 
who was “full of grace and truth” was, by His human 
presence in the world, declaring the heart, or bosom, of 
God to men. 

What is God like? Christ is the answer. When we 
see Him placing His hands on little children to bless them, 
healing the sick, raising the dead, weeping at the tomb, and 
weeping over a city, burning hot at the Pharisees, and 
dying to save the lost, that is what God is like. 


Second, He Came To Reveat Man. 

He was not only perfectly human: He was the perfect 
human being. He was God’s ideal man. He is the perfect 
example to all believers, though never an example to the 
human. He was God’s ideal man. He is the perfect ex- 
ample to all believers, though never an example to the 
world. “Let this mind be in you which was also in Christ 


= OO 


bs Wi sae E ERASE Cosa ate F PN 


aE 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Jesus.” “For even hereunto were ye called: because Christ 
also suffered for us, leaving us an example, that ye should 
follow in his steps” (1 Pet. 2:21). “For to me to live 
is Christ.” And the final work of saving grace will be 
to have conformed us into the image of His Son. When 
we see Him we shall be perfect men. We shall be like 
Him. 


Third, He Came To PROVIDE A SACRIFICE FOR SIN. 


It is folly to argue against the revealed law of the 
kingdom of God “that without the shedding of blood, 
there is no remission.” To deny this theme is to break 
the crimson thread that binds the Book of God. It is to 
render every revelation useless; for unless all is true, noth- 
ing can be known to be true in God’s Holy Oracles. What 
could be more unscientific and illogical than that some 
portions of this one revelation should be relied upon for 
faithful testimony, while other portions are rejected? 
Jesus Christ laid aside the garments of His glory, was 
made in the likeness of men and being found in fashion 
as a man humbled himself, and became obedient unto death, 
even the death of the cross. He shed His precious blood 
as a ransom for us all, else His cross is foolishness and 
the Word of God is branded as unreliable, misleading, and 
untrue. Because that perfect sacrifice for sin .has been 
made, we may call upon men to believe on this Savior 
and be saved. This is the Gospel of God’s grace. The 
work is finished. The Savior has died. “But we see 
Jesus, who was made a little lower than the angels for 
the suffering of death, * * * that he should taste death 
for every man” (Heb. 2:9). “The Son of man is come 
to seek and to save that which is lost” (Luke 19; 10). 


— 167 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Fourth, He Came To DESTROY THE WORKS OF THE 
DEVIL. 

Of this undertaking we could know nothing beyond 
that which is told us in the Bible. There are several im- 
portant passages on this theme: “Forasmuch then as the 
children are partakers of flesh and blood, he also himself 
likewise took part of the same; that through death he 
might destroy him that had the power of death, that is, 
the devil; and deliver them who through fear of death 
were all their lifetime subject to bondage” (Heb. 2:14, 
15). .“For this purpose the Son of God was manifested, 
that he might destroy (undo) the works of the devil” 
(1 John 3:8). “And you, being dead in your sins and 
the uncircumcision of your flesh, hath he quickened to- 
gether with him, having forgiven you all trespasses; blot- 
ting out the handwriting of ordinances that was against us, 
which was contrary to us, and took it out of the way, nail- 
ing it to his cross; and having spoiled principalities and 
powers, he made a shew of them openly, triumphing over 
them in it” (Col. 2:13-15). “Now is the judgment of 
this world: now shall the prince of this world be cast out” 
(John 12:31). “The prince of this world is judged” 
(John 16: 11). 

Satan had held men in his authority on no other 
grounds than that they were sinners and that God was 
holy. God could not be complicated with sinners apart 
from redemption by blood; but Satan could. By the cross 
of Christ the sin of the whole world was borne and there 
is now no ground for Satan’s claim on any person in the 
world. Satan’s claim was broken. Principalities and 
powers were triumphed over in the cross. 

The wages of sin is death, and, up to the cross, Satan 


— 168 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


had the power of death; but by the cross that power was 
forever transferred to the Lord of Glory. “I am he that 
liveth, and was dead; and, behold, I am alive for evermore, 
Amen; and have the keys of hell and of death.” Now 
there is deliverance from the fear of death. The Lord 
Jesus came from heaven to earth and partook of flesh and 
blood that He might undo the works of the devil. 


Fifth, He Came Tuar He Micut Be a MerciIFuUL 
AND FAITHFUL HicH Priest IN THINGS PERTAINING TO 
Gop. 

The high priest of the Old Testament is a type of the 
present high priesthood of Christ. This is one of the 
themes of the Epistle to the Hebrews. The office of the 
high priest was to represent the people before God in the 
holy of holies. He went in once a year, and then only 
with blood. Christ has now entered into the heavenly 
tabernacle once for all, and not without blood: He hath 
shed His own blood. Note several passages from the 
whole argument of this great Epistle: “For verily he 
took not on him the nature of angels; but he took on him 
the seed of Abraham. Wherefore in all things it behooved 
him to be made like unto his brethren, that he might be 
a merciful and faithful high priest in things pertaining to 
God” (2:16, 17). “Now of the things which we have 
spoken this is the sum: We have such an high priest, 
who is set down on the right hand of the throne of the 
Majesty in the heavens” (8:1). “But Christ being come 
an_ high priest of good things to come, by a greater and 
more perfect tabernacle, not made with hands, that is to 
say, not of this building; neither by the blood of goats and 
calves, but by his own blood he entered in once into the 
holy place, having obtained eternal redemption for us”

The Lord Jesus Christ, we are told, became in all

Seventh, He Came To BE Heap Over a New Crea-

Christ entered the human family that He might be- 
come the “Last Adam,” the Head of a new creation. Be- 
cause of His human relationship and all that He accom- 
plished in His life, death and resurrection there is given 
unto Him: 

(a) A New Name, “Wherefore God hath highly

name: that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow”

(b) A New Glory, “And the Word was made flesh,

He Who is the eternal Son, Jehovah God, was also 
the son of Mary, the boy of Nazareth, the teacher and 
healer in Judea, the guest at Bethany, the Christ of Cal- 
vary. He will yet be the King of Glory, as He is now 
our Savior, our High Priest, our Brother, our coming 
Bridegroom and Lord.

THE SON OF GOD—HIS DEITY 


A. B. WINCHESTER. 


Why discuss this question? In view of the manifold 


“declarations of the inspired Word of God, which, beyond 


the shadow of a doubt, establish this central doctrine of 
our holy faith, may we not take it for granted? 


All the more so because every great confession of 
Christendom embodies it in its very heart. From the so- 
called Apostles’ Creed, the Nicene, the Athanasian, and 
about thirty other ancient creeds, down to the Augsburg, 
the Heidelberg, the Genevan, the Westminster, the Thirty- 
Nine Articles, the Sermons, Notes and Disciplines of John 
Wesley, etc., etc., all with one voice and impressive em- 
phasis proclaim “Jesus, Lord, to the glory of God the 
Father” (Phil. 2:11); and “honour the Son, even as they 
honour the Father.” (John 5:23.) 


Doubtless because the deity of Jesus Christ is the very 
citadel of Christianity, therefore more than any other doc- 
trine, save perhaps the atoning blood of the Son of God, 
has the arch-enemy sought persistently and deceitfully to 
discredit it. No doubt through Satan’s malign suggestion 
infidels and ignorant persons, whether wilfully or other- 
wise, have perverted and continue to pervert the plain 
teaching of Scripture respecting this fundamental of funda- 
mentals, thus compelling the faithful witnesses of our Lord 


— 173 — 


ee 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Jesus to “contend earnestly for the faith” and to meet each 
new phase of error by a restatement of “the faith once 
for all delivered to the saints.” (Jude 3.) 

In every period of the Christian era there have not 
been wanting some enemies of the church who have en- 
deavored either by blasphemous denial or by pretended 
patronage of the man Jesus, to rob our blessed Lord of the 
crown of Deity. But it has been reserved largely for the 
twentieth century to have within the church professed 
followers, and even ministers of Christ, who on the unsup- 
ported ground of superior scholarship take sacreligious 
liberties with God’s Word, and whilst with elusive terms 
and subtle sophistry purporting to restore the real Christ, 
are in fact as derogatory to the kingly and divine majesty 
of the holy Son of God and as hateful in their attempt to 
betray the Christ to His enemies, as was the blistering kiss 
of Iscariot on that awful night in Gethsemane. One of 
these in a recent article in a church magazine on “God- 
making” says: “Modern scholarship has the task to reach 
down through a mass of dogma and creed formulated 
through the ages, and bring to light again the Gospel of 
Jesus in all its beautiful and saving simplicity.” To the 
uninitiated that has a reverent and captivating sound. The 
discriminating reader sees at once that the “beautiful and 
saving simplicity” is to be restored by the degradation of 
the Person of the Lord Jesus to a mere man, the elmina- 
tion of the supernatural from the record, and the substitu- 
tion of the ipse dixit of Professors Pfleiderer, Shailer 
Matthews, Rauschenbusch, etc., etc., for the authority of 
the inspired Word of God. He seems to treat the words of 
the rationalists far more reverently and as of much greater 
reliability than the Word of God. He quotes one of these—

was he not head of the Federation of the Churches of 
Christ in America?—without editing: “As we pass from 
Jesus to the apostles, from the synoptics to John and Paul, 
we become conscious of a change of atmosphere. That 
which was secondary in the preaching of Jesus, now becomes 
prominent. The Messianic claims of Jesus are put to the 
forefront and magnified out of all proportion to the im- 
portance Jesus Himself attached to them.” 


Oh that some humble, devout and instructed disciple 
could but take some of these poor learned professors aside, 
as Aquila and Priscilla did the learned and eloquent Ap- 
ollos, and “expound unto them the way of God more 
accurately!” (Acts 18: 24-26.) They would then learn that 
the message was identical in purpose and spirit and that 
the progress in the revelation was not due to the specula- 
tions, traditions, philosophizings, and mental image-makings 
of men, but a fulfilment of the Word of the Lord as 
recorded in such passages as John 16:12-14; 14:26. 


It is high time to warn the unwary of these professed 
friends of Jesus Christ who dare to sit in judgment on 
the Everlasting Word—which shall one day judge them 
(John 5:22, 23; 12:47, 48; Rev. 22:18, 19)—and if it 
does not say what their vain pride of reason suggests it 
ought to have said, they alter, amend, twist its language, 
and hypothesize until it does say it, or declare it (without a 
tittle of evidence) an interpolation, and rule it out of 
the text. 


So many Christians, alas! but little acquainted with 
the Word of God, or the Holy Spirit, the divine Teacher 
(John 16:13; 1 John 2:27) tremble when professors or 
doctors of divinity speak slightingly of the inspired Word 


— 175 — 


+ 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


and deem it presumption to oppose such derogatory state- 
ments with a “thus saith the Lord.” Let such ponder the 
deliverance of a professor who occupies the Chair of 
Psychology in a celebrated college in the United States and 
who has made exhaustive investigation among professors 
and teachers in universities and colleges in America. He 
is reported to have said: “More than fifty per cent of the 
most distinguished professors in American colleges do not 
believe in God. Only about thirty-five per cent. believe 
in the immortality of the soul.” What title or capacity have 
such professors (for bless God we have scholars of the first 
magnitude who are humble and loyal disciples of the Lord 
Jesus) to say anything about the Christ of God, or about 
His holy Word? One humble student of Scripture who 
has been “born from above” even if without academic cul- 
ture knows more of the mind of God, than any number of 
such professors whatever their mere intellectual equipment. 
For it is written: “Now the natural men (i. e., the psychical 
or unspiritual, however mentally trained or informed) 
receiveth not the things of the Spirit of God: for they are 
foolishness unto him: and he cannot know them, because 
they are spiritually judged” (or discerned). (1 Cor. 2:14.) 

This much of warning I deemed dutiful to give con- 
cerning “the foes of one’s own household” (Matt. 10:36), 
for those “blind guides” are the greatest injury and hind- 
rance to Christ’s cause in the world today. 

And now coming more directly to the subject in hand, 
no reasonable person will look for a full and orderly state- 
ment of the proofs of our Lord’s Deity within the limits 
of one address. Trusting in the blessed Holy Spirit Who 
is ever ready to guide the humble and obedient into truth, 
I hope to show that Scripture is saturated with this precious

truth in every part. I pray that I may help to confirm the 
faith of those Christians who, though having been qualified 
by the Holy Spirit to call Jesus “Lord,” have not perceived 
the immutable foundations in the Word on which that 
doctrine securely reposes. It may add not merely intellectual 
joy to their blessed spiritual experience, but may enable them 
to fortify youth against the plausible and heretical, rational- 
istic speculators who use the positions which Christianity has 
made possible to destroy confidence in the inspired Word, 
and faith in our divine Lord. Fortunately it is not a question 
of translation depending upon the investigator’s knowledge 
of Hebrew or Greek. A few texts offered in support of the 
Deity of our Lord may lose some force by an alternative 
reading or varied rendering, but more are strengthened by 
sanction of the highest authorities, such as: Titus 2:13, 
A. V. “Looking for that blessed hope and the glorious ap- 
pearing of the great God and our Savior Jesus Christ.” 
The R. V. “Of our great God and Savior” showing that the 
One who appeared (v. 11) bringing salvation and the One 
who is to appear in glory is the same One, and He is no 
other than “our great God and Savior” But as I said, it 
it not a matter of a few texts which may be in dispute as 
to best translation or reading, for the subject is interwoven 
with the whole warp and woof of the Scripture. 

I pray you let us approach this subject in the spirit 
of worshippers, anything less would be nothing short of 
profanity.

of righteousness arise.” Here we have (a) a prerequisite 
condition, “you who fear my name”; (b) a sure promise,

tunate you are, if you have witnessed, as I have, a sunrise

from a high mountain. First the scarce perceptible light 
like a gray mist pushing back the night’s sable robe; then 
the gradual whitening of the horizon, deepening to roseate 
and golden glow; then the turquoise pierced by shaft after 
shaft, beam after beam, glory upon glory until the earth 
and heaven were filled with the brightness of the robe of 
their Creator Who covered Himself “with light as with a 
garment.” Which things are an allegory of the progress 
of our knowledge of the Lord Jesus the Sun of Righteous- 
ness. How many Christians are yet in the “mist” stage of 
the knowledge of Christ! How earnestly Paul prayed for 
the Christians at Colosse in regard to the increase in the 
knowledge of Christ! (Col 1:9, 10.) The measure of our 
Christian attainment, usefulness and joy, is in exact ratio 
to the measure of our knowledge of the Lord Jesus. (2 
Peter 1:2-11.) Then shall ye know if ye follow on to- 
know the Lord. “The path of the just is as the shining 
light, that shineth more and more unto the perfect day.” 
The doctrines, promises, prophecies, types, precepts, 
and every part of Scripture, though of immeasurable value 
are yet lustreless until seen in the light of the incarnate 
God. Take for example one type of Christ—the uncon- 
sumed burning bush, in Exodus 3. And first consider the 
approach of Moses to that mystery, as showing how we 
ought to “draw nigh.” When in that lonely land of Midian 
tending his father-in-law’s flock, “the angel of the Lord 
appeared unto him in a flame of fire out of the midst of a 
bush; and he looked, and, behold, the bush burned with 
fire, and the bush was not consumed. And Moses said, 
I will now turn aside, and see this great sight, why the 
bush is not burnt. And when the Lord saw that he turned 
aside to see, God called unto him out of the midst of the 


— 178 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


bush, and said, Moses, Moses. And he said, Here am I. 
And he said, Draw not nigh hither: put off thy shoes from 
off thy feet, for the place whereon thou standest is holy 
ground. * * *” (Exodus 3:3-6). Beloved, is not that 
same Angel of the Covenant here? And is it not befitting 
that we should “put off our shoes” since this is also “holy 
ground”? 

The bush burning and unconsumed was a great mys- 
tery. Moses might have turned from it to consider some- 
thing “practical’”—as men of affairs say. If he had done 
so what a vision, an experience, a life-work, a character, 
and a glory he would have missed! All progress of knowl- 
edge in any kind is made possible only by the recognition 
at once of fact and of mystery. Every fact has its mys- 
tery, and each mystery has its fact. The scientific pro- 
cedure is to make the known, the stepping stone to the 
unknown; to advance from the simple to the complex; 
from the fact to the mystery. To invert that order, ignore 
the fact and begin with the mystery is unscientific and an 
effective barrier to any possible advance in knowledge. 
Remember this is the inexorable law of advance in knowl- 
edge of any kind, secular or religious. “Great is the 
mystery of godliness: God was manifest in the flesh.” 
Ra tor (1 Pini /3:216:))  Inestudying-this#greatwmys- 
tery” we must follow the same order, t. e., first the fact, 
then the mystery. This is precisely what rationalistic 
theologians and skeptics have not done. Moses was scien- 
tific. His attention was arrested by the fact of the bush 
and the fact of the flame. He would investigate the facts 
reverently and carefully, waiting patiently for the unfold- 
ing of the mystery. Beloved, do not miss that important 
lesson. That lowly bush burning unconsumed is a type 


SY 


— 179 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


radiant with the glory of the angel of the Covenant, our 
gracious and glorious Lord Jesus Christ. It foreshadows 
Him in the mysterious constitution of His complex Person 
and in the great redeeming work which necessitated for 
its accomplishment the union (not blending) of the divine 
and human natures in one mysterious and glorious Person. 
The flame in the unconsumed bush typified the presence 
of Jehovah—Jesus, anticipating, as in other types, the 
future appearing in “flesh” of the great God and our 
Savior Jesus Christ. 


The same who said to Moses: “J am the God of 
your fathers” hath spoken in His Word to us: “Before 
Abraham was I am.” “No man hath ascended up to heaven, 
but he that came down from heaven, even the Son of man 
which is in heaven.” (John 3:13.) 


The lowly bush was the type of His humanity “who 
was meek and lowly.” The bush (humanity) subduing the 
otherwise insufferable effulgence of the flame (divine 
glory). In Deut. 4:24, we read that “the Lord thy God 
is a consuming fire.” Exodus 24:17: “And the sight of 
the glory of the Lord was like devouring fire.’ Heb. 
12:29: “our God is a consuming fire.” When we read 
elsewhere that Jesus endured the Father’s wrath and 
apply to the type here (as we might to other types such 
as sin-offering), we see the fire of holiness and justice 
burning in Jesus Christ, yet “unconsumed”—dying yet 
living; humbled yet exalted; defeated yet victorious. It 
was when Jehovah saw the helplessness of Israel under 
the cruel oppressor that he appeared in the flaming bush 
and said “J am come down to deliver them out of the 
hand of the Egyptians, and to bring them up out of that 


— 180 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


land into a good land and a large, unto a land flowing with 
milk and honey.” (Exodus 3:8.) 

And when God sent His own Son “in the likeness 
of sinful flesh and for sin” (after He saw our helpless- 
ness and woe) it was to deliver us from a more hopeless 
and bitter bondage than the Egyptian. The cry had been 
wrung from the lips of each member of a ruined race “O 
wretched man that I am! who shall deliver me from the 
body of this death?” (Rom. 7:24.) “And I looked and 
there was none to help * * * therefore mine own 


arm brought salvation.” (Isa. 63:5.) “For when we 
were yet without strength, in due time Christ died for the 
ungodly.” 


Israel then through Jehovah’s grace was brought into 
covenant relationship; believers through grace in Christ 
are made heirs of a new covenant. They were brought 
out of the land of bondage and misery into a land of 
liberty and “flowing with milk and honey.” Believers 
through the “good will of Him that dwelt in the bush” 
“Who gave himself for our sins, that he might deliver us 
from this present evil age” (Gal. 1:4), have been brought 
out and have been “sealed with that holy Spirit of promise, 
which is the earnest of our inheritance until the redemp- 
tion of the purchased possession, unto the praise of his 
glory.” (Eph. 1:13, 14.) We are thus “brought out” 
and “brought in.’ When He redeemed them He called 
Israel His son by adoption. “When the fulness of the 
time was come, God sent forth his Son, made of a woman, 
made under the law, to redeem them that were under the 
law, that we might receive the adoption of sons.” (Gal. 
4:4, 5.) Even this cursory glance at surface truths in 
one type may help to confirm what we have already said 


— 181 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


that this doctrine of the eternal Son our Savior is diffused 
through the entire volume of God’s inspired Word. 

It is impossible to deny that there are difficulties. 
The doctrine is particularly repugnant to the so-called 
modern mind. To such with their vain trust in mere reason, 
and no real trust in the God of revelation, the mystery is 
flatly described as incredible and absurd. Forget not how- 
ever that denying the Deity of Jesus Christ does not lessen 
but multiplies difficulties. Assuredly the difficulties of unbe- 
lief are more and greater than the difficulties of faith. 
Infinitely mysterious as is the “great mystery”’—“God 
manifest in flesh”—yet it is the solvent, if accepted in 
faith, of every other mystery. 

Mystery indeed! That a babe born in a manger in 
Bethlehem, growing naturally as a child in the house of 
a carpenter of Nazareth, should be at the same time the 
God of all men and the Creator and upholder of all worlds, 
is a great mystery indeed and must remain so. It is 
enough for the humble Christian to remember that God’s 
Word says it is true, and all prophecy and types point to 
that central truth. “The Word was made flesh, and dwelt 
among us, (and we beheld His glory, the glory as of the 
only begotten of the Father,) full of grace and truth.” 
(John 1:14.) If Jesus is a divine Person He is one in 
essence, nature and glory with the Father and with the 
Holy Ghost. The mysterious distinction which exists be- 
tween the Father, the Son, and the Holy Ghost is not a 
distinction affecting their unity—they are “one in sub- 
stance, equal in power and glory,” not independent, but 
related. In vindication of this claim consider briefly (1) 
the testimony of the Scriptures to the pre-incarnate Christ. 
Isa.9:6; “Unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given:
