separate” (2 Cor. 6:17). Having these promises, they 
are to cleanse themselves “from all filthiness of the flesh 
and spirit, perfecting holiness (sanctification) in the fear 
of God” (2 Cor. 7:1). “This I say then, Walk in the 
Spirit, and ye shall not fulfil the lust of the flesh” (Gal. 
5616): 

Sixth, Man may sanctify persons and things. “For 
the unbelieving husband is sanctified by the wife, and the 
unbelieving wife is sanctified by the husband, else were your 
children unclean; but now are they holy” (sanctified, 1 
Cor. 7:14). “And the priests shall sanctify the people.” 
“So they sanctified the house of the Lord.” 

Seventh, One thing may sanctify another thing. “For 
whether is greater, the gold, or the temple that sanctifieth 
the gold?” “For whether is greater, the gift, or the altar 
that sanctifieth the gift?” (Mt. 23:17, 19). 

From this very limited consideration of the Scriptures 
on the subject of Sanctification and Holiness, it is evident 
that the root meaning of the word is to set apart unto a holy 
purpose. The thing set apart is sometimes cleansed and 
sometimes it is not. Sometimes it can itself partake of 
the character of holiness and sometimes, as in the case of 
an inanimate thing, it cannot. Yet a thing which of itself 
can be neither holy nor unholy, is just as much sanctified 
when set apart unto God as is the person whose moral 
character is subject to transformation. It is also evident 
that where these moral qualities exist, cleansing and puri- 
fication are sometimes required in sanctification; but not 
always. 

` Thus far our consideration has been only of the gen- 
eral meaning of the words and their application both in the 


— 271 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN. 


Old and in the New Testaments. Beyond this very brief 
examination of the whole latitude of the Bible teaching 
about Sanctification and Holiness, we have yet to consider. 


III. The deeper aspect of truth concerning the sanctifi- 
cation in the New Testament. 


Though the exact meaning of the words “sanctify,” 
“holy,” and “saint” is unchanged, there is a far deeper 
reality indicated by their use in the New Testament than 
is indicated by their use in the Old Testament. The Old 
Testament is a “shadow of good things to come.” We 
are primarily concerned with the New Testament revela- 
tion, which may be considered in three divisions: 


First, POSITIONAL SANCTIFICATION. 


This is a sanctification, holiness, and sainthood which 
is ours by the operation of God through the body and 
shed blood of our Lord Jesus Christ. We, who are saved, 
have been redeemed and cleansed in His precious blood, 
forgiven all trespasses, made righteous through our new 
headship in Him, justified, and purified. We are the sons 
of God. All of this indicates a distinct classification and 
separation, deep and eternal, through the saving grace of 
Christ. It is based on facts of position which are true of 
every Christian. Hence every believer is now said to be 
positionally sanctified, holy, and is therefore a saint before 
God. This position bears no relationship to our daily life 
more than that it should inspire us to holy living. Our 
position in Christ is, however, according to the Scriptures, 
the greatest incentive to holiness of life. 

The great doctrinal Epistles observe this order. They 
first state the marvels of saving grace, and then conclude 


— 272 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


with an appeal for a life corresponding to the divinely- 
wrought position. (Note Rom. 12:1; Eph. 4:1; Col. 3:1.) 
We are not now accepted in ourselves: we are accepted in 
the Beloved. We are not now righteous in ourselves: He 
has been made unto us righteousness. We are not now 
redeemed in ourselves: He has been made unto us re- 
demption. We are not now positionally sanctified by our 
daily walk: He has been made unto us sanctification. Posi- 
tional sanctification is as perfect as He is perfect. As 
much as He is set apart, we, who are in Him, are set 
apart. Positional sanctification is as complete for the 
weakest saint as it is for the strongest. It depends only on 
our union and position in Christ. All believers are classi- 
fied as “the saints.” So, also, they are classified as “the 
sancunedi a (notes Acts, 20 o2 1, Cor. 1:2+62 11: eh; 
10:10, 14; Jude 1). The proof that imperfect believers are 
nevertheless positionally sanctified and are therefore saints, 
is found in the First Epistle to the Corinthians. Corinthian 
Christians were unholy in life (1 Cor. 5:1, 2; 6:1-8), but 
they are twice said to have been sanctified (1 Cor: 1:2; 
6:11). 

By their position, then, Christians are rightly called 
“holy brethren” and “saints.” They have been “sanctified 
by the offering of the body of Jesus Christ once for all 
(Heb. 10:10), and are “new men” who are “created in 
righteousness and true holiness” (Eph. 4:24). Positional 
sanctification and positional holiness are “true” sanctifica- 
tion and holiness. In his position in Christ, the Christian 
stands righteous and accepted before God forever. Com- 
pared to this, no other aspect of this truth can have an 
equal recognition. But let no person conclude that he is 


— 273 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


holy, or sanctified, in life because he is now said to be holy, 
or sanctified, in position. 

While all believers are sanctified positionally, there is 
never a reference in any of these Scriptures to their daily 
lives. The daily-life aspect of sanctification and holiness 
will be found in another and entirely different body of 
truth which may be termed, 


Second, EXPERIMENTAL SANCTIFICATION. 

As positional sanctification is absolutely disassociated 
from the daily life, so experimental sanctification is abso- 
lutely dissassociated from the position in Christ. Experi- 
mental sanctification, may depend (1) on some degree of 
yieldedness to God, (2) on some degree of separation from 
sin, or (3) on some degree of Christian growth to which 
the believer has already attained. 


1. Experimental Sanctification the Result of Yielded- 

ness to God. 

Whole self-dedication to God is our reasonable serv- 
ice: “That ye present your bodies a living sacrifice, holy, 
acceptable unto God, which is your reasonable service” 
(Rom. 12:1). By so doing the Christian is classified and 
set apart unto God by his own choice. There is an element 
of finality and completeness possible in this. Within the 
sphere of his own knowledge of himself, the believer may 
definitely choose the mind and will of God as the rule of 
his life. This yielding to the will of God may be complete 
and final. This is self-determined separation unto God and 
is an important aspect of experimental sanctification. “And 
being servants unto God, ye have your fruit unto holiness” 
(sanctification, Rom. 6:22). 

Sanctification cannot be experienced as a matter of 


— 274 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


feeling or emotion any more than justification or forgive- 
ness. A person may be at peace and be full of joy be- 
cause he believes these things to be true. So also, by 
yielding unto God, a new infilling of the Spirit may be 
made possible which will result in a blessedness in life 
hitherto unknown. This might be either sudden or gradual. 
In any case it is not the sanctification that is experienced : 
it is the blessing of the Spirit made possible through sanc- 
tification or a deeper separation unto God. Experimental 
sanctification is such because of its effect upon the daily 
life, and in contrast to positions which are in no way related 
to daily life. 

2. Experimental Sanctification the Result of Freedom 

from Sin. 

The Bible takes full account of the sins of Christians. 
It does not teach that only sinless people are saved, or 
kept saved; on the contrary, there is faithful consideration 
of, and full provision made for, the sins of saints. These 
provisions are both preventive and curative. The ques- 
tion of sin in the believer is taken up exhaustively in the 
First Epistle of John. One passage (2:1,2) may be taken 
as a key to this Epistle. It reads: “My little children, 
these things write I unto you, that ye sin not.” This much 
relates to the prevention of sin in the Christian, “And if 
any man (Christian) sin, we have an advocate with the 
Father, Jesus Christ the righteous: and he is the propi- 
tiation for our sins.” This relates to the cure of the sin of 
Christians. Much Scripture is written “that we be not 
sinning,” but we are also told that if we do sin we have 
abundant provision from God for its cure. These things 
which are written, are not written to encourage us to sin: 


IT 


— 275 — 


(os ae en RE SE TD 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


they are written “that we be not sinning.” “Shall we con- 
tinue in sin that grace may abound? God forbid.” He 
alone can forbid, and He will forbid. Such are the marvel- 
ous provisions in grace for the eternal keeping of the child 
of God. 

We may conclude from these and many other Scrip- 
tures that the child of God need not sin. To this end the 
Savior has died (Rom. 6:1-14). To this end we have a 
message written (1 John 2:1,2)). To this end we are 
indwelt by the Spirit of God (Gal. 5:16). It is the pur- 
pose of the Father that His children shall be free from 
sin in order that He may have fellowship with them; for 
“truly our fellowship is with the Father and with his Son, 
Jesus Christ.” The basis upon which we may have fellow- 
ship with the Father and with His Son is qualified: we 
must walk in the light (1 John 1:7), which means to live 
by the power of the Spirit and instantly to confess every 
known sin. Because of the Advocate and because of our 
confession, God is free to forgive and to cleanse from all 
unrighteousness. We must not say that we have no sin 
nature (1:8): This would be to deceive ourselves. We 
must not say that we have not sinned (1:10): This would 
be to make Him untrue. It does not become a Christian to 
boast of himself; but every true victory should be ack- 
nowledged to the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ. 

Has any child of God reached a complete deliverance 
from sin? This question should never be confused with the 
facts concerning positional sanctification, nor with the facts 
connected with sanctification through our yieldedness to 
God. The answer to this question may be stated thus: 
While the believer is definitely trusting the sufficiency of 


— 276 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


the Spirit for his victory and fulfilling every condition, he 
will be divinely kept from sinning (Gal. 5:16; Rom. 8:2; 
6:14). This statement is not based upon any personal ex- 
perience: it is based on the Word of God. ‘The Christian 
never reaches a place where he cannot sin. On the other 
hand, the Scriptures plainly teach that, in spite of the 
fallen nature, there is deliverance for the believer from the 
bond-servitude to sin through his union to Christ in His 
death and resurrection (Rom. 6:1-10), and through the 
enabling power of the indwelling Spirit (Rom. 8:2; Gal. 
5:16). This victory will be realized just so long as it is 
claimed by faith. Such is the divinely provided prevention 
from sinning. 

The old nature, with its incurable disposition to sin, 
remains in every believer so long as he is in this body. He 
is therefore disposed to sin. The sin nature itself is never 
said to have died. It was crucified, dead, and buried with 
Christ; but since this was accomplished two thousand years 
ago, the reference is to a divine judgment against the nature 
which was gained by Christ when He “died unto sin.” 
There is no Bible teaching to the effect that some Chris- 
tians have died to sin and some have not. The passages 
include all saved persons (Gal. 5:24; Col. 3:3). All be- 
lievers have died unto sin in Christ’s death; but not all 
believers have claimed the riches which were provided for 
them by that death. We are not asked to die experimentally, 
or to enact His death: we are asked to “reckon” ourselves 
to be dead indeed unto sin. This is the human responsi- 
bility (Rom. 6:1-14). 

If through weakness, willfulness, or ignorance the 
Christian does sin, there is a cure. On the human side 


— 277 — 


2 a SE e e E a E E 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


SS 


there must be a genuine confession and repentance of heart 
(1 John 1:9; 2 Cor. 7:8-11). On the divine side there is 
“an Advocate with the Father,” “and the Father is faithful 
and just to forgive us our sins, and to cleanse us from all 
unrighteousness.” Experiences of failure and defeat should 
be growing less as we increasingly discover the marvels of 
His power and grace and the utter helplessness of our own 
strength. Every restoration, forgiveness and cleansing is 
a renewal of experimental sanctification. 


3. Experimental Sanctification in Relation to Chris- 
tian Growth. 


Christians are immature in wisdom, knowledge, ex- 
perience and grace. In all these things they are appointed 
to grow, and their growth should be manifest. They are 
to “grow in grace and in the knowledge of our Lord and 
Savior Jesus Christ.” Beholding the glory of the Lord as 
in a glass, they are “changed into the same image from 
glory to glory, even as by the Spirit of the Lord.” This 
transformation will have the effect of setting them more 
and more apart. They will, to that extent, be more sanc- 
tified. 


A Christian may be “blameless,” though it could not 
be truthfully said of him that he is “faultless.” The child 
laboring to form his first letters in a copybook may be 
blameless in the work he does; but the work is not fault- 
less. We may be walking in the full measure of our under- 
standing today, yet we know that we are not now living in 
the added light and experience that will be ours tomorrow. 
There is perfection within imperfection. We who are so 
incomplete, so immature, so given to sin, may “abide in 


— 278 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Him.” We may have fellowship with the Father and with 
His Son. There is also imperfection within perfection. 
We who are so incomplete, so immature, so given to sin, 
are even now positionally sanctified and complete in Him— 
our Lord Jesus Christ. 

Christian growth and experimental sanctification are 
not the same, for one is a cause and the other its effect. 
The Christian will be more set apart as he grows into the 
image of Christ by the Spirit. To state that he will be more 
experimentally sanctified as he grows in grace and in the 
knowledge of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ, does not 
necessarily question his present purity or victory in daily 
life; it is only to declare that he will be more set apart as 
he grows in the likeness of his Lord. It is to consider ex- 
perimental sanctification in the broadest and most general 
meaning of the word. 

The present transforming work of the Spirit within the 
believer is unto an ultimate goal. We therefore yet con- 
sider, 

III. Ultimate Sanctification. 

This aspect of sanctification which is related to our 
final perfection, will be ours in the glory. By His grace 
and transforming power He will have so changed us— 
spirit, soul and body—, that we will be “like him,” and “con- 
formed to his image.” He will then present us “faultless” 
before the presence of His glory. His bride will be free 
from every “spot and wrinkle.” It therefore becomes us to 
“abstain from every appearance of evil. And the very God 
of peace sanctify you wholly; and I pray God your whole 
spirit and soul and body be preserved blameless unto the 
coming of our Lord Jesus Christ.” 


— 279 — 


THE GRACE OF GOD 


J. C. Masses, D. D. 


I was asked to speak on “The Grace of God in Con- 
trast to the Law.” This will explain the references to 
law in my message. 

In John 1:14 we find the text, for the only possible 
means by which I could preach to you this morning is to 
forget you are preachers and preach to you as sinners. 
Therefore I must needs have a text. 

“And the Word was made flesh, and dwelt among us, 
(and we beheld his glory, the glory as of the only begot- 
ten of the Father,) full of grace and truth.” Also in 
Ephesians 2:8 we read: “For by grace are ye saved.” 

All the outgoings of God toward man may be prop- 
erly expressed in terms of holiness. Grace is the holiness 
of God directed toward man in an effort to recover man 
from his state of sin and to restore him to perfect and 
unbroken fellowship with God. 

Law is the holiness of God directed toward man in 
an effort to govern him in accordance with the will of 
God. 

Grace concerns itself with nature. Law concerns itself 
with conduct. 

Grace has to do with life and its issues; law with deeds 
and* their results. 

The principle underlying law is restraint. The princi- 
ple underlying grace is constraint. 

Law compels; grace impels. 


oe EE ES RS aE 
— 281 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Law writes its mandates upon tables of stone and upon 
the pages of books. Grace writes its will in the hearts and 
minds and upon the wills of men. 

Law fails, always fails, because the medium of law is 
the flesh, and the flesh is weak. Grace triumphs, for the 
medium of grace is His divine love, which endures and 
cannot fail. Law is partial; grace is complete. Law is a 
schoolmaster, a tutor. Grace is the indwelling Person, 
who is Himself teacher. Law is a marvel; grace is a 
miracle. 

Grace incorporates, fulfills and accomplishes the law. 

Here is a lad who studies at his desk under the hard 
scrutiny of his natural enemy, the teacher. He is doing 
his best to learn the multiplication table, and it is law to 
him. It is hard work, and he is in rebellion against it, and 
against the teacher from the moment that he comes into his 
class. But there comes a time when, by the grace of his 
higher knowledge, he has incorporated the law of the mul- 
tiplication table into algebra, trigonometry, calculus and 
astronomy. The grace of the higher knowledge of mathe- 
matics is not the rejection of the law of the tables, the 
taskmaster of knowledge, but its incorporation and its com- 
pletion. 

An illustration may help us to understand how grace 
enters human life, changing its quality and transforming 
its character. At High Bridge, a great steel structure spans 
the Kentucky River. There is a story that when this 
bridge was first built work of construction proceeded from 
both sides of the river banks simultaneously. The comple- 
tion of the structure was planned for mid-forenoon of a 
Summer day. It was a great event and the news of the 
completion drew a multitude of people to witness the lay- 


— 282 — 


Ci E i cae 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 
nl 


ing of the last rails and the initial crossing of the great 
trains of passengers and commerce. 


The hour came. According to schedule the last rails 
were laid where they were supposed to meet, but to the 
consternation of all concerned, they failed to meet by six 
or eight inches, leaving an impassable gap in the track so 
that the engines dare not move across. 


Strong men who had given their time and devoted their 
interests to this enterprise for months were variously af- 
fected. Some wept. Some cursed. All were stricken with 
a disappointment approximating despair. But the young 
civil engineer in charge of the construction, went out to the 
telegraph station on the bank of the river and wired a 
message to his chief in Cincinnati, explaining the situation 
and asking for instructions. An hour later, to his own 
amazement and to the incredulity of all who saw it, came 
back the message: “Wait until one o’clock.” They sup- 
posed that the shock of disappointment has unsettled his 
reason, but because he was chief engineer, and the author- 
ity was his to decide, there was nothing to do but to wait 
with such patience as they possessed. 


Then as they waited the sun climbed its majestic way 
up the steps øf heaven, reached and passed the zenith and 
moved on toward its decline. All the while it was sending 
down upon the earth not alone the radiance of its light, 
but its penetrating heat. That heat entered the air and 
into all vegetation, into the earth itself and incidentally 
into the steel rails, expanding them. So at one o'clock, 
with the heat of the sun entering and lengthening them, 
the rails came together. The great bolts, were driven in, 
riveting them, and the work was done. Immediately the 


— 283 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


traffic of the nation moved out over the completed bridge 
in perfect security and satisfaction. 

There was a time when God and man were separated 
by an impassable chasm of human sin, alienating man 
from God and separating God from man. Across it man 
could not pass and God would not till His Son, the Sun of 
righteousness, arose with healing in His wings and from 
the zenith of divine compassion, upon the cross, shed 
forth the rays of His healing. He there satisfied the 
demands of God and entering into the hearts of men, 
changes and transforms them from unbelief and sinful 
rebellion into faith and fellowship with the Father. Thus 
the chasm is bridged and the two are made one. That is 
the work of divine grace. 


Grace has done what law cannot do. 

One of the profoundest convictions wrought into my 
soul from the study of God’s word, is that the Grace of 
God makes us to be what we are constantly striving to 
possess. I mean, for a concrete illustration to say, that 
grace does not make us righteous. Grace makes the child 
of God righteousness. We will come nearer understanding 
this when these mortal bodies are freed from their limita- 
tions of sense. One who claims to know declares that in 
the other world where the spirits of just men are made 
perfect, those who hold communion with nature in its 
glorified form, do not speak for instance of the flowers as 
being fragrant, but as being fragrance. Thus we need to come 
to understand now in the fullness and the clearness of a 
larger divine conviction, that the child of God is not simply 
holy in nature and character, but that he is holiness unto 
the Lord. Grace contemplates no smaller miracle than our 
transformation from such as we are into that holiness 


— 284 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


produced by the indwelling Spirit of God. The language 
of grace is: “Ye died and your life is hid with Christ in 
God,” and so that “it is no longer I that live, but Christ 
liveth in me.” 

The method of grace is comprehensive, embracing all 
life. Charles Wagner, the French apostle of The Simple 
Life, has written : “Love is the free vast horizon wherein the 
soul can expand its wings.” Let us seize upon his defini- 
tion of love and apply it to grace: Grace then, is the free 
vast horizon of God’s love in which the soul of the sinner 
can expand its wings of faith and fly on toward the reali- 
zation of the indwelling fullness of God in Christ Jesus. 

Grace has three tasks: It is corrective; it is regenera- 
tive; and it ts transformative. 

First, Grace 1s corrective. The tragedy of human ex- 
perience lies in the statement: “Sin entered the world and 
death through sin”. Sin has marked the pathway of human 
fife and human sorrow with countless gravestones upon 
which that sentence is an epitaph. The whole pathway of 
human failure finds its explanation in this statement: “Sin 
entered the world and death through sin”. The state of 
sin in human life is not an incident nor an accident. It is 
a choice. I know the modern theory is that every new 
fall is a flight upward. But God deliver us from such an 
upward flight. Sin is a wound, a bruise and a festering 
sore. In perverse stubbornness, man strikes his head 
against the law of God, flings himself against His sover- 
eign Person, defies His authority and refuses submission 
to His reign. Sin is that thing within man which leads 
him to set himself against God and to persist in his chosen 
course against God. Thus man invites the curse of God. 

I think that we as Christian men ought sometimes to 


ie 5 


a I AR RR ESE ERT EE ES, 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


come to understand the clear, distinct, vital and plain issue- 
which Jesus Christ made when he said: “If any man will- 
come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross 
and follow me.” It is a mere travesty upon truth to devote 
days or weeks to self-denial. Jesus Christ never taught 
such pandering to the flesh. What the Master meant with 
reference to that statement (and I believe I am correctly 
interpreting Him in saying what he meant): “If any man 
will come after me, let him deny himself”, was to put the 
self of man against the self of God and to say to us that one 
or the other by man’s choice must be put on the cross. 
Once for all the self-life was to die in Christ on the cross 
by deliberate choice, or once for all the Christ was to die 
in the self-choice. Sin makes its throne-room in this 
flesh, the carnal nature. Sin reigns, and where sin reigns 
there is moral chaos. Conscience protests against its man- 
date. The voice of God, as the sound of gentle stillness 
which speaks within so that every man may hear, the voice 
which may be heard even in throbbing traffic of the thronged 
street—that voice so gentle and yet so persuasive that 
there is no place in all the moral or physical universe from 
which it can be shut out—the voice of God speaks in truth 
and infinite love. 

He warns; He woos; He invites and corrects; He re- 
news His invitations; in a thousand marvelous ways He 
manifests His fatherly compassion toward the sinner. But 
sin makes dull the ears and makes rebellious the will. It sets 
the heart of the sinner against that loving voice of the 
tender Father. The warnings of the Holy Spirit are not 
heeded, though His words as sign posts on the road and, 
red lights of truth are there. His love is flaunted in the 
very face of the Christ who gave Himself for us. 


SC Sey 


— 286 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


The Holy Spirit is resisted as He takes the things of 
Christ and presents them to men. His testimony concern- 
ing Christ is impatiently refused, disbelieved and discred- 
ited. Man is too busy with the sinful deeds which occupy 
his life, engross his attention and delight his carnal appe- 
tites. He presents everywhere the pitiable spectacle of 
one made in the likeness of God, now having a broken will, 
a seared conscience, with judgment dethroned, conduct 
perverted, character ruined. The soul is lost as it becomes 
a slave to fear and ceases to believe in the love and com- 
passion of God, to desire His fellowship or to abide in His 
truth. With fearful moral stupidity, the sinner clings to 
his sin. His shame becomes his glory; his belly his god; 
the indulgences of his fleshly appetites become the very 
essence of his daily life, till his one business in life would 
seem to be the prostitution of the very blessings of God 
to the service of the Devil. Man takes the highest functions 
of his mind, the holiest passions of his soul, the most sacred 
organs of his body and turns all this aside from their 
original design and yields them as instruments unto the 
service of Satan, the arch-enemy of God and man. Thus 
man identifies himself with that traitor who, originally in- 
trusted with the direction of the worship of man Godward, 
seeks now everywhere to pervert man’s worship, turn 
it aside from God, Who alone is worthy to receive it and to 
make himself the object thereof. 

Observe the results of that treachery and infidelity 
on the part of the man who yields his powers to the service 
of sin. Notice its effect first in his mind, for man’s whole 
thought-life has been vitiated by the presence and power of 
sin within. When man thinks unaided and undirected by 
the Spirit of God, he thinks perversely. The vain imagina- 


— 287 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


tions of his heart are to do wickedness continuously. He 
sets himself up as a very god to determine his own path- 
way and find his own destiny. The man who, undirected 
by God’s Spirit, sets himself against Christ, his will against 
the will of God, has already prostituted and perverted his 
intellectual capacity, his mental power, to the service of 
sin and offered it to the adversary. Man’s soul may 
thrill with such a holy passion of conscious fellowship 
with God, that like Enoch, who walked together with God, 
going in the same direction, keeping step one with the 
other, and holding converse by the way, till it is vitally 
true that where one is the other is. And man may thus 
realize that the abiding place of God is with man. Yet 
man, so constituted and capacitated, takes these highest 
and holiest passions of his soul and makes them to be 
the perverted slaves of sensuous desire and sensuous 
service of the god of this world. 

So also our bodies, made to be temples of the Holy 
Ghost, are meant to be clean and pure and sanctified. 
Every single member of these physical bodies is turned 
aside by choice of the sovereign will, resident within, to 
follow the lusts of the flesh and the dictates of the Devil. 
How we misuse these temples of the Holy Spirit! 

There is no voice to which the sinner’s soul is so 
willing to listen, there is no voice which seems so sweet 
to him as the voice of sin. The sinner’s will has been taken 
captive. The sinner’s heart is held enmeshed. The sinner 
has himself been hypnotized by that old serpent, the Devil. 
The seducer has enamored him. Satan speaks in tones 
of the master, and the most familiar picture of all those 
that have been presented, has been the listening ear of 
the human slave to the voice of his Satanic master. It is 


2 OR eat 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


a sorry picture; a troublesome one; an awful but a true 
one. And he comes to believe that ‘his necessities, his 
ambitions, his power, are bound up in this world with 
the worship of and obedience to the evil one. He bows 
before him in worship. He stands before him to serve. 
He views the things of this world which are desirable, the 
things of the highest passion and desire of the soul, things 
that make wise, things that make rich, things that make 
powerful, and things that make comfortable, and all those 
things he must have even at the price of the unhinging of 
his knees to the god of this world who stands before him 
as he stood before the divine Master saying: “All these 
will I give thee, if thou wilt fall down and worship me.” 

Law looks upon the sinner thus prostituting himself and 
cries: “Away with such a fellow from the world. He is 
not fit to live.” And law appeals to truth and truth 
points to his sentence of death long ago written: “The soul 
that sinneth, it shall die.” That is the final word. 

Is there no help? The sinner stands at the judgment 
bar of truth. He has but to plead guilty. There is no 
other plea. You cannot make stern, even-handed justice 
weigh in the balance with miserable human failure. Is 
there none to help? Grace steps forth and says: “There 
is an advocate.” The law indeed shall have its last ounce 
of flesh, not one jot or tittle shall fail, not one sinner shall 
escape. The last dregs of the cup of sin shall be wrung out, 
the last penalty of law shall be enforced, only all that 
applies to the sinner shall reach him in the person of 
Another, his substitute provided by the Grace of God. 

A mother and a son, the mother a widow and the son 
an orphan, had grown their hearts together, welded by the 
growing process into one life by a miracle of love. She 


— 289 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


lived in him and he lived in her, twenty or more years. 
Then the boy fell in love with another woman. She de- 
manded of him more and gave to him less. Her love was 
of different quality. In her utter selfishness, she came to 
be intensely jealous of the mother-love, so that at last 
she made demand upon him on this wise: “If you really 
love me, you must bring me your mother’s heart in your 
hand.” So mightily had the glamour of lust blinded the 
son’s eyes, that immediately he went away to do the bidding 
of his mistress. Interpreting her life and her professed 
love in the light of his own heart’s flaming passion, he saw 
in her demand no crime. The alabaster box of his love 
had been broken and poured upon her unworthy life. 
Having given all, he now hastened, though dazed and con- 
fused, to do her bidding. He looked upon the mother who 
bore him and with whom his love had been one. 
And then borne along by that other love, he slew her and 
cut out her heart. He came back upon his way to his 
mistress with the heart of his mother in his hand, and as he 
went over the rugged road to her house, he stumbled and 
fell. And the heart of his mother fell from his hand and 
bounded along the road, and suddenly, as he tried to 
recover himself from his own fall, that heart of his mother 
turned and said: “Oh, my son, did you hurt yourself?” 
We have been one with the Father, God. Yet we 
looked into the face of Satan and gave him our love. 
Satan said to us: “If you will serve me, go cut out the heart 
of God and give it to me.” That is what we did to the 
Christ on the cross. He was God manifest in the flesh, 
and you and I, sinful and enamored with that other love, 
tore open His side and tore out His heart, and then the 
cry came: “Did you hurt yourself. I die to heal your

GOD HATH SPOKEN

hurt.” My sins and your sins, not in part but the whole, 
were laid on the cross. The barriers of sin were broken 
down, the last separating stain of sin was cleansed away 
in the heart of the man, when the Son of God yielded His 
_ blood for our life. 

We have really been one in heavenly places here in 
Christ Jesus. It has been refreshing indeed, and I am 
counting on that fellowship of your faith to understand 
what I mean when I declare to you what to me is the crown 
of rejoicing in all my spiritual confidence—it is that in 
Christ Jesus we become partakers of the heavenly na- 
ture. Grace is Regenerative. The boldness of that éaith 
which declares oneself to be one with the Father and the 
Son is not misplaced. Our Lord Jesus has provided noth- 
ing less for us than that sinful men, redeemed from the 
curse of sin by the shedding of His blood on the cross and 
by the operation of His Spirit within, becomes in nature 
and privilege the child of God. Peter in his first epistle 
declares that: “By the exceeding precious promises of 
God, we are made partakers of the divine nature.” Christ 
is our life, Christ lives within. These mortal bodies pro- 
vide Him with a sanctuary, a residence. We are not our 
own. He has purchased us unto Himself to be His home, 
temples or sanctuaries of the Holy Christ. 

This thought is further set forth in the inspired state- 
ment concerning the Holy Spirit’s use of Gideon. Gideon 
was called of God for a service. Therefore he must be 
equipped of God for that service. The Revised Version, 
margin, declares that the Holy Spirit put on Gideon as 
a garment. I suppose that means nothing less than that 
the Spirit of God made Gideon’s body the instrument of 
His activities and the agent of His energies in the work 


— 291 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


He had to do in delivering His people Israel from their 
bondage. We stand with awe, amazed at so great 
a miracle in the far-away past. How greatly the Holy 
Spirit honored that man—poor tenement of clay—whose 
life He made a dwelling place for Himself. 

But Gideon had no higher privilege under the reign 
of law than you and I have under the reign of grace in 
Christ Jesus. For while it is true that Christ exercises all 
authority in heaven and in earth, it is equally true that in 
the Person of the Holy Spirit, He resides within the 
believer and exercises His power and authority from that 
residence—a residence which He makes His throne-room 
in the earth. 

Now I hold in my hand a steel bar of the finest quality. 
It has passed through the fires and been rid of its dross. 
Now it is able to stand any stress or strain or to bear 
great weights. But it is just a steel bar. Here upon the 
table is a dish of sand mingled with iron filings. Now I 
lay the steel bar in contact with these filings in the dish. 
Nothing happens. I lift the bar. I bring it in contact with 
the sand and the filings from every possible angle, but 
nothing happens. I remove the bar and bring it in contact 
first with a strong electric current. By that contact it 
does not change in shape, weight nor color. However, it 
does change in quality, for by contact with the electric 
current, the steel bar becomes a steel magnet. Now I lay 
it upon the dish filled with sand and filings and it takes hold 
with power upon every steel filing in the dish and both 
draws and holds it to itself. 

So I bring this life of mine—body, mind and soul— 
into contact with the problems of sin and righteousness 
in the world. But I find myself utterly without power. 


— 292 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


What I would not, I do. What I would, I do not. The 
very things which should repel me, attract. The deeds 
I should refuse, I do. Spiritually the positive and negative 
forces of life seem to be all mixed. Life thus loses both 
its charm and its power. Disappointment is a common 
experience. Spiritual and moral failure is a daily reali- 
zation. 

But now I bring this life by faith into cuntact with 
the Son of God. Immediately a divine energy flows into 
the life thus touched by Him. Social problems may re- 
main unchanged. Life’s arena of conflict and struggle may 
remain the same. The moral forces that oppose may all be 
identical with those that were. But power in the Holy 
Ghost now adds new equipment to the life in Christ. Its 
problems of environment and association and occupation 
have lost their terrors. Victory takes the place of defeat. 
A new energy within constantly prompts toward service 
and equips for service. A new life has been born. Self 
has died. Contact with Christ accomplished that. The 
believer is crucified with Christ unto the world. Equally 
he dies unto the old life which he lived and to the energy 
in which he lived it. Of him the Spirit Himself has 
written: “Ye died and your life is hid with Christ in 
God.” Out of that death of self emerges a new and 
deathless life with a new and deathless energy, as Christ 
came up from the grave nevermore to die, since by the 
resurrection He is declared to be the Son of God with 
power, even so out of the death of self the believer in 
Christ comes forth in the power of endless life. He is 
the same man with a new energy. He is the same indi- 
vidual, but with new power. Whatever his need is, he now 
finds that the grace of God is sufficient for him. 


=— 293 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Grace is Transformative. Grace is not only corrective 
and regenerative, it is also transformative. Blessed be 
the ‘name’ oi our «Christ! )>He “restores; ‘Hex renews 
and, through grace, He works within us. It is to the man 
rescued from his state of sin and renewed into a spiritual 
life by the indwelling Christ, that the inspired injunction 
is given: “Work out your own salvation with fear and 
trembling”, not neglecting the fact that “it 1s God who 
worketh in you both to will and to do His good pleasure”. 

The Spirit of the living God coming into the believer 
takes possession of him. All the believer’s faculties He 
claims for His own purposes. In the picturesque and 
striking language of the second chapter of The Acts, de- 
scribing the experience of the disciples in the upper room 
on the day of Pentecost, it is said that He sat upon each 
one of the disciples. Appearing as tongues of fire parted 
asunder, the Holy Spirit filled the house in which they 
were and sat upon each one of them. He squatted. It is 
the proclamation of a divine act in regard to the life of the 
believer on the part of the Holy Spirit similar to that 
which occurred to the pioneers on our frontier taking out 
“squatter claims” of land not otherwise claimed. Faith 
had cast out from their lives the strong man of sin. Thus 
had come to them a great moral cleansing. Now a new 
Owner enters into this house, swept and garnished through 
the forgiveness of sins, lays claim to it, takes possession of 
it. Henceforth all the territory of this life is His by 
virtue of His sovereign authority. The Owner of the re- 
deemed life not only holds title to it, but resides within it. 

From this point, the heavenly Master moves on to 
complete conquest of the life thus possessed. Henceforth 
He is forever making conquest, conquering, disciplining, 


= 204 me 


| 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


bringing into subjection its powers and faculties. Every 
range of its activity is henceforth His. All its relations 
are His concern. All its vital powers come through Him 
and must be used for Him. All the territory of life must 
be through Him, brought into subjection to the Christ. 

That is the purpose of grace. Grace has a charming 
sound! Oh, grace is a marvelous experience! Grace 
eliminates, imparts and develops. It eliminates evil from 
the heart of men; imparts instructions to the minds of 
men; develops all the faculties of men in the service of 
God. 

Grace illuminates the understanding, so that man, 
under the tutelage of this divine grace, beholding as in 
a mirror the glory of the Lord, is changed by the Holy 
Spirit into the very likeness of his Lord. His eyes are 
enlightened by the Grace of God. The Holy Spirit trans- 
forms the sinner and makes him to be like that holy One. 
“Behold what manner of love the Father hath bestowed 
upon us that we should be called the children of God.” 
And such we are. It is no misnomer, and we are not 
simply adopted sons. We certainly are not bastards. We 
are born of the Spirit, through faith into the family of 
God. Even now we are His children. Though it does 
“not yet appear what we shall be; but we know that when 
he shall appear, we shall be like him; for we shall see 
him as he is.” 

I have heard somewhere of a village in Europe which 
had beyond its confines one of the most marvelous bits 
of scenery anywhere to behold in the world. It is said that 
whenever a visitor came to that village, whether he was a 
stranger or not, the citizens of the village came and said: 
“Have you seen our wonderful scenery? May we show it 


0 S AE 
— 295 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


to you ?” And they would lead him out to where the hills 
piled their mighty stones one upon the other. They would 
point out the broad plains on either side of the flowing 
river bank; show him in these plains the many gardens 
filled with flowers with their rich and varied colorings; 
they would tell him of the fragrance of these flowers and, 
in fancy, enable him to hear the hum of the bees as they 
sipped the nectar from the pollen, and gathered honey 
from the storehouses of these richly beautiful flowers. 
They would call his attention to clouds flowing above like 
fleecy flocks in the meadows of heaven, and enable him to 
see with them the many marvels of nature’s beauty, a de- 
scription of which no words can provide. 

But before reaching this marvelous scenery, they 
always had an embarrassing moment. Between the village 
and the scenery stood a great unsightly boulder of rock. 
They were accustomed to say to their visitors and friends: 
“It is unfortunate that between us and this scene there is 
an ugly rock that spoils the view. It is our custom to 
blindfold those who come with us so that the beauty of 
the scene beyond may not be spoiled by the ugliness of 
the stone between.” Thus they went around the ugliness 
of the stone and in glowing pride, pointed again and again 
with ever renewing zeal to their richest of all treasures. 

But one day not only a visitor but a stranger came to 
town. He heard their story, but refused to be blindfolded. 
The more they talked of the rich and glowing scenery 
beyond, the more he seemed to be interested in the ugly 
rock before him. He was so impressed that he hardly 
listened to them. He turned again and again to look at 
that big ugly rock. At last they gave up in despair the 
effort to really interest so dull a soul. It was at this point 


— 296 — 


scene snes ca ee ea 
GOD HATH SPOKEN 
E 


that he left them and hurried back to the village. But 
soon he came again bearing in his hand a kit, from which, 
when he had opened it, he took chisel and mallet. Then 
he put a ladder against the face of that great rock and 
with his tools he set to work. 

The visitor that day proved to be Michel Angelo. 
And when he had finished the task to which he laid his 
hand, the whole face of that rock was changed. Its ugli- 
ness was gone. Its unsightliness had disappeared forever. 
In the place of the ugly boulder, stood an angel with out- 
stretched wings. Now the story goes that when visitors 
come to the village, they do not ask them: “Have you seen 
our beautiful scenery?” but “Have you seen our angel?” 

This story may be applied to our daily lives. To 
many a child of God there is the promise of some life 
achievement, some moral beauty, some spiritual excellence 
to be seen in the distance, to be realized in the years to 
come. But between this and that lies a barrier of present 
shortcomings. The barren ugly rock of failure; of that 
spiritual hardness which is unable to respond to the best 
and the highest appeal of God to the soul of man. There 
is only one way to have relief from the sins and failures, 
and from the disappointment of our present lives. There 
is only one path by which we may come to the present 
realization of serving God in the beauty of holiness. The 
hardness of all hearts may be softened and changed by the 
Spirit of God. The ugliest of all life’s failures may be 
transformed into life’s victories by His power. There is 
no surer fact in all the gospel story than that the purpose 
of the Holy Spirit is to give to the believer victory in 
place of sorrow, steadfastness rather than instability, 
power in place of weakness. All that man by nature has

— 297 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


not in his life, by grace he may have. The Spirit of God 
creates within us the power for the perfect likeness of the 
Son of God. Some day we must all share that likeness. 
We may share it to-day. If we come under the Master’s 
discipline He Himself will chisel out of the hard and un- 
sightly places in our lives, not an angel with outstretched 
wings, but His own perfect image. So that more and 
more we will in the triumphant present anticipate the 
perfect vision promised us—the vision of that face which 
will at once become the crowning satisfaction and. the 
crowning joy of all His redeemed for time and eternity. 

It is for this reason that the constant demand of the 
Spirit is that we yield ourselves, that we become pliable 
and plastic in His hands. If He has His way with us, He 
will perfect His purpose in us and so accomplish His work 
in our lives that even here and now our mortal will begin 
to put on immortality and our corruptible, incorruptibility. 
For by grace we are saved. |

GOD’S REDEMPTIVE PLAN 


GEORGE E. GUILLE 


God’s Redemptive Plan can be stated in a few words: 
it is the establishment in the earth of a universal empire 
under the personal reign of His Son. 

The address of the Apostle James, recorded in Acts 
15 :14-17, is one of the most important single statements of 
that plan that can be found in the New Testament. It is as 
follows: “Simeon hath declared how God at the first did 
visit the Gentiles, to take out of them a people for his 
name. And to this agree the words of the prophets; as it 
is written, After this I will return, and will build again the 
tabernacle of David, which is fallen down; and I will build 
again the ruins thereof, and I will set it up; That the res- 
idue of men might seek after the Lord, and all the Gentiles, 
upon whom my name is called, saith the Lord, who doeth 
all these things.” James, indeed, says nothing here of an 
empire or kingdom, but he refers to the prophets who do 
so and quotes from Amos 9:11 a prophecy that has plainly 
to do with the kingdom. 

Central in the counsels of God is the cross of the Lord 
Jesus Christ and the central fact of all history. There re- 
demption was accomplished. Sin, that made redemption 
necessary, was dealt with and put away. “It is finished!” 
was the triumphant cry that forever closed that mighty 
transaction. In proof of it behold the rent vail; God no 
longer in darkness and the way into His presence opened 
wide to redeemed sinners; behold the rent rocks; the foun- 


— 299 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


dation of the old creation broken up to lay the foundation 
of the new; behold the rent graves: revealing the power of 
His death over death; behold Him rising, resplendent, 
leaving the grave forever in defeat behind; hear the voice 
that speaks in Psalm 2:7, hailing Him, “Thou art my son; 
this day have I begotten thee;” and behold Him exalted in 
the highest heavens, crowned with glory and honor! 

But nailed to that cross is a title, written in Hebrew 
and Greek and Latin—for all the world to read: “This is 
Jesus of Nazareth, the King of the Jews.” And there that 
title remains in spite of the chief priests’ appeal to the 
Roman governor to change it, for He is the King of the 
Jews! Going up to heaven, He took that title with Him. 
He is coming back again to make it good! 

Yes, the kingdom must have a king, and here He is, 
nailed like a felon, to a cross of shame. But that cross it 
is that secures His kingly title. Last king of David’s line, 
He is crowned now, not with diadems but with thorns, 
symbol of the curse that, by death, He must remove. 

WHAT THE Krincpom Is 


The kingdom which in the plan of God, is to be set up 
on the earth, is the kingdom promised to David. That prom- 
ise is recorded in 2 Sam. 7:4-17, and confirmed by Jeho- 
vah’s oath in the 89th Psalm, as follows: “I have made a 
covenant with my chosen, I have sworn unto David my 
servant, Thy seed will I establish forever, and build up thy 
throne to all generations * * * I will make him my first- 
born, higher than the kings of the earth * * * My covenant 
will I not break, nor alter the thing that is gone out of my 
lips. Once have I sworn by my holiness that I will not lie 
unto David. His seed shall endure forever, and his throne 
as the sun before me.” There can be no difficulty in identi- 


‘— 300 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


fying the Blessed One whom God here designates as “My 
first-born, higher than the kings of the earth.” It is not 
David, nor Solomon, but David’s greater Son, our Lord 
Jesus Christ, and this Peter declared on the day of Pente- 
cost when, after quoting the 16th Psalm, he said: “There- 
fore being a prophet, and knowing that God had sworn with 
an oath to him, that of the fruit of his loins, according to 
the flesh, he would raise up Christ to sit on his throne; He 
seeing this before spake of the resurrection of Christ” 
Acts 2E2081)! 

The promise to David was by no means the last word 
in the Old Testament as to the kingdom. One after an- 
other the prophets, in glowing phrases, set forth the glories 
of the kingdom, now in broad outline, now in fulness of 
detail. They tell us that it is to be set up by “the God of 
heaven” upon the earth, and that Jerusalem, restored and 
beautified, is to be its metropolis, answering finally to its 
description in the 48th Psalm and the sermon on the 
mount as “the city of the great king.” A converted rem- 
nant of Jews becomes its nucleus, enlarging as it gathers 
the scattered nation of Israel back to the land of its fathers 
and then embracing the redeemed Gentiles. Its King and 
Ruler is to be a descendant of David, of virgin birth, born 
at an appointed time, in Bethlehem, and He is to establish 
the kingdom with power, overturning Gentile world-power, 
judging His enemies, removing the curse from creation and 
reigning in righteousness, thus fulfilling His title as Prince 
of Peace and bringing universal peace, at last, to the world. 

_Opening the New Testament, we discover no change 
in this Old Testament conception and description of the 
Kingdom. The angel announces to the virgin that she is 
to bring forth a Son and with the announcement confirms to 
Pe a a a 

-— 301 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


her the covenant to David, saying: “He shall be great, and 
shall be called the Son of the Highest; and the Lord God 
shall give unto him the throne of his father David: And he 
shall reign over the house of Jacob forever; and of his 
kingdom there shall be no end.” (Luke 1:32, 33.) “The 
throne of David” has the same meaning here that it has in 
Isaiah 9:7, where it is said of “The Prince of Peace”: “Of 
the increase of his government and peace there shall be no 
end, upon the throne of David, and upon his kingdom, to 
order it, and to establish it with judgment and with justice 
from henceforth even forever. The zeal of the Lord of 
hosts will perform this.” 


Now the opening statement of the New Testament is, 
“The book of the generation of Jesus Christ, the son of 
David.” As Son of David He is Heir to David’s throne, 
and his genealogy is traced back to David to show that He 
was born invested with the title to that throne. The legal 
title is established before the divine one is made manifest. 
Then appears the predicted forerunner, proclaiming the 
kingdom at hand; and the King, sealed and anointed from 
heaven, and from heaven declared to be the Father’s be- 
loved Son, takes up the same proclamation, and on the 
mountain—constant symbol of the kingdom—presents, in 
the sermon on the mount, His inaugural, in which are set 
forth the righteous principles of the kingdom, principles 
that shall be righteously enforced when the figure—sitting 
on the mountain, becomes a fact: the kingdom established 
in power under our Lord Jesus Christ, and when Jerusalem 
becomes what He there declares it to be: “The city of the 
Great King.” Following the sermon on the mount are 
twelve miracles—the kingdom number—signs that accredit 
Him as King. But how does Israel receive her King? As 


— 302 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


early as the eleventh chapter of Matthew we hear His 
denunciation of woe upon the cities wherein His mighty 
works were done. He is rejected. Chapter 12 shows how 
thorough that rejection has become. Then, in chapter 13, 
forsaking the house, that speaks of His relationship with 
Israel, He goes out to the seaside—the symbol of the Gen- 
tiles—where He utters the seven parables of the kingdom, 
in which are revealed what He calls “the mysteries of the 
kingdom of heaven.” 

The Messianic kingdom of the prophets has been in- 
troduced in the world. Here its King has been anointed 
and His authoritative proclamation has gone forth. But it 
is not established with power and not manifest in the earth, 
because the King is rejected and Satan is still the prince of 
this world. 

What we see are “the mysteries of the kingdom.” We 
see only one-fourth of the seed of the Word-producing 
fruit. We see tares, sown by Satan, growing with the 
wheat till the end of the age. We see this monstrosity 
called Christendom, with its false religion that men mistake 
for Christianity. We see the three measures of meal cor- 
rupted by leaven, sneakingly hidden in it—Christendom 
corrupting the Truth of Christ, the mystery of iniquity 
working. But we see the church as “a pearl of great price” 
to purchase which Christ gave all that He had. And we - 
see Israel as a treasure still hidden in the world. 

In the 16th chapter of Matthew, the Lord foretells His 
death and speaks for the first time of the church, as a still 
future thing, which He will build. 

A few days before His death He delivered the Olivet 
discourse in which He set forth, in answer to the disciples’ 
questions, the events that are to mark the end of the age. 


— 303 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Passing over the centuries from the cross to the time of the 
end, He tells of war, famine and pestilence as the beginning 
of sorrows and of the great tribulation that shall follow 
these sorrows. And then, leaving not so much as an hour 
between the time of His predicting these things and the 
time of their fulfillment for a millennium to intervene, He 
says, “Immediately after the tribulation of those days shall 
the sun be darkened, and the moon shall not give her light, 
and the stars shall fall from heaven, and the powers of the 
heavens shall be shaken: And then shall appear the sign of 
the Son of man in heaven: and then shall all the tribes of 
the earth mourn, and they shall see the Son of man coming 
in the clouds of heaven with power and great glory.” The 
rejected King shall return to the earth at the close of the 
great tribulation, to establish His millennial reign. 

After His resurrection, and once more upon a moun- 
tain, He speaks of what belongs to Him as King: “all power 
in heaven and in earth,” and commissions a Jewish remnant 
to teach the nations His commandments, saying nothing of 
the church nor of the gospel of His grace, by which the 
church is to be called. 

All this New Testament revelation of the kingdom of 
heaven and of the King of that kingdom is found in Mat- 
thew’s gospel alone. The other gospels present far differ- 
_ ent testimonies. Luke is the gospel for the Gentiles, and 
in it we hear for the first time the word “salvation,” while 
at its close we find a commission, not to a Jewish remnant 
but to every believer, to preach repentance and forgiveness 
of sins in His name among all nations. 


Tuer MYSTERY 


But the King must have a bride. And the purpose of 
God respecting this bride for His Son, the Apostle declares 


— 304 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


to be “a great mystery” (Eph. 5:32), a “mystery, which 
from the beginning of the world hath been hid in God” 
(Eph. 3:9), and “kept secret since the world began” (Rom. 
16:25), unrevealed in the Old Testament, yet hidden there 
in type and shadow. 

In Genesis 24 we read of a father’s purpose that 
Isaac, son of his love, born of divine power, laid upon the 
altar and received from the dead “in a figure” (Heb. 
11:19), should have a bride to share his wealth and posi- 
tion as the father’s heir. But this purpose is not disclosed 
until after the death of Sarah. 

In that story of old is shadowed forth the purpose of 
God, that His Son, raised from the dead and “appointed 
heir of all things” (Heb. 1:2), should have a bride to share 
His inheritance and His glory, a purpose never fully re- 
vealed until after the setting aside of Israel, of which 
Sarah’s death and burial among the Gentiles are the figure. 
It is with this purpose that the opening words of James’ 
address have to do: “God at the first did visit the Gentiles, 
to take out of them a people for His name:” not to make 
of them, but to take out from among them such a people. 
Scripture nowhere affirms the purpose of the gospel to be 
the conversion of the world in this present age. If that 
were its purpose it has failed as many times as there have 
been generations since Pentecost, for never has an entire 
generation been converted. This people for His name from 
among the Gentiles is the church, soon to be manifested in 
glory as the bride. 

The Old Testament types demand that this bride shall 
be a Gentile. Moses, presenting himself for the first time 
as the deliverer of his brethren, was rejected. Withdraw- 
ing into Midian, he married a Gentile. When he came the 


at ee a EE 
— 305 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


second time, with his Gentile wife, his brethren received 
him and he led them out of Egypt. So Christ “came unto 
His own, and His own (brethren, Israel) received him 
not.” Rejected by His brethren, He has gone up on high 
from whence has come the Holy Spirit to call His bride. 

The story of Joseph and his brethren is another adum- 
bration of this plan of God. That well-beloved son was 
sent by his father to his brethren. The brethren, at the 
suggestion of Judah (same name as Judas), sold him, for 
the price of a slave, to the Gentiles who, on false accusa- 
tion, shut him up in prison. Coming, in the third year, out 
of prison he was presented with a Gentile wife, when his 
brethren, forced by famine into Egypt, were reconciled to 
him and he reigned over them as he had foretold. So the 
Father sent His well-beloved Son to Israel, His brethren 
according to the flesh, who turned Him over to the Gentiles 
to be nailed to the cross. Rising on the third day from the 
prison-house of death He has gone back to God while His 
bride is being called. By and by, having received the 
church to Himself, and with her presenting Himself a sec- 
ond time, His brethren shall be reconciled to Him and own 
Him as their King. 

At the cross, where redemption was accomplished, the 
bride was purchased. Into the side of the Lord Jesus a 
Roman soldier thrust a spear, “and forthwith came there 
out blood and water. And he that saw it bare record.” 
The blood is the purchase price of His bride and the water 
her cleansing, as it is written: 

“Christ loved the church, and gave Himself for it; that 
He might sanctify and cleanse it with the washing of water 
by the word, that He might present it to himself.” (Eph. 
5:25-27.) 


— 306 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


The scene at the cross has its prototype in the garden 
of Eden. “The Lord God caused a deep sleep to fall upon 
Adam * * * and he took one of his ribs, and closed up the 
flesh instead thereof; And the rib * * * made he a woman, 
and brought her unto the man. And Adam said, this is now 
bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh.” (Gen. 2: 21-23.) 
That deep sleep is a figure of the deeper sleep of death that 
overtook the last Adam on the cross, when “The very spear 
that pierced His side drew forth the blood to save.” 

There in Eden that woman, taken out of the side of 
the man is seen sharing his dominion over creation in a 
sabbath of rest. And it is all a shadow of things to come, 
the apostle says—when the church as the bride of Christ 
shall share with Him His crown rights as King in that mil- 
lennial sabbath now rapidly drawing near. 

Peter, in 1:10-12 of his first epistle, tells us that the 
prophets of old searched diligently as to “what manner of 
time the Spirit of Christ which was in them did signify, 
when he testified beforehand the sufferings of Christ, and 
the glory that should follow.” That a lapse of time between 
the sufferings and the glory should occur was plain to them, 
but what should transpire during that lapse of time was 
hidden from their eyes. Now it is revealed through Paul 
as the period of the mystery of the church. 

But as Abraham’s purpose concerning a bride for Isaac 
was kept secret till after the death and burial of Sarah, so 
this mystery remained hidden until after the setting aside 
of Israel—her death and burial nationally. 

The church was formed on the day of Pentecost but, 
as yet, there was no revelation of the mystery. The first 
seven chapters of Acts are the record of events that cover 
a transitional period, in which the kingdom was again pro- 


— 307 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


claimed to Israel, but with no word of Christ going to the 
Gentiles. Stephen’s address to the Sanhedrin ended that 
period, being the last word to Israel as a nation. Instead 
of receiving his message they stoned him, and in his death 
he became the messenger of the Lord’s parable in Luke 
19 which the citizens sent to the nobleman to say, “We 
will not have this man to reign over us.” This explains 
why Stephen saw Jesus standing at the right hand of God, 
when the testimony of the epistles is that He sits there. 
He was waiting for the verdict of the nation. It is then 
that we read of the conversion of Saul of Tarsus and of 
his commission to the Gentiles. The calling of the Gentile 
bride begins. Included, of course, are believers from 
among the Jews. 

When God’s purpose for this age shall be accomplished 
in the completion of the bride—and it may be that before 
this Conference closes the last of the people for His name 
shall be saved—our Lord shall descend from heaven and 
suddenly and miraculously catch her away to be with Him- 
self, in fulfillment of 1 Thess. 4:13-18: “But I would not 
have you to be ignorant, brethren, concerning them which 
are asleep, that ye sorrow not, even as others which have 
no hope. For if we believe that Jesus died and rose again, 
even so them also which sleep in Jesus will God bring with 
him. For this we say unto you by the word of the Lord, 
that we which are alive and remain unto the coming of 
the Lord shall not prevent them which are asleep. For 
the Lord himself shall descend from heaven with a shout, 
with the voice of the archangel and with the trump of 
God; and the dead in Christ shall rise first: Then we 
which are alive and remain shall be caught up together 
with them in the clouds, to meet the Lord in the air: and 


a ESE SS SSS? 


308 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


so shall we ever be with the Lord. Wherefore comfort 
one another with these words.” 

Now this removal of the church from the earth is 
another of the mysteries revealed by Paul, as, in I Cor. 
15:51, 52, he affirms: “Behold, I show you a mystery; 
we shall not all sleep, but we shall all be changed, in a 
moment, in the twinkling of an eye, at the last trump: for 
the trumpet shall sound, and the dead shall be raised in- 
corruptible, and we shall be changed.” 

THE GREAT TRIBULATION 

Contemporaneous with the mystery of the church is 
“the mystery of iniquity,” of which the apostle, in II 
Thess. 2:7, 8, writes as follows: “For the mystery of in- 
iquity doth already work: only he who now letteth (re- 
straineth) will let, until he be taken out of the way. And 
then shall that Wicked be revealed.” The mystery of in- 
iquity shall culminate in the Man of sin, but not until the 
Holy Spirit, resident in the church and restraining the 
working of that mystery, shall be taken away. 

Bereft of its safeguard by the removal of the church 
to heaven, the world, with the Man of sin at its head, be- 
comes the scene of those divine judgments foretold in the 
book of The Revelation from chapter 6 through chapter 
18. Those events, as I have said, are what the Lord, 
in the discourse on the Mount of Olives, describes as the 
beginning of sorrows and the great tribulation. The Man 
of sin, at the beginning of his career, makes a covenant 
with the Jewish state in Palestine, under the terms of 
which are revived the ancient rites of the Jews’ religion in 
the temple that is yet to be built by them. At the height of 
his career he shall go into that temple and, suppressing the 
restored ritual of Israel, shall set himself in the place of 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


God, demanding universal homage. This event, the Lord 
indicates to the pious Jews then in the land as a signal for 
the beginning of the tribulation, saying, “When ye, there- 
fore, shall see the abomination of desolation, spoken of by 
Daniel the prophet, stand in the holy place (of the temple) 
* * * then let them which be in Judea flee into the moun- 
tains * * * for then shall be great tribulation * * *. Imme- 
diately after the tribulation of those days * * * they shall 
see the Son of man coming in the clouds of heaven with 
power and great glory.” 


Tue REBUILDING oF Davin’s House 

Thus the great tribulation ends with the coming of 
Christ in glory, and it is this event that the second part of 
James’ address sets forth: “After this I will return, and 
will build again the tabernacle of David, which is fallen 
down.” 

James’ quotation is from Amos 9:11, 12. On turning 
to that Scripture we find that what follows the quotation is 
a prophecy of Israel’s restoration. 

The first mention in Scripture of this return of the 
Lord is in Deut. 30:3 where Moses, in speaking to Israel of 
her restoration, says, “Then the Lord thy God * * * will 
return and gather thee from all the nations, whither the 
Lord thy God hath scattered thee.” And in Matthew 
24, immediately following the verse which speaks of 
“the Son of man coming in the clouds of heaven with 
power and great glory,” it is written, “And He shall send 
His angels with a great sound of a trumpet, and they shall 
gather together his elect from the four winds, from one 
end of heaven to the other.” (Matt. 24:30, 31.) These 
elect are what James calls “the residue of men”—the ten 
scattered tribes of Israel. 


— 310 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


Not a creed in Christendom contains a reference to 
the future of the Jews, yet more space is given to this sub- 
ject in Scripture than to any other, and “I would not, 
brethren, that ye should be ignorant of this mystery,” says 
the Apostle in Rom. 11:25, “lest ye should be wise in your 
own conceits; that blindness in part is happened to Israel, 
until the fulness of the Gentiles be come in. And so all 
Israel shall be saved: as it is written, There shall come out 
of Zion the Deliverer, and shall turn away ungodliness 
from Jacob; for this is my covenant unto them.” 


Israel, blinded, exiled, and scattered, while the people 
for God’s name is being taken from among the Gentiles, 
shall, when the fulness is reached, be regathered, repatri- 
ated and saved, and is yet to have her greatest glory under 
the restored dynasty of David, with Jesus Christ, its sole 
Heir, occupying David’s throne. Then shall the promised 
land be possessed. Only a small strip on either side the 
Jordan has ever been occupied. But the land given to 
Israel extends from the Mediterranean to the Euphrates 
and from the River of Egypt on the south it embraces all 
the land of the Hittites on the north. We are just begin- 
ning to realize how vast that ancient empire of the Hittites 
was. Much of that land is desert, but God has said that the 
desert shall blossom like the rose, that pools shall spring up 
in the wilderness and that the mirage shall become a lake. 

God’s covenant with David concerning his tabernacle, 
i. e., his house, and his throne cannot be broken, for “Thus 
saith Jehovah, if ye can break my covenant of the day, and 
my covenant of the night and that there should not be day 
and night in their season; then may also my covenant be 
broken with David my servant, that he should not have a 
son to reign upon his throne * * *, Thus saith Jehovah, 


— 311 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


if my covenant be not with day and night, and if I have 
not appointed the ordinances of heaven and earth, then will 
I cast away the seed of Jacob, and David my servant, so 
that I will not take any of his seed to be rulers over the 
seed of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob; for I will cause their 
captivity to return, and have mercy on them.” (Jer. 33:20, 
21, 25, 26.) The only way in which that promise can be 
fulfilled is in the return of the Lord Jesus Christ, to whom 
the throne of David has been given. 

THE GENTILES 

When Israel shall thus be converted and restored to 
their land, blessing shall come to the Gentiles, and with 
this James concludes his address: “And all the Gentiles, 
upon whom my name is called, saith the Lord, who doeth 
all these things.” 

God’s purpose, as declared in His covenant with Abra- 
ham, is to bless the nations through the Jew. That cove- 
nant, like the covenant with David, was confirmed by Je- 
hovah’s oath on Mount Moriah as He saw Abraham’s only 
son offered to Him upon the altar, and beheld, in that figure 
His own beloved Son delivered to death and raised up 
from it. But once again Jehovah made an oath: this time 
to His Son. It is recorded in the 110th Psalm: “Jehovah 
hath sworn, and will not repent, Thou art a priest forever 
after the order of Melchizedek.” 

Melchizedek, both priest and king, appeared to bless 
Abraham on the part of the Most High God immediately 
after the threshing of Gentile nations. So, at the close of 
the judgment scenes of the tribulation, shall the true 
Melchizedek who now appears in the presence of God for 
us come forth as King of Righteousness and King of 
Peace, not to give up His priesthood but to fulfill Zech- 


— 312 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


ariah’s prophecy and to be a priest upon His throne, priest 
and King in one, to minister blessing to those over whom 
He shall reign, as foretold in the 72d Psalm. 


Thus the three-fold oath of God guarantees the per- 
petuity of Christ’s priesthood, His crown rights on the 
throne of David, and the blessing of the Gentiles under 
His reign, as King. During the great tribulation, through 
the preaching of the gospel of the kingdom by a converted 
remnant of Jews, an innumerable company of Gentiles 
shall be saved. These are they who are set on the right 
hand of the King when He comes in His glory to judge the 
nations, and to whom He shall say, “Come, ye blessed of 
my Father, inherit the kingdom.” 

In Moses’ prophetic song in Deut. 32 he bids the 
nations rejoice with Jehovah’s people Israel, and many a 
Scripture enlarges upon this as it tells how the promise to 
Abraham shall, be fulfilled. For example, Micah 4, which 
opens with a prophecy of the establishing of the kingdom 
in the earth, declares in verse 2 that “many nations shall 
come, and say, come, and let us go up to the mountain of 
the Lord, and to the house of the God of Jacob; and he 
will teach us of his ways, and we will walk in his paths: 
for the law shall go forth of Zion, and the word of Jehovah 
from Jerusalem.” And the last four verses of Zechariah 8 
give a similar prediction; which closes with these words: 
“Thus saith Jehovah of hosts; in those days it shall come 
to pass, that ten men shall take hold out of all languages of 
the nations, even shall take hold of the skirt of him that is 
a Jew, saying, We will go with you: for we have heard 
that God is with you.” 

And thus, at length, shall there be a converted world, 
when the promise to David is fulfilled and Messiah’s king- 


— 313 — 


GOD HATH SPOKEN 


dom is established in the earth; not by present agencies 
working; not by twentieth century civilization; not by auto- 
mobiles, airships and world-wars, as a recent writer would 
have us believe, but by the personal return of the Lord 
Jesus Christ with His bride, the church, which shall have 
been previously caught away to meet Him. 

Then shall the rewards of believers be manifest for 
they shall reign with the King. Then shall Satan be shut 
up in the bottomless pit to practice his deceptions among 
the nations no more for a thousand years. Then shall the 
groaning creation be emancipated from the curse that has 
overspread it because of sin, the earth, at last, yielding her 
increase. Then shall the nations beat their swords into 
plowshares and their spears into pruning hooks, learning 
war no more. Then shall the earth be filled with the 
knowledge of the glory of the Lord as the waters cover the 
sea. “The mouth of the Lord hath spoken it!”
